# The Russian Revolution and the USSR under Stalin - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches how the largest land empire in Europe lost its government twice in eight months, and how the party that picked up the second of those collapses turned itself, inside a quarter of a century, into an industrial state that could build an atomic bomb and could not reliably supply its own citizens with eggs. It begins in the reign of Nicholas II, Tsar from 1894 until 1917, with an autocracy that had survived the revolution of 1905 by conceding an elected Duma and a list of civil liberties in the October Manifesto, and then dismissed the First Duma after ten weeks and the Second after a similar interval before changing the franchise so that peasants and urban workers could not vote. It teaches the balance sheet of the last tsarist decade honestly, because that balance sheet is the whole inevitability debate: Stolypin's repression and his land reform, the two million peasants who had bought their own land by 1916 and the three and a half million who went to Siberia, the factory inspectors and the sickness and accident insurance scheme of 1912, the fifty thousand extra primary schools opened by 1914 - set against a peasant population growing by a million and a half a year, the Lena goldfields shootings of April 1912 and the strike waves that followed them, the deportations and the university surveillance that alienated peasants, workers and the intelligentsia together, and a dynasty discredited by Rasputin and by the manner of Stolypin's murder. It teaches the First World War as the variable that converted that fragility into breakdown - the transport failure that left food in the countryside and bread queues in the capital, Brusilov's real success in 1916, Nicholas's decision to take personal command in August 1915 and the political consequence of that decision. It teaches February and October 1917 as two different events with two different casts, and it teaches the abdication of 2 March 1917 in the imperial train near Pskov, the refusal of the Grand Duke Michael, the dual power of a Provisional Government under Lvov and then Kerensky sharing the capital with a Petrograd Soviet whose Order No. 1 told soldiers whom to obey, the return of Lenin in April and the April Theses, the June offensive, the July Days, the Kornilov affair, and the night of 25-26 October on which Red Guards took the telegraph offices and the railway station and arrested a cabinet. It teaches the argument about what October was - the Soviet reading, the traditional liberal reading in Adam Ulam's and Richard Pipes's versions, and the libertarian reading - and it refuses to settle it by preference. It teaches the consolidation: the first decrees on land, workers' control and the eight-hour day; the Constituent Assembly in which the Bolsheviks won 175 seats out of about 700 and which was dispersed in January 1918; the Cheka under Felix Dzierzynski; the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk and its price of a third of the farming land and half the heavy industry; the civil war with Kolchak, Denikin, the Czechoslovak Legion and the foreign contingents; war communism, the Red Terror and the killing of the imperial family at Ekaterinburg in July 1918; and the New Economic Policy of 1921 with the argument about whether it was a retreat or an alternative road. It teaches Stalin's rise as an organisational problem rather than a personal one - the General Secretaryship held from April 1922, the appointments, the local machines, the conferences, and the four oppositions defeated one after another rather than all at once - and it teaches socialism in one country and permanent revolution as the doctrinal currency in which those defeats were transacted. It teaches collectivisation and the Five-Year Plans as a single decision with two faces, and it teaches the famine of 1932-33 with the precision such an event requires: the grain-procurement and grain-export series set beside the harvest series, the attributed estimates rather than a confident total, and the explicit refusal to supply a number the sources do not give. It teaches the Purges through what the evidence permits - the mechanism, the named beneficiaries and victims, the two sharply different sets of figures, the Ryutin episode, and the argument between Robert Conquest's totals, the official KGB figures released in the early 1990s and Ronald Suny's wider counting. It teaches the parts of the system that are usually left out of an answer and are usually where the marks are: nationality policy and the two-handed federal bargain over a population that was 47 per cent non-Russian; women, work and the family legislation of the mid-1930s; the literacy campaign and the school system; religion and the destruction of the clergy; socialist realism in literature, music, painting and film; the culturedness campaign; the communal apartment and the barrack. It teaches the 1936 Constitution as a text to be read against a practice, and the totalitarian, revisionist and post-revisionist positions as rival readings of the same archive. It teaches the achievements and the costs in the same paragraph, because the examiner's question is almost always about the relation between them. Responsibility here is stated about governments, parties, commissariats, police organisations and named individuals - never about nations, peoples or whole social classes. The military course of the Second World War belongs to the Second World War owner, interwar diplomacy belongs to the international-relations owner, the fascist and militarist regimes belong to theirs, and the Cold War begins where this session ends: with a brain haemorrhage on 5 March 1953.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of a textbook chapter, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter narrates 1905, then 1917, then the civil war, then Stalin, and arrives at a verdict that the revolution was either glorious or criminal. That structure produces the standard failing script: a chronicle that has described a transformation without ever explaining a mechanism, and a moral conclusion that no evidence in the answer was ever used to test. Here the inherited structure is described before the crisis, because the war did not invent Russia's problems. The two revolutions are separated before either is explained, because collapsing them is the single most expensive error in the topic. Consolidation is taught before Stalin, because the instruments Stalin inherited were built between 1917 and 1922 and the Leninism-Stalinism question cannot be answered without knowing exactly which ones. Collectivisation and industrialisation are taught as one decision, because they were financed out of each other. The Purges are taught after the economic transformation, because the terror of 1936-38 is unintelligible without the social upheaval of 1929-33 that preceded it. Society, nationality and culture come before the constitutional and interpretive debates, because a verdict about totalitarianism reached without evidence about ordinary life is a slogan. Only then does the session turn to continuity, counterfactuals and conversion into marks.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the subject's master chronology, the official syllabus mapping, the folder README and answer-worthiness audit, the one core world-history book read for this session, or a verified official question ledger | Assert it. Dates, names, decrees, figures the sources record, and recorded scholarly positions can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

One further habit belongs with those labels. Where a standard connecting name, date or institution is needed to hold a sequence together, this session supplies it rather than retreating into vague formulations such as "a decree was issued", "the opposition was removed" or "there were show trials". Every such item is verified, and its provenance is recorded in the final ledger rather than repeated in the teaching. Where the evidence base is internally inconsistent, or where a page of the text used here is unreadable, the gap is displayed at the point of use instead of being smoothed over.

Eight disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where a fluent answer can be badly wrong.

**The sensitive-event discipline.** Three episodes in this topic - the famine of 1932-33, the Purges, and the wartime and postwar deportations - involve mass death. They are taught with the same evidential care as everything else and with one additional rule: **no total is stated that the sources used here do not state, and no total they do state is presented as settled.** ✅ The evidence base records an estimate of over five million peasant deaths from starvation in 1932-33; Ronald Suny's addition of four to five million famine deaths to the wider repression totals to reach ten to eleven million destroyed lives; Robert Conquest's figures for 1937-38 of about seven million arrests, about a million executions and about two million deaths in the camps; official KGB figures released in the early 1990s of 700 000 executions in the same period and 3.6 million people in camps and prisons at the end of the 1930s; and Alexander Yakovlev's estimate of sixty to seventy million victims of communism after 1917. ⚠️ Those are not variants of one number; they count different things over different spans using different definitions, and an answer that averages them has produced a figure that no historian holds. The correct operation is to name the estimator, state what is being counted, and say that the range is contested. ⚠️ A script that supplies a single confident total for any of these events is not being precise; it is asserting something the record does not support.

**The attribution discipline.** Responsibility is stated about governments, parties, commissariats, police organisations, congresses, local officials and named individuals - never about nations, peoples, or entire social categories. ⚠️ Formulations such as "the Russians wanted revolution", "the peasants resisted modernisation" or "the kulaks sabotaged the harvest" are treated in this session as errors of fact, and they appear in the remediation set. The last of them matters most: ✅ Lowe records that all peasants who owned any property at all, whether they were classified as kulaks or not, were hostile to collectivisation, which means the official category and the actual social group did not coincide. Adopting the official category as a description of a real actor is adopting one side's propaganda as evidence.

**The anti-inevitability discipline.** "Inevitable" and "bound to happen" appear here only where a named position is being reported, and always beside the position that rejects them. ✅ The sources used here carry both at full strength: the Soviet view that, given the flouting of the 1905 promises, revolution was bound to come sooner or later and the war actually delayed it; Sheila Fitzpatrick's judgement that the regime was so vulnerable to any jolt or setback that it is hard to imagine that it could have survived long, even without the war; and against them Christopher Read's view that the overthrow of the monarchy was by no means inevitable and that the pre-1914 situation could have continued indefinitely provided there was no war, with Robert Service's formulation that Russia was a vulnerable plant but not doomed to the root-and-branch revolution of 1917. ⚠️ An answer that states either as settled has misreported the state of knowledge.

**The two-revolutions discipline.** ✅ The basic owner's instruction is explicit: never treat 1917 as a single event. February/March removed the Tsar; October/November brought the Bolsheviks to power. ⚠️ The two have different triggers, different decisive actors, different degrees of planning and different outcomes, and almost every high-value distinction in the topic depends on holding them apart.

**The calendar discipline.** ✅ Russia used the Julian calendar, thirteen days behind the Gregorian, until 1918. The February Revolution began on 23 February 1917 by the Julian calendar, which was 8 March elsewhere; the Bolsheviks took power on 25 October, which was 7 November elsewhere. This session follows the core account's convention - Julian for internal Russian events until early 1918, Gregorian for international events and for everything after the changeover - and states which calendar is in use wherever confusion is possible. ⚠️ "The October Revolution happened in November" is not a paradox to be resolved; it is two calendars, and saying so in one clause is worth more than a paragraph of hedging.

**The evidentiary discipline for numbers.** The sources used here carry a specific and finite set of quantities: the Constituent Assembly seat distribution; the Brest-Litovsk losses as proportions; the Cheka's recorded suppression and execution figures for 1918-19; the civil-war death total of at least eight million set against 1.7 million Russian war deaths; the grain harvest, state-procurement, export and livestock series; the 1932-33 export tonnage; the collectivisation proportion of over 90 per cent by 1937; the comparative pig-iron, steel, coal and electricity table; Stakhanov's 102 tons; the literacy and school figures; the women's-employment percentages; the clergy figures; the urban population increase of 31 million between 1926 and 1939; the real-wage and consumption comparisons for 1933 and 1937; the wartime and postwar totals; and the repression estimates listed above. No other number appears anywhere in this session. Where the standard literature has series these sources do not carry - regional famine breakdowns, camp populations year by year, deportation totals by nationality, sectoral output by plan - the session says so and supplies nothing.

**The instrument discipline.** Every decree, plan and institution in this topic is taught by what it actually did, not by its name. The land decree is taught by the fact that nationalisation without compensation was what made redistribution possible and restitution impossible; the ban on factions is taught by the expulsion mechanism it created; the General Secretaryship is taught by the appointment power attached to it; the 1936 Constitution is taught by the single-candidate ballot that made its franchise clause inoperative. ⚠️ A script that names institutions without stating their powers is describing letterheads.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics own material this one constantly touches. The military course of the First World War belongs to its owner; this session uses only the recorded domestic effects of that war on the Russian state. Interwar diplomacy, the League, the Comintern's international operations and the alignments of the later 1930s belong to the international-relations owner; this session carries Soviet foreign policy only as far as the boundary statement in Lesson 21 and hands over there. The fascist and militarist regimes belong to the rise-of-fascism owner; this session uses the comparison only where the totalitarian debate requires it. The military course of the Second World War belongs to the Second World War owner; this session uses only the recorded aftermath inside the USSR. The Cold War and everything after March 1953 belong to the Cold War owner. ⚠️ A script on Stalin's economy that opens on the Treaty of Versailles, or closes on the Berlin blockade, is answering somebody else's question.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Dependency-led question | Stage |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What is actually being explained here - a revolution, two revolutions, or the construction of a state? | Foundation |
| 2 | What kind of society and state was late-imperial Russia, and what did 1905 actually change? | Foundation |
| 3 | 1906-14: was the regime recovering or decaying? The inevitability question, stated properly | Foundation |
| 4 | How did the war of 1914-17 convert a fragile state into a collapsing one? | Core |
| 5 | February/March 1917: what happened, in what order, and who made it happen? | Core |
| 6 | Dual power: why did a government and a soviet in the same city produce paralysis? | Core |
| 7 | April to September 1917: the four episodes that decided who would inherit the collapse | Core |
| 8 | October/November 1917: the seizure, and the argument about what it was | Core |
| 9 | The first four months: decrees, an assembly dispersed, a police force and a punitive peace | Core |
| 10 | The civil war, 1918-21: who fought whom, who intervened, and why the Reds won | Core |
| 11 | War communism and the Red Terror: forced by circumstance, or intended from the start? | Core |
| 12 | The New Economic Policy: a retreat, an alternative road, or a trap? | Core |
| 13 | 1922-29: how a party secretary became the state, and what the doctrines were for | Core |
| 14 | Collectivisation and the famine of 1932-33: the decision, the mechanism, and the evidence rules | Core |
| 15 | The Five-Year Plans: what was built, who paid, and how the achievement should be weighed | Core |
| 16 | The Purges: mechanism, victims, figures, and the limits of what can be claimed | Advanced |
| 17 | Nationality, federalism, women, the family and social mobility | Advanced |
| 18 | Education, religion, literature, music, cinema and the manufacture of a citizen | Advanced |
| 19 | The 1936 Constitution and the totalitarian debate: text against practice, and three schools | Advanced |
| 20 | War, vindication and the final years, 1945-53 | Advanced |
| 21 | Continuity or rupture? Counterfactuals, the balance sheet, the boundaries, and the marks | Synthesis |

## Lesson 1 - What is actually being explained: one title, three subjects, four questions

Progress: 1/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: why the topic title names three different subjects; the four questions an examiner can ask under it; the vocabulary that must be fixed before any of them can be answered; the calendar; the syllabus clause this topic sits under; the five boundaries; and the six openings that lose marks before the first paragraph ends

### Visual: one title, three subjects, and the question each one answers

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   THE DIAGNOSTIC THAT RUNS ALL SESSION:
   read the stem, decide WHICH of the three subjects it names, and
   then use only the evidence bank that belongs to that subject.
   A causation answer that drifts into evaluation has changed the
   question; an evaluation answer that opens with 1905 has wasted
   a third of its words.

*The diagram is the reason a candidate who knows the whole topic can still score badly. ⚠️ The three subjects share a chronology and share almost no evidence, and the commonest failure in this part of the syllabus is answering all three whichever one is asked.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Try writing the sentence most candidates write: "The Russian Revolution of 1917 was caused by the misrule of the Tsar and the hardships of the First World War, and led to the establishment of communism under Lenin and Stalin." Read it again and count the claims it smuggles in. It says "the Revolution", singular, when there were two, eight months apart, with different casts. It says "caused by", as though a cause list were an explanation. It puts misrule and war in the same clause, as though they were the same kind of thing - one is a condition that lasted decades, the other an event that lasted three years. And it says "led to", which quietly converts a contested seizure of power into an outcome nobody had to decide.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the most valuable thing you can do in the first thirty seconds of this topic is not recall. It is sorting. Decide whether the stem is asking you to explain a collapse, explain a seizure, or evaluate a transformation. Those are three different essays, and a script that blends them is not being comprehensive; it is being unfocused at length.

### 2. Four questions, and the four different things they test

| The stem sounds like | It is really testing | The evidence it wants | The evidence it does not want |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ *Why did tsarism collapse?* | Long-run structural weakness converted by war into breakdown | 1905 and its failure; land; labour; repression; dynasty; war-driven state failure | Bolshevik organisation; anything after March 1917 |
| ✅ *Distinguish February and October* | Two revolutions with different actors, methods and outcomes | The February chronology; dual power; the four episodes; the October mechanics | Stalin, collectivisation, the purges |
| ✅ *How did the Bolsheviks consolidate power?* | Decree, coercion, war-winning and tactical retreat operating together | Decrees; the Assembly; the Cheka; Brest-Litovsk; the civil war; the NEP | The causes of the February collapse |
| ✅ *Assess Stalin's transformation of the USSR* | Achievement **and** cost, held in the same answer | The plans; collectivisation; the famine and terror evidence; social policy | A narrative of 1917 |

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner supplies exactly this directive map, and adds three further rows that matter: a question on the *fall of the Provisional Government* is testing dual power, war, land and legitimacy rather than weakness in the abstract; a question on *whether power rested on terror* is testing terror **plus** ideology, social mobility, propaganda and genuine support, not terror alone; and a question on *effect on society* is testing peasants, workers, women, nationalities, education and mobility, not politics.

### 3. Vocabulary that must be fixed before anything else

⚠️ Six words do real work in this topic and are routinely used loosely. Fixing them once saves definitional wobbling later.

| Word | What it denotes here | The error it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Soviet** (lower case) | ✅ An elected council of workers' and soldiers' representatives; the Petrograd body was elected at the end of February 1917, before the abdication, and similar bodies appeared in Moscow and the provincial cities | Confusing an institution with a country. "The Soviets invaded" is a category error in 1917 |
| **Bolshevik / Menshevik** | ✅ The two wings of the Social Democrat Labour Party after the split of 1903 over an election to the editorial board of *Iskra*; the names mean majority and minority | Treating them as two parties with unrelated doctrines |
| **Social Revolutionary** | ✅ A separate, non-Marxist party wanting a mainly agrarian society based on peasant communities operating collectively | Filing every revolutionary under "Marxist" |
| **Dual power** | ⚠️ The condition in which a government holds office while a rival body holds the loyalty of the armed force that would have to enforce its decisions | Writing that the Provisional Government "was weak", which describes the symptom |
| **War communism** | ✅ The requisition-and-nationalisation regime of the civil-war years, under which the Red Army enforced grain procurement and banks, railways, shipping and hundreds of large factories were nationalised by June 1918 | Treating it as a considered economic doctrine rather than a contested question in the literature |
| **Collectivisation** | ✅ The merging of small peasant holdings into large collective farms, *kolkhoz*, jointly owned by the peasants and directed by the state | Using it as a synonym for mechanisation, which is what it promised, not what it was |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Bolshevik-Menshevik distinction is worth one exact sentence in any answer on 1917, because it is the cheapest available demonstration of precision. ✅ The recorded difference is doctrinal and organisational at once: Lenin wanted a small, disciplined party of professional revolutionaries and believed that because industrial workers were a minority they must work with the peasants; the Mensheviks were content with open membership and held that revolution could not occur until Russia was fully industrialised and workers outnumbered peasants. ✅ The account then adds the point examiners like most - on that criterion **the Mensheviks were the stricter Marxists and Lenin was the one moving away from Marxism.**

### 4. The calendar, settled in one paragraph and then used

✅ **Fact:** Russia used the Julian calendar, thirteen days behind the Gregorian one used in the rest of Europe, and adopted the Gregorian calendar in 1918. The events Russians call the February Revolution began on 23 February 1917 by the old calendar, which was 8 March outside Russia; the Bolshevik seizure of 25 October was 7 November elsewhere. ✅ The core account's own convention, followed here, is Julian for internal Russian events until 1 February 1918 and Gregorian for international events.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the calendar is not trivia. It is a discrimination device. A candidate who writes "the October Revolution, 7 November 1917 by the Gregorian calendar" has told the examiner in six words that the reading was done from a source and not from a summary.

### 5. Where this topic sits in the syllabus, and what it owns

✅ **Fact:** the official General Studies Paper I clause reads: *"History of the world will include events from 18th century such as industrial revolution, world wars, redrawal of national boundaries, colonization, decolonization, political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc.-their forms and effect on the society."* ✅ The folder's syllabus mapping routes this topic to that clause as a direct owner.

⚠️ Note precisely which sub-clause it owns and which it does not. ✅ The mapping makes the doctrine-owner of "political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism" a different file - the overview topic's doctrine section - and routes the **historical records** of those philosophies to topics like this one. ⚠️ The consequence for answer-writing is sharp: **this topic owns what communism did in one state; it does not own what communism is.** A question asking you to explain socialism as a doctrine is not answered from here. A question asking about the forms a political philosophy took and its effect on a society is answered from here, and the effect-on-society half is where the marks concentrate, which is why Lessons 17 and 18 exist.

### 6. The five boundaries, stated once and observed

| Material | Owner | What this session may use |
|---|---|---|
| The military course of the First World War; the eastern front as a campaign | The First World War owner | ✅ Only the recorded domestic consequences inside Russia: supply, transport, command and legitimacy |
| The League, interwar diplomacy, the Comintern abroad, the alignments of the later 1930s | The international-relations owner | ✅ Only the boundary statement in Lesson 21, and the recorded fact that Lenin expected the revolution to spread and that it did not |
| Italian fascism, Nazism, Japanese militarism | The rise-of-fascism owner | ✅ Only the comparison required by the totalitarian debate in Lesson 19 |
| The military course of the Second World War | The Second World War owner | ✅ Only the recorded aftermath inside the USSR: the casualty and devastation totals, and the postwar policies they explain |
| The Cold War; the superpower system; everything after March 1953 | The Cold War owner | ✅ Nothing. This session ends on 5 March 1953 |

### 7. Six openings that lose marks before the first paragraph ends

1. ⚠️ "The Russian Revolution of 1917..." - singular. The correction costs four words and buys the examiner's attention.
2. ⚠️ "Russia was a backward country..." - a judgement standing in for evidence, and one the same sources complicate with schools, insurance schemes and land purchases.
3. ⚠️ "The people rose up..." - no such actor. Named crowds, strikers, garrisons, parties and committees acted.
4. ⚠️ "Lenin planned the revolution from exile..." - he was in Switzerland until April and in Finland during the July crisis; the planning of October is recorded as Trotsky's.
5. ⚠️ "Stalin betrayed the revolution..." - a thesis stated as a premise. It may be argued in Lesson 21; it cannot be assumed in line one.
6. ⚠️ "Communism failed because..." - the wrong unit. The stem almost always asks about a policy, a regime or a period, not about a doctrine's destiny.

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** all this sorting is over-engineering. The examiner wants to know what happened; a well-told narrative with accurate dates will score.

⚠️ **Reply:** it will score, and it will stop somewhere in the middle band, because narrative answers a question nobody asked. ✅ Observe what the core account's own end-of-chapter questions actually demand: how far the impact of the First World War was the **main reason** for the downfall of Nicholas II; how far Lenin's **leadership** was the main reason for Bolshevik success; whether February was a **spontaneous** uprising; whether the Bolsheviks seized power or merely **picked it up**; whether the "Stalin Revolution" is an **accurate** description; whether Stalin's power rested **almost entirely** on terror. ⚠️ Not one of those is a request for a story. Every one of them asks for a weighting, and a weighting cannot be produced from a narrative by adding a concluding paragraph.

### 9. Examination use

✅ **Fact:** the ledgers held in this repository record **no** previous-year question owned solely by this topic across 2018-2026. ⚠️ That is not a reason to prepare it lightly; it is a reason to prepare it structurally. The full audit, the exact wording of the one adjacent demand this topic supports, and the rehearsal set are in the verified-question section after Lesson 21.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the tsarist regime collapse in 1917?
> ⚠️ Distinguish between the February and October revolutions of 1917.
> ⚠️ Examine the effects of the Bolshevik revolution on Russian society.

**Answer-use guidance.** Sort first, then write. ⚠️ One opening sentence that names which of the three subjects you are answering is worth more than two paragraphs of background, because it tells the examiner that the selection of material that follows was deliberate.

### Traps

- Writing "the Revolution" without a month.
- Using "Soviet" to mean the country before December 1922, when the USSR was formally constituted.
- Treating Bolsheviks and Mensheviks as two parties rather than two wings of one, and forgetting that the Mensheviks were the stricter Marxists.
- Filing the Social Revolutionaries under Marxism. ✅ The account records that they were not Marxists and did not approve of increasing industrialisation.
- Confusing the doctrine clause of the syllabus with the historical-record clause, and writing a definition of communism instead of an account of a state.
- Beginning an evaluation of Stalin's economy with the causes of the February revolution.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ One title, three subjects: a collapse to March 1917; a seizure and consolidation to 1924; a transformation from 1928 to 1953.
2. ✅ Two revolutions in 1917: February/March removed the Tsar, October/November brought the Bolsheviks to power.
3. ✅ Julian calendar, thirteen days behind, to 1918: 23 February = 8 March; 25 October = 7 November.
4. ✅ The 1903 split was over an election to the *Iskra* editorial board; Bolshevik means majority, Menshevik minority - and on strict Marxist criteria the Mensheviks were the orthodox party, Lenin the one departing from Marx.
5. ✅ The Social Revolutionaries were non-Marxist and wanted an agrarian society of collectively operating peasant communities.
6. ⚠️ Dual power is a mechanism - office without force - not a synonym for weakness.
7. ✅ The syllabus clause is the political-philosophies and effect-on-society clause; this topic owns the historical record, not the doctrine.
8. ⚠️ Five boundaries: the First World War's course, interwar diplomacy, the fascist regimes, the Second World War's course, and the Cold War.
9. ⚠️ The six openings to avoid, of which the costliest is the singular "Revolution".

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem reads: *"To what extent was Stalin's power in the 1930s based on terror?"* State in three sentences what the stem is testing, what evidence banks it opens, and which large body of material it excludes.

**Model answer:** It is testing a weighting, not a description, so the answer must concede that terror was real and then show what else carried the regime. ✅ It opens the evidence banks on the Purges, on ideology and propaganda, on the social mobility created by the destruction of the old elite and the expansion of education, and on the material improvements the sources record for the mid-1930s - rationing abolished in 1936, canteen meals, free healthcare and education, and the privileges of the new elite. It excludes almost the whole of 1917-24: the February causation bank, the dual-power material and the civil war belong to a different subject and earn nothing here beyond a single clause on the coercive instruments Stalin inherited.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that "to what extent" invites a balanced-looking answer that never commits. It invites a committed answer with a stated margin - terror as necessary but not sufficient, with the other factors named and ranked.

---

## Lesson 2 - Late-imperial Russia and the settlement of 1905: what was conceded, and what was withheld

Progress: 2/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the structure of the late-imperial state and society; why Nicholas II survived 1905; the exact contents of the October Manifesto; the three demands it did not address; the fate of the first two Dumas and the franchise change that followed; and why a constitutional experiment that failed is more explosive than one never attempted

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.1(a), on the survival of 1905, the October Manifesto's contents, the demands it left out and the four Dumas
🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson
📰 CA Found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the 1905 settlement as a promise, and the ledger of what happened to it

```text
   THE OCTOBER MANIFESTO, 1905: FOUR PROMISES
   +----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | PROMISED                         | WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENED           |
   +----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | An elected parliament (Duma)     | First Duma 1906: dispersed by    |
   |                                  | troops after ten weeks           |
   +----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | Civil liberties: conscience,     | Secret police continued to root  |
   | speech, assembly, association    | out revolutionaries in the       |
   |                                  | universities; mass deportations  |
   +----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | Universal suffrage for Duma      | Second Duma 1907 dispersed; the  |
   | elections                        | voting system then changed to    |
   |                                  | deprive peasants and urban       |
   |                                  | workers of the vote              |
   +----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | No law to operate without the    | Third (1907-12) and Fourth       |
   | Duma's approval                  | (1912-17) Dumas lasted longer    |
   |                                  | because they were conservative   |
   |                                  | and had no power: the Tsar       |
   |                                  | controlled the ministers and the |
   |                                  | secret police                    |
   +----------------------------------+----------------------------------+

   THE THREE DEMANDS THE MANIFESTO DID NOT ADDRESS AT ALL
   -> improvements in industrial working conditions and pay
   -> cancellation of redemption payments (owed since the abolition of
      serfdom in 1861; they had reduced over half the rural population
      to dire poverty)
   -> an amnesty for political prisoners

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   THE MECHANISM TO CARRY FORWARD:
   a concession that is granted, publicised, and then hollowed out
   does not restore legitimacy. It supplies a documented standard
   against which the regime can afterwards be measured - and in
   1917 it was.
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*Read the right-hand column as a single sentence and the 1917 collapse begins to look less mysterious. ✅ The account states the point without flourish: Nicholas seems to have had very little intention of keeping to the spirit of the October Manifesto, having agreed to it only because he had no choice.*

### 1. Why the autocracy survived 1905

✅ **Fact:** Nicholas II ruled as an autocrat from 1894 - that is, as the account defines it, as someone who rules the country as he sees fit without being responsible to a parliament. ✅ Unrest reached a climax in 1905 with the defeats in the war against Japan of 1904-05; there was a general strike and an attempted revolution.

✅ **Fact:** four reasons are recorded for his survival, and they are worth learning as a set because three of them will be absent in 1917:

1. His opponents were not united.
2. There was no central leadership - the whole thing had flared up spontaneously.
3. Most of the army remained loyal.
4. He was willing to compromise at the critical moment by issuing the October Manifesto.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the highest-value comparison in the first half of the topic, and it costs two sentences. In 1905 the army held and the concession worked. In February 1917 the Petrograd garrison mutinied and there was no concession left to make, because the one available had already been made and discredited. **The difference between 1905 and 1917 is not the depth of grievance. It is the reliability of the armed force and the exhaustion of the concession.**

### 2. What the Manifesto contained, exactly

✅ **Fact:** the Manifesto promised an elected parliament, the Duma; basic civil liberties for the population - freedom of conscience, of speech, of assembly and of association; universal suffrage in elections for the Duma; and the rule that no law could begin to operate without the Duma's approval. ✅ It appeared to grant many of the demands of the moderate liberal reformers, and it gave tsarism a breathing space in which Nicholas had an excellent opportunity to make a constitutional monarchy work and to throw himself on the side of the moderate reformers.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the fourth clause. A legislative veto for an elected chamber is not a cosmetic concession; on paper it is the core of a constitutional monarchy. That is precisely why its non-operation mattered so much. ⚠️ The regime did not refuse constitutionalism; it announced constitutionalism and then governed as though it had not. Those are different political acts with different consequences, and the second is the more corrosive.

### 3. The three unaddressed demands, and why the second is the important one

✅ **Fact:** the Manifesto did not address improvements in industrial working conditions and pay, the cancellation of redemption payments, or an amnesty for political prisoners.

✅ **Fact:** redemption payments were annual payments to the government by peasants in return for their freedom and some land, following the abolition of serfdom in 1861, and although the peasants had received their legal freedom these compulsory payments had reduced over half the rural population to dire poverty.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this single fact converts the land question from a grievance into a structure. ✅ Emancipation in 1861 was not free; it was a purchase on credit, and the credit was still being serviced by more than half the rural population four decades later. ⚠️ That is why "land hunger" is an inadequate phrase for what was happening in the Russian countryside. The peasantry was not merely short of land; it was paying for land it had been told it was given. Any political movement that promised to cancel that transaction had an enormous constituency waiting for it - and in 1917 two movements promised exactly that.

### 4. The two dispersed Dumas, and the franchise change

| Duma | Dates | What happened |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ First | 1906 | Not democratically elected: all classes could vote, but the system was rigged so that landowners and the middle classes would be in the majority. Even so it demanded confiscation of large estates, a genuinely democratic electoral system, the right to approve the Tsar's ministers, the right to strike and the abolition of the death penalty. Dispersed by troops after ten weeks |
| ✅ Second | 1907 | The same fate; afterwards Nicholas changed the voting system, depriving peasants and urban workers of the vote |
| ✅ Third | 1907-12 | Much more conservative, and therefore lasted longer |
| ✅ Fourth | 1912-17 | The same. Both occasionally criticised the government, but had no power, because the Tsar controlled the ministers and the secret police |

✅ **Fact:** Nicholas was apparently heard to remark, of the First Duma's demands, that if things continued to go on like this, "we should find ourselves close to being a democratic republic. That would be senseless and criminal."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that remark is the most economical evidence in the whole of the pre-1917 section, because it settles a question of intention that would otherwise have to be inferred. ⚠️ It is also the correct answer to a stem asking how far Nicholas fulfilled the promises of 1905: not a survey of the four Dumas, but the demonstration that the franchise was narrowed *after* universal suffrage had been promised, with the recorded reasoning attached.

### 5. The observers' surprise, and what it reveals

✅ **Fact:** some foreign observers were surprised at the ease with which Nicholas ignored his promises and dismissed the first two Dumas without provoking another general strike. ✅ The recorded explanation is that the revolutionary impetus had subsided for the time being, and many leaders were either in prison or in exile.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the evidential basis for the whole "recovery" case of Lesson 3, and it is also its limit. The quiescence of 1907-11 was produced by exhaustion and repression, not by satisfaction. ⚠️ A political system can be stable in the sense that nothing is happening and unstable in the sense that nothing has been settled, and distinguishing those two is the single analytical move that makes the inevitability debate intelligible rather than a clash of assertions.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** all of this is a constitutional story about a parliament that never mattered. The real causes were economic - land, bread and factory conditions - and the Duma is a distraction.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right about the weight and wrong about the function. Economic grievance supplies the mass; the constitutional failure supplies the politics. ✅ The evidence is in the demands of the First Duma itself, which were simultaneously constitutional and social - a democratic franchise and ministerial responsibility alongside confiscation of large estates, the right to strike and abolition of the death penalty. ⚠️ Those were one programme, and dispersing the body that carried it foreclosed the only channel through which grievance could have become legislation. That is what makes the economic grievance politically dangerous rather than merely painful, and it is why the strongest answers on the collapse treat 1905-07 as the moment when reform stopped being available.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far did Nicholas II fulfil the promises made in the October Manifesto?
> ⚠️ Why did the tsarist regime survive 1905 but not 1917?
> ⚠️ Examine the social and economic structure of late-imperial Russia.

**Answer-use guidance.** For the first, do not narrate four Dumas. ⚠️ Take the four promises, take the record against each, and let the narrowing of the franchise after the Second Duma carry the verdict; close with the recorded remark about a democratic republic being senseless and criminal. For the second, use the four survival factors of 1905 as a checklist and test each of them against 1917; the army line does the most work.

### Traps

- Calling 1905 a revolution that succeeded, or one that failed. ✅ The account calls it an attempted revolution that forced concessions, which is a third thing.
- Describing the Manifesto vaguely as "promising reforms" instead of naming the four promises.
- Forgetting the emancipation of 1861 and the redemption payments, which is where the land question becomes a structure rather than a complaint.
- Writing that the Duma was abolished, or that the franchise was merely "restricted". ✅ Two were dispersed and two sat full terms without power; and the franchise change took the vote from peasants and urban workers, the two largest constituencies.
- Treating the Tsar's remark about a democratic republic as colour. It is the evidence of intent.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Nicholas II, Tsar 1894-1917, ruling as an autocrat - not responsible to a parliament.
2. ✅ 1905: defeat by Japan in the war of 1904-05, a general strike and an attempted revolution.
3. ✅ Four survival factors: divided opponents, no central leadership, a loyal army, and a timely concession.
4. ✅ Manifesto contents: a Duma; freedom of conscience, speech, assembly and association; universal suffrage for Duma elections; no law without Duma approval.
5. ✅ Not addressed: industrial conditions and pay, cancellation of redemption payments, amnesty for political prisoners.
6. ✅ Redemption payments date from the abolition of serfdom in 1861 and had reduced over half the rural population to dire poverty.
7. ✅ First Duma 1906, rigged franchise, radical demands, dispersed by troops after ten weeks; Second Duma 1907 the same; franchise then narrowed against peasants and urban workers.
8. ✅ Third 1907-12 and Fourth 1912-17: conservative, longer-lived, powerless - the Tsar controlled ministers and police.
9. ✅ Nicholas's recorded remark: a democratic republic "would be senseless and criminal".
10. ⚠️ The mechanism: a publicised concession that is hollowed out leaves a documented standard for later prosecution.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the failure of the 1905 settlement matter more, for explaining 1917, than the absence of any settlement would have done?

**Model answer:** Because it changed what a challenge to the regime had to argue. ✅ The Manifesto conceded a parliament, four civil liberties, universal suffrage for Duma elections and a legislative veto, and the record against those promises is the dispersal of the First Duma after ten weeks, the dispersal of the Second, and a franchise change that stripped the vote from peasants and urban workers; the account's summary is that Nicholas had very little intention of keeping to the spirit of the Manifesto and had agreed to it only because he had no choice. ⚠️ An unreformed autocracy can be attacked only in the name of an abstraction; a reformed one that has repudiated its own reform can be attacked in the name of its own published undertakings, which is a far easier case to make and a far wider one to recruit on. That is also why the Soviet reading of the period - that the "unfinished" revolution of 1905-06 could not long be delayed - had material to work with, even though, as Lesson 3 shows, it is not the only defensible reading.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 1905 as a dress rehearsal for 1917. The casts, the triggers and above all the army's behaviour differ, and the sources record 1905 as an episode that stabilised the regime for roughly a decade.

## Lesson 3 - 1906-14: two ledgers, one regime, and the inevitability question stated properly

Progress: 3/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Stolypin's repression and his land reform; the recorded strengths of the regime after 1906; the four recorded weaknesses; the revolutionary parties and what separated them; the discrediting of the dynasty; and the five positions on inevitability, set out as they are recorded rather than resolved by preference

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.1(b), (c) and (d), on Stolypin, the recorded strengths and weaknesses, the party revival and the inevitability verdict
🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson
📰 CA Found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the same decade, entered in two accounts

```text
   1906-1914: THE STRENGTHS LEDGER          THE WEAKNESSES LEDGER
   +-----------------------------------+  +-----------------------------------+
   | Stolypin, prime minister 1906-11  |  | LAND REFORM FAILING BY 1911       |
   | repression: some 4000 executed    |  | peasant population growing at     |
   | over the next three years         |  | 1.5 million a year; farming       |
   |                                   |  | methods too inefficient to feed   |
   | AND reform: redemption payments   |  | it; Stolypin assassinated 1911    |
   | abolished; peasants encouraged to |  | - "perhaps the only man who       |
   | buy land: ~2 million had done so  |  | could have saved the monarchy"    |
   | by 1916; ~3.5 million emigrated   |  |                                   |
   | to Siberia with their own farms;  |  | INDUSTRIAL UNREST                 |
   | a class of kulaks emerges         |  | Lena goldfields, April 1912:      |
   |                                   |  | 270 striking gold miners shot     |
   | FACTORIES: more under inspection; |  | -> over 2000 strikes in 1912;     |
   | profits up; 1912 sickness and     |  | 2400 in 1913; over 4000 in the    |
   | accident insurance scheme         |  | first seven months of 1914        |
   |                                   |  |                                   |
   | EDUCATION: 1908 programme for     |  | REPRESSION: police rooting out    |
   | universal education within ten    |  | revolutionaries among students    |
   | years; 50 000 extra primary       |  | and lecturers; mass deportations  |
   | schools opened by 1914            |  | of Jews -> three key groups       |
   |                                   |  | alienated at once: peasants,      |
   | OPPOSITION: revolutionary parties |  | industrial workers, intelligentsia|
   | short of money, split, leaders    |  |                                   |
   | in exile, "seemed to have lost    |  | PARTIES REVIVING from 1912:       |
   | heart"                            |  | Bolsheviks and Mensheviks;        |
   |                                   |  | Pravda founded 1912               |
   |                                   |  |                                   |
   |                                   |  | DYNASTY DISCREDITED: Stolypin's   |
   |                                   |  | murder; Rasputin; the Duma's 1912 |
   |                                   |  | request ignored                   |
   +-----------------------------------+  +-----------------------------------+
                        |                             |
                        +--------------+--------------+
                                       |
              BOTH LEDGERS ARE TRUE. THAT IS THE WHOLE DEBATE.
              July 1914: a Bolshevik-organised general strike in the
              capital, with demonstrations, shootings and barricades -
              and it ended on 15 July, days before the war, with the
              government still in control of the army and the police.
```

*Neither column is propaganda. ⚠️ The candidate's task is not to choose a column but to say what each one licenses: the left licenses a claim about trajectory, the right a claim about fragility, and only the war decides between them.*

### 1. Stolypin: the two halves of one policy

✅ **Fact:** Peter Stolypin was prime minister from 1906 to 1911. ✅ He introduced strict repressive measures, with some 4000 people executed over the next three years. ✅ He also brought in reforms and made determined efforts to win over the peasants, believing that, given twenty years of peace, there would be no question of revolution. ✅ Redemption payments were abolished and peasants were encouraged to buy their own land; about 2 million had done so by 1916 and another 3.5 million had emigrated to Siberia where they had their own farms. ✅ The result was the emergence of a class of comfortably-off peasants - *kulaks* - on whom the government could rely for support against revolution, or so Stolypin hoped.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** hold on to that last clause, because it has a long afterlife. The kulak category enters Russian politics in the 1900s as a deliberate government creation - a stratum manufactured to be conservative. ⚠️ Two decades later the same category is targeted for destruction on the grounds that it is standing in the way of progress. **The class that collectivisation set out to eliminate was substantially a product of the last tsarist attempt to prevent revolution.** That single continuity, stated in one sentence, is worth more in an answer on collectivisation than a paragraph of description.

### 2. The other recorded strengths

✅ **Fact:** as more factories came under the control of inspectors there were signs of improving working conditions; as industrial profits increased the first signs of a more prosperous workforce could be detected; and in 1912 a workers' sickness and accident insurance scheme was introduced. ✅ In 1908 a programme was announced to bring about universal education within ten years, and by 1914 an extra 50 000 primary schools had been opened. ✅ At the same time the revolutionary parties seemed to have lost heart: short of money, torn by disagreements, and with their leaders still in exile.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the schools figure is the one to carry. It is the most concrete evidence available here for the recovery case, and it also sets up a comparison the answer can make later: ✅ in 1917 under half the population could be described as literate, and by the 1939 census 94 per cent in the towns and 86 per cent in rural areas of those aged nine to forty-nine were literate. ⚠️ The tsarist expansion was real and it was from a very low base; the Soviet expansion was larger and from the base the tsarist programme had reached. Neither claim needs to be diminished to make the other.

### 3. The four recorded weaknesses

| Weakness | The recorded evidence | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Failure of the land reforms** | By 1911 it was clear Stolypin's reforms would not have the desired effect, partly because the peasant population was growing at 1.5 million a year and partly because farming methods were too inefficient to support it; Stolypin was assassinated in 1911, removing one of the few really able tsarist ministers and perhaps the only man who could have saved the monarchy | ⚠️ A reform can be genuine and still be outrun by demography. The agrarian problem was not neglected; it was attempted and lost |
| ✅ **Industrial unrest** | A wave of strikes set off by the shooting of 270 striking gold miners in the Lena goldfields in Siberia in April 1912; over 2000 separate strikes in that year, 2400 in 1913, and over 4000 in the first seven months of 1914 | ⚠️ The trend line is the evidence, not the totals. Unrest was accelerating in the months immediately before the war |
| ✅ **Government repression** | Little relaxation of repressive policy; the secret police rooted out revolutionaries among university students and lecturers and deported masses of Jews, ensuring both groups were firmly anti-tsarist; the government had alienated three of the most important sections of society - peasants, industrial workers and the intelligentsia | ⚠️ This is the structurally decisive entry. A regime can survive alienating one of those three; alienating all three simultaneously leaves no coalition available |
| ✅ **Revival of the revolutionary parties** | As 1912 progressed the fortunes of the Bolsheviks and Mensheviks revived; the new Bolshevik newspaper *Pravda* appeared in 1912, extremely important for publicising Bolshevik ideas and giving political direction to the already developing strike wave | ⚠️ Note the order in the source: the strike wave was *already developing*, and the newspaper gave it direction. Organisation followed grievance; it did not manufacture it |

### 4. The parties, named once and used thereafter

✅ **Fact:** both the Bolsheviks and the Mensheviks developed from the Social Democrat Labour Party, Marxist in outlook. ✅ Karl Marx (1818-83) set out his ideas in the *Communist Manifesto* (1848) and *Das Kapital* (1867); he held that economic factors were the real cause of historical change, that workers were everywhere exploited by capitalists, that a fully industrialised society would see the workers rise against their exploiters and run the country in their own interests - the dictatorship of the proletariat - and that at that point the state would wither away.

✅ **Fact:** Lenin helped to edit the revolutionary newspaper *Iskra*, and it was over an election to its editorial board in 1903 that the party split into Lenin's supporters, the Bolsheviks, and the rest, the Mensheviks. ✅ The recorded difference is set out above in Lesson 1 section 3 and is not repeated here; the operative point for this lesson is that the split was eleven years old and unhealed when the war began. ✅ The Social Revolutionaries were a separate party, not Marxist, disapproving of increasing industrialisation and wanting a mainly agrarian society based on peasant communities operating collectively after the overthrow of tsarism.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three-party map is what makes 1917 legible. ⚠️ Two of the three did not think a socialist revolution was due, and the third was a minority within a minority. That is the correct frame for the Constituent Assembly result of 1918 in Lesson 9, and it is why the strongest answers on the Bolshevik seizure do not begin with Bolshevik strength.

### 5. The dynasty

✅ **Fact:** the royal family was discredited by a number of scandals. ✅ It was widely suspected that Nicholas was party to the murder of Stolypin, who was shot by a member of the secret police in the Tsar's presence during a gala performance at the Kiev opera; nothing was ever proved, but Nicholas and his right-wing supporters were probably not sorry to see the back of Stolypin, who was becoming too liberal for their comfort. ✅ More serious was the family's association with Rasputin, a self-professed holy man who made himself indispensable to the Empress Alexandra through his ability to help the ailing heir Alexei, who had inherited haemophilia; Rasputin became a real power behind the throne and attracted public criticism, and Alexandra preferred to ignore the scandals and the Duma's request of 1912 that he be sent away from the court.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the temptation with Rasputin is to over-tell him, and the basic owner warns against exactly that - a question on the collapse of tsarism is not answered with a Rasputin story. ⚠️ The disciplined use is one clause with a function: personal legitimacy is the resource an autocracy has instead of constitutional legitimacy, and by 1916 the dynasty had spent it. The Stolypin murder matters for the same reason and is the less-used example, which is why it is the better one to deploy.

### 6. Five positions on inevitability, and what each one licenses

| Position | As recorded | What an answer may claim on it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Traditional liberal** | The regime had obvious weaknesses, but before 1914 living standards were improving and, given time, the chances of revolution would have diminished; the strengths were beginning to outweigh the weaknesses and the monarchy would probably have survived had Russia stayed out of the war | ⚠️ That the war was necessary to the outcome |
| ✅ **Soviet** | Given the Tsar's deliberate flouting of his 1905 promises, there was bound to be a revolution sooner or later; the situation was deteriorating again before Russia's involvement in the war, so the inevitable completion of the "unfinished" revolution of 1905-06 could not long be delayed - and the outbreak of war actually **delayed** the revolution | ⚠️ That grievance was rising independently of the war. Note the sting in the tail: on this view war is not the cause but a postponement |
| ✅ **Kennan and Haimson** | The tsarist regime would have collapsed sooner or later even without the First World War to finish it off | ⚠️ That collapse was probable; not that the specific revolution of 1917 was |
| ✅ **Fitzpatrick** | "The regime was so vulnerable to any kind of jolt or setback that it is hard to imagine that it could have survived long, even without the war" | ⚠️ That the vulnerability was general rather than war-specific |
| ✅ **Read and Service** | Read: the overthrow of the monarchy was by no means inevitable, and the situation in the years immediately before 1914 could have continued indefinitely provided there was no war. Service: Russia was in a condition of "general brittleness", a "vulnerable plant", but "it was not doomed to suffer the root-and-branch revolution of 1917. What made that kind of revolution possible was the protracted, exhausting conflict of the First World War" | ⚠️ That the *type* of revolution, not merely its timing, depended on the war |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Service's formulation is the most useful in an examination because it separates two claims that are usually fused. Was *some* upheaval likely? Probably yes, and the July 1914 general strike in the capital, with demonstrations, shootings and barricades, is the evidence. Was a *root-and-branch* revolution producing a one-party state likely? That is a different question, and the evidence for it is much weaker. ⚠️ **The safe verdict, and the one the advanced owner endorses, is that the regime was vulnerable before 1914 and that the First World War made collapse overwhelmingly more likely without rendering every step predetermined.**

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the recovery case rests on selective indicators. Two million land purchases against a peasantry of many tens of millions, an insurance scheme, some schools - these are marginal improvements dressed up as a trajectory.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection has force on scale and none on direction, and the distinction matters. ✅ The same account that records the improvements records their inadequacy in the same breath: whatever improvements had taken place, they were obviously not enough to remove all the pre-1905 grievances, and the land reforms were failing by 1911 because population growth outran them. ⚠️ So the recovery case is not that Russia was becoming stable; it is that the direction of movement was positive in several measurable respects and that the terminal crisis therefore required an external shock. That is a modest claim, and it is the claim Read and Service actually make. ⚠️ The answer that treats the recovery case as an apologia for tsarism has misread it; it is a claim about causation, not about merit.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far were the revolutions of 1917 inevitable?
> ⚠️ How far was Russia a modernised industrial state by 1914?
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of Stolypin's reforms.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the inevitability demand the structure is fixed and should be rehearsed: pre-1914 strengths, pre-1914 weaknesses, the war as accelerator, balanced verdict. ⚠️ The single most common way to lose marks is to present the historiography as a list of names. Present it as two claims - about timing and about type - and then allocate the named positions to those claims. ⚠️ For the modernisation demand, the disciplined answer uses the industrial-unrest figures to show a real industrial working class existing before 1917 while conceding that it was numerically small relative to the peasantry.

### Traps

- Writing that Stolypin was a reactionary or that he was a liberal. ✅ The record is both: roughly 4000 executions and the abolition of redemption payments.
- Giving the land-reform figures without the demographic figure that defeated them.
- Presenting the strike statistics as a total rather than as a rising series through 1912, 1913 and the first seven months of 1914.
- Treating the revival of the revolutionary parties as the cause of the strike wave. ✅ The account has *Pravda* giving direction to an already developing wave.
- Making Rasputin the explanation. He is evidence of a symptom.
- Stating any position on inevitability as settled, in either direction.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Stolypin, prime minister 1906-11: some 4000 executions in three years; redemption payments abolished; about 2 million peasants had bought land by 1916; about 3.5 million emigrated to Siberia; a kulak class emerges as an intended conservative prop.
2. ✅ Factory inspection expands; 1912 sickness and accident insurance; 1908 universal-education programme; 50 000 extra primary schools by 1914.
3. ✅ Land reform failing by 1911: peasant population growing at 1.5 million a year; Stolypin assassinated 1911.
4. ✅ Lena goldfields, April 1912: 270 striking gold miners shot; over 2000 strikes in 1912, 2400 in 1913, over 4000 in the first seven months of 1914.
5. ✅ Repression alienated peasants, industrial workers and the intelligentsia simultaneously.
6. ✅ The 1903 split; *Pravda* founded 1912; the SRs separate and non-Marxist.
7. ✅ The dynasty discredited by the manner of Stolypin's murder and by Rasputin; the Duma's 1912 request ignored.
8. ✅ July 1914: a Bolshevik-organised general strike in the capital, ending on 15 July with the government still controlling army and police.
9. ✅ Five recorded positions on inevitability: traditional liberal; Soviet (war delayed it); Kennan and Haimson; Fitzpatrick; Read and Service.
10. ⚠️ The safe verdict: vulnerable before 1914; the war made collapse overwhelmingly more likely without making every step predetermined.

### Concept check

**Question:** Separate the two distinct claims buried inside the question "were the revolutions of 1917 inevitable?", and show which recorded positions bear on each.

**Model answer:** The first claim is about **timing**: was some serious upheaval probable in the short term? ✅ On this the evidence is strong on both sides - the strike series rising from over 2000 in 1912 to over 4000 in the first seven months of 1914, and a Bolshevik-organised general strike in the capital in July 1914 with demonstrations, shootings and barricades, against a government that still controlled the army and the police and had just seen 50 000 primary schools opened and an insurance scheme introduced. Kennan, Haimson and Fitzpatrick support probability; Read denies it. The second claim is about **type**: was a revolution that destroyed the entire social structure and produced a one-party state probable? ⚠️ Here the evidence is far weaker, because the party that produced that outcome was a minority within a minority in 1914, and the account records the revolutionary parties as short of money, split and led from exile only two years earlier. Service's formulation captures the distinction exactly: brittleness yes, but not doom, and what made root-and-branch revolution possible was the protracted, exhausting conflict of the war. ⚠️ The verdict that follows is not a compromise but a division of labour - structural weakness explains why upheaval was likely; the war explains why it took the form it did.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Soviet position as merely propaganda. It contains a testable and partly supported claim - that deterioration resumed before 1914 - and its distinctive twist, that the war *delayed* rather than caused the revolution, is a serious argument that an answer should meet rather than ignore.

## Lesson 4 - 1914-17: how a war converted fragility into collapse

Progress: 4/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the boundary with the First World War owner; the four mechanisms by which the war destroyed the state's capacity; the paradox of a competent army and an incompetent supply system; the decision of August 1915 and its political cost; and the moment at which the elite concluded that the Tsar had to go

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.1(e), on the war failures, supply and transport, the August 1915 appointment and the elite turn of January 1917
