### Visual: the transmission chain from a war to a bread queue

```text
   THE BOUNDARY, MARKED FIRST
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE EASTERN FRONT AS A CAMPAIGN - battles, fronts, casualties |
   | and the strategic conduct of the war - BELONGS TO THE FIRST   |
   | WORLD WAR OWNER. Nothing about it is re-derived here.         |
   | THIS SESSION USES ONLY THE DOMESTIC TRANSMISSION.             |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+

   MECHANISM 1: SUPPLY            MECHANISM 2: TRANSPORT
   incompetent and corrupt        arms and ammunition slow to reach
   organisation; shortage of      the front; and, decisively, there
   equipment                      was PLENTY OF FOOD IN THE COUNTRY
        |                         but it did not reach the big cities
        |                         because the military monopolised
        |                         most of the trains
        |                              |
        +--------------+---------------+
                       |
              BREAD SCARCE AND VERY EXPENSIVE IN THE CAPITAL
              -> note what this is NOT: it is not a harvest failure.
                 It is a distribution failure produced by the war.
                       |
   MECHANISM 3: COMMAND           MECHANISM 4: LEGITIMACY
   Nicholas appoints himself      by January 1917 the aristocracy,
   supreme commander, Aug 1915;   the Duma, many industrialists and
   his tactical blunders throw    the army are turning against him -
   away the gains of Brusilov's   not to make a revolution but to
   1916 offensive; he draws on    PREVENT one that might sweep away
   himself the blame for later    the whole social structure
   defeats and the high death
   rate
                       |
              GENERAL KRIMOV TO A SECRET MEETING OF DUMA MEMBERS,
              END OF 1916: "We would welcome the news of a coup
              d'etat. A revolution is imminent and we at the front
              feel it to be so. If you decide on such an extreme
              step, we will support you. Clearly there is no other
              way."
                       |
              BY FEBRUARY 1917 THERE IS NOBODY LEFT TO DEFEND THE
              AUTOCRACY - which is the condition, not the trigger.
```

*The chain is worth memorising in this order because it answers the commonest sub-question in the topic: why were there bread riots in a country with plenty of food? ✅ Because the trains were carrying soldiers.*

### 1. What the war did that the pre-war crisis had not

✅ **Fact:** historians agree that Russian failures in the war made revolution certain, causing troops and police to mutiny, so that there was nobody left to defend the autocracy.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read that sentence for its logic and not merely for its content. The recorded mechanism is not that the war made people angrier. It is that the war destroyed the *instrument of repression*. ⚠️ In 1905 most of the army remained loyal and the regime survived a general strike, a mutiny in the fleet and an attempted revolution. In 1917 the garrison of the capital mutinied within four days of the first bread riot. **Grievance explains the crowds; the collapse of the coercive apparatus explains the outcome.** A script that lists wartime hardships and stops has explained the crowds only.

### 2. The supply and transport failures, exactly

✅ **Fact:** the war revealed incompetent and corrupt organisation and the shortage of equipment. ✅ Poor transport organisation and distribution meant that arms and ammunition were slow to reach the front; and although there was plenty of food in the country, it did not get to the big cities in sufficient quantities, because most of the trains were being monopolised by the military. ✅ Bread was scarce and very expensive.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "plenty of food in the country" is the most examinable clause in the whole war section, because it forecloses the lazy explanation. ⚠️ The February revolution did not begin in a famine. It began in a distribution crisis inside a country with food, which means the target of popular anger was necessarily the *administration* rather than fate or weather. That is a political fact, and it is why bread riots became a political revolution within four days rather than remaining a subsistence protest.

### 3. The paradox the strongest answers use

✅ **Fact:** Norman Stone has shown that the Russian army acquitted itself reasonably well, and Brusilov's 1916 offensive was an impressive success. ✅ Nicholas made the fatal mistake of appointing himself supreme commander in August 1915; his tactical blunders threw away all the advantages won by Brusilov's offensive, and drew on himself the blame for later defeats and for the high death rate.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this pair of facts is the most efficient way to demolish the standard caricature of a hopeless Russian war effort, and it converts a narrative into an argument. ⚠️ If the army could produce an impressive offensive in 1916, then the collapse of 1917 cannot be attributed to military incapacity as such; it has to be attributed to the political and administrative system that could not sustain, supply or lead what the army could do. ⚠️ And the August 1915 decision is the hinge, because it is the point at which an institution's failures became one man's personal responsibility. An autocrat who takes personal command cannot afterwards blame the generals. **Nicholas converted a systemic failure into a personal indictment, and did so voluntarily.**

### 4. The moment the elite changed sides

✅ **Fact:** by January 1917 most groups in society were disillusioned with the incompetent way the Tsar was running the war. ✅ The aristocracy, the Duma, many industrialists and the army were beginning to turn against Nicholas, feeling that it would be better to sacrifice him to avoid a much worse revolution that might sweep away the whole social structure. ✅ General Krimov told a secret meeting of Duma members at the end of 1916: "We would welcome the news of a coup d'etat. A revolution is imminent and we at the front feel it to be so. If you decide on such an extreme step, we will support you. Clearly there is no other way."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** note the motive recorded, because it is counter-intuitive and therefore valuable. The elite turned against the Tsar **in order to prevent a revolution**, not to make one. ⚠️ That is the fact that makes the Katkov reading of February in Lesson 5 intelligible rather than merely cynical, and it also supplies the sharpest available formulation for a 15-mark answer: *by the end of 1916 the monarchy had lost not only the streets but the people who had the most to lose from losing the streets.*

### 5. Comparison: three ways of stating the war's causal role

| Formulation | What it claims | Status |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ "The war caused the revolution" | The war is sufficient | Unsafe. It cannot explain the 1912-14 strike series, the constitutional failure, or the land question, all of which predate it |
| ⚠️ "The war merely triggered an inevitable revolution" | The war is incidental | Unsafe in the other direction, and contradicted by Read and Service on the *type* of revolution that followed |
| ⚠️ "The war converted a survivable vulnerability into an unsurvivable one by destroying the state's administrative and coercive capacity" | The war is the necessary converting variable acting on pre-existing structures | ✅ Supported by the whole evidence chain of this lesson and consistent with the advanced owner's recorded verdict |

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** naming the war as the converting variable is a way of exculpating the regime. On this account the autocracy was merely unlucky.

⚠️ **Reply:** only if the war is treated as a natural disaster, and it was not. ⚠️ The decision to enter and continue the war was a decision of the same government, taken by the same autocrat who had narrowed the franchise, and the single most damaging military-political act of the period - the assumption of supreme command in August 1915 - was a personal choice with no constitutional check on it, precisely because no such check had been permitted to develop. ⚠️ The war is therefore not external to the regime's record; it is where the regime's structural feature, unaccountable personal rule, produced its most expensive consequence. **Saying that the war was decisive is not saying that the regime was blameless; it is saying where the blame attaches.**

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far would you agree that the impact of the First World War was the main reason for the downfall of Nicholas II in 1917?
> ⚠️ Why did tsarism collapse?
> ⚠️ Assess the domestic consequences of the First World War for Russia.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the word "main" is doing all the work and the answer must produce a ranking rather than a list. The workable ranking is: the war is the proximate and necessary cause; the constitutional failure of 1906-07 is what made the war fatal rather than merely damaging; the land and labour questions supply the mass; the dynasty's disrepute removes the last resource. ⚠️ Concede the recovery evidence explicitly - it is what makes the answer look read rather than remembered - and then show that recovery evidence is precisely what makes the war decisive.

### Traps

- Writing that Russia was starving in February 1917. ✅ There was plenty of food in the country; the trains were the problem.
- Describing the Russian army as uniformly incompetent. ✅ Stone's finding and the Brusilov offensive are on the record.
- Omitting August 1915. It is the single most consequential political decision of the Russian war.
- Treating the elite's turn against Nicholas as revolutionary sympathy. ✅ It was the opposite: an attempt to pre-empt revolution.
- Re-fighting the eastern front. That belongs to the First World War owner and earns nothing here.
- Using "the war caused the revolution" as a thesis. It is a premise pretending to be one.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The recorded mechanism: war failures made troops and police mutiny, leaving nobody to defend the autocracy.
2. ✅ Incompetent and corrupt organisation, shortage of equipment and arms slow to the front; and, decisively, plenty of food in the country that did not reach the cities because the military monopolised the trains, so bread was scarce and very expensive.
3. ✅ Stone: the army acquitted itself reasonably well; Brusilov's 1916 offensive was an impressive success.
4. ✅ August 1915: Nicholas appoints himself supreme commander; blunders throw away Brusilov's gains; he draws the blame for later defeats and the death rate.
5. ✅ By January 1917 the aristocracy, the Duma, many industrialists and the army are turning against him to avoid a worse revolution - Krimov telling Duma members at the end of 1916 that a coup would be welcomed and that "Clearly there is no other way."
6. ⚠️ The distribution-not-famine point is the highest-value single clause in this lesson.
7. ⚠️ The safe causal formulation: the war converted a survivable vulnerability into an unsurvivable one by destroying administrative and coercive capacity.
8. ⚠️ Naming the war as decisive locates blame rather than removing it: the war was prosecuted by the same unaccountable personal rule.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain, in four steps, how a war fought hundreds of kilometres away produced bread riots in Petrograd in February 1917 - and say what the explanation rules out.

**Model answer:** Step one: the military monopolised most of the trains, so although there was plenty of food in the country it did not reach the big cities in sufficient quantities, and bread became scarce and very expensive. Step two: the same transport and organisational failure - incompetent and corrupt organisation, shortage of equipment, arms and ammunition slow to reach the front - meant the war itself went badly, so the shortages could not be redeemed by victory. Step three: Nicholas's assumption of supreme command in August 1915 attached the resulting defeats and death rate to him personally, so administrative failure became dynastic failure. Step four: by January 1917 the aristocracy, the Duma, many industrialists and the army had concluded that he should be sacrificed to prevent a worse revolution, which removed the regime's last defenders before the first crowd formed. ⚠️ What the explanation rules out is the famine reading: this was not a subsistence crisis produced by nature but a distribution crisis produced by administration, which is exactly why the anger had a political address.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the army's mutiny in February proves the army had been beaten. The Petrograd garrison was a reserve and training force in the capital; Stone's assessment of the field army's performance and the Brusilov offensive belong to a different question and should not be merged with it.

## Lesson 5 - February/March 1917: the chronology, the abdication, and the argument about who made it

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the eight-day sequence from bread riot to abdication; the point at which troops stopped firing; the Duma's advice and its rejection; the abdication in the imperial train and the refusal of the Grand Duke Michael; and the four recorded readings of what kind of revolution this was

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.2 opening calendar note and §16.2(a), on the February sequence, the abdication and the from-above/from-below debate
🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson
📰 CA Found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: eight days, with the hinge marked

```text
   PETROGRAD, FEBRUARY 1917 (JULIAN CALENDAR; ADD 13 DAYS FOR THE
   GREGORIAN DATE OUTSIDE RUSSIA)

   23 Feb | BREAD RIOTS BREAK OUT
          | rioters quickly joined by thousands of strikers from a
          | nearby armaments factory
             |
          | THE TSAR ORDERS TROOPS TO USE FORCE. 40 PEOPLE KILLED.
          | at this point the regime is still doing what worked in 1905
             |
   >>>>>> THE HINGE >>>>>>
          | SOME TROOPS BEGIN TO REFUSE TO FIRE ON UNARMED CROWDS
          | THEN THE WHOLE PETROGRAD GARRISON MUTINIES
          | -> everything after this point is about who fills a vacuum,
          |    not about whether the regime can hold
             |
          | MOBS SEIZE PUBLIC BUILDINGS, RELEASE PRISONERS FROM JAILS,
          | TAKE OVER POLICE STATIONS AND ARSENALS
             |
          | THE DUMA ADVISES A CONSTITUTIONAL MONARCHY
          | NICHOLAS REFUSES and sends more troops to restore order
             |
          | THE DUMA AND THE GENERALS CONCLUDE HE MUST GO;
          | senior generals tell him the only way to save the monarchy
          | is to renounce the throne
             |
   2 Mar  | ABDICATION, in the imperial train standing in a siding near
          | Pskov, in favour of his brother the Grand Duke Michael
             |
          | NOBODY HAD MADE SURE MICHAEL WOULD ACCEPT.
          | HE REFUSED. THE RUSSIAN MONARCHY CAME TO AN END.

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   TWO DETAILS THAT SEPARATE A READ ANSWER FROM A REMEMBERED ONE:
   (1) the abdication was in favour of a brother, not a son;
   (2) the dynasty ended by a declination, not by a decree.
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The hinge is the third box. ⚠️ Before it, this is 1905 repeating; after it, there is no armed force in the capital that will act for the Tsar, and every subsequent decision - the Duma's, the generals', Nicholas's own - is taken in that knowledge.*

### 1. The sequence, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the first revolution began on 23 February when bread riots broke out in Petrograd. ✅ The rioters were quickly joined by thousands of strikers from a nearby armaments factory. ✅ The Tsar sent orders for the troops to use force to end the demonstrations, and 40 people were killed. ✅ Soon some of the troops began to refuse to fire at the unarmed crowds and the whole Petrograd garrison mutinied. ✅ Mobs seized public buildings, released prisoners from jails and took over police stations and arsenals. ✅ The Duma advised Nicholas to set up a constitutional monarchy, but he refused and sent more troops to Petrograd to try to restore order. ✅ This convinced the Duma and the generals that Nicholas, who was on his way back to Petrograd, would have to go; some of his senior generals told him that the only way to save the monarchy was for him to renounce the throne. ✅ On 2 March, in the imperial train standing in a siding near Pskov, the Tsar abdicated in favour of his brother, the Grand Duke Michael. ✅ Unfortunately nobody had made sure that Michael would accept the throne, so when he refused, the Russian monarchy came to an end.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence contains its own argument if it is told in order. Nicholas was offered a survivable outcome twice - a constitutional monarchy, and then a regency-style succession - and the first he refused and the second he mishandled. ⚠️ **The monarchy in Russia did not fall to a republican movement. It fell because the person holding it declined the two forms in which it could have continued.** That is a sharper thesis than "the Tsar was overthrown", and it is fully supported by the recorded sequence.

### 2. What nobody planned

✅ **Fact:** most people expected the autocracy to be replaced by a democratic republic with an elected parliament.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** hold that sentence against the rest of the topic. In early March 1917 the expected destination was a parliamentary republic; within eleven months an elected constituent assembly would be dispersed by armed force. ⚠️ Nothing in February pointed to the outcome of January 1918, which is the strongest single piece of evidence against reading 1917 backwards from 1918. A February answer that anticipates Bolshevism has imported a conclusion into its own premises.

### 3. Four readings of February, and the evidence for each

| Reading | As recorded | Evidential strength |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Revolution from above** (George Katkov) | The conspiracy among the elite was the decisive factor: nobles, Duma members and generals forced Nicholas to abdicate in order to prevent a real mass revolution developing | ⚠️ Strong on the final act - the abdication was procured by generals and Duma members - and supported by the Krimov statement of late 1916. Weak on the first four days, which no elite organised |
| ✅ **Revolution from below, spontaneous** (W. H. Chamberlin, 1935) | "One of the most leaderless, spontaneous, anonymous revolutions of all time." The revolution from below by the masses was decisive because it threw the elite into a panic; without the crowds on the streets there would have been no need for the elite to act | ⚠️ Strong on causation and on the sequence. It also accounts for why the elite acted when it did |
| ✅ **Revolution from below, organised** (Soviet historiography) | Agreed with Chamberlin that it came from below, but denied it was spontaneous, making a strong case that the Bolsheviks had played a vital role in organising strikes and demonstrations | ⚠️ Has to contend with the recorded absence of the Bolshevik leadership - Lenin in Switzerland - and with the finding that many activists among the workers were not affiliated to any political group |
| ✅ **Social-historical** (Christopher Read, Diane Koenker, Steve Smith) | A mass uprising organised from below but not necessarily by the Bolsheviks; workers were motivated by economic considerations rather than politics; in Smith's words, "it was an outburst of desperation to secure the basic material needs and a decent standard of living" | ⚠️ The best-evidenced reading of the crowds themselves, and the one that explains why the movement had no programme beyond bread and an end to the war |

✅ **Fact:** none of the traditional liberal historians thought the revolutionary parties had played a significant role in organising the events.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four readings are not mutually exclusive once the event is divided into its phases, and that division is the highest-scoring move available. ⚠️ **Phase one - 23 to 26 February - is from below and substantially unorganised: bread, wages and war-weariness, with activists present but no party directing. Phase two - the garrison mutiny - is from below and decisive, because it disarms the regime. Phase three - the abdication - is from above: generals and Duma members acting to contain what phases one and two had produced.** That is the formulation the advanced owner endorses as the mains use: **a mass uprising that panicked the elite into decisive action.**

### 4. What the Duma did, and what it was

✅ **Fact:** the Duma, struggling to take control, set up a mainly liberal provisional government with Prince George Lvov as prime minister; in July he was replaced by Alexander Kerensky, a moderate socialist.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "struggling to take control" is not decoration. The body that formed the successor government was the Fourth Duma - the conservative, powerless chamber elected on the narrowed franchise of 1907 that had never been a check on the Tsar. ⚠️ It therefore inherited office without inheriting either a democratic mandate or an administrative machine, and it did so in a city whose armed force had just mutinied and whose workers had just elected a rival body. Lesson 6 is about what that combination produced.

### 5. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** dividing February into three phases is a scholastic device. The revolution was one event and the historians are arguing about emphasis.

⚠️ **Reply:** they are arguing about causation, and the phases are how the argument is tested. ⚠️ Ask the counterfactual that the disagreement turns on: if the garrison had obeyed the order to fire, would the elite have moved? Katkov's reading implies yes, because the conspiracy was the driver; Chamberlin's implies no, because the elite acted out of panic at the crowds. ✅ The recorded sequence supports Chamberlin - the elite's decisive intervention follows the mutiny, not the reverse, and the Krimov statement of late 1916 shows that elite disaffection existed for months *without* producing action. ⚠️ Disaffection that waits for a crowd is a reaction, not a cause. The phases are what make that visible.

### 6. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far would you agree that the February/March revolution which overthrew the Russian monarchy was a "spontaneous uprising"?
> ⚠️ Distinguish the February and October revolutions of 1917.
> ⚠️ Why did the Romanov dynasty fall?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the spontaneity demand, do not answer yes or no. Divide the event, allocate a verdict to each phase, and then state the composite: unorganised in origin, decisive through the mutiny, completed from above. ⚠️ Quote Chamberlin's formulation once - "one of the most leaderless, spontaneous, anonymous revolutions of all time" - and then qualify it with Smith's economic finding, which shows what the crowds were actually demanding.

### Traps

- Dating February by the Gregorian calendar without saying so.
- Writing that the Tsar was deposed. ✅ He abdicated, and the monarchy ended when Michael declined.
- Saying he abdicated in favour of his son. ✅ The abdication named his brother, the Grand Duke Michael.
- Crediting the Bolsheviks with organising February. ✅ Lenin was in exile in Switzerland and returned only in April.
- Treating "spontaneous" and "from below" as the same claim. Soviet historiography accepted the second and denied the first.
- Omitting the garrison mutiny, which is the causal hinge of the whole event.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 23 February (Julian): bread riots in Petrograd, joined by strikers from a nearby armaments factory.
2. ✅ Troops ordered to use force; 40 people killed; then troops refuse to fire and the whole Petrograd garrison mutinies.
3. ✅ Mobs seize public buildings, release prisoners, take police stations and arsenals.
4. ✅ The Duma advises a constitutional monarchy; Nicholas refuses and sends more troops.
5. ✅ Senior generals tell him renunciation is the only way to save the monarchy.
6. ✅ 2 March: abdication in the imperial train near Pskov, in favour of the Grand Duke Michael, who refuses; the monarchy ends.
7. ✅ Katkov: elite conspiracy decisive. Chamberlin: leaderless, spontaneous, anonymous; the crowds panicked the elite.
8. ✅ Soviet historiography: from below but organised, with a Bolshevik role. Read, Koenker and Smith: from below, not necessarily Bolshevik, and economically motivated; Smith's "outburst of desperation".
9. ✅ The expectation in March 1917 was a democratic republic with an elected parliament.
10. ✅ The Duma set up a mainly liberal provisional government under Prince George Lvov; Kerensky replaced him in July.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script asserts that "the February Revolution was organised by the Bolsheviks". Identify the three evidential problems with the claim and state the strongest version of the claim that the evidence will support.

**Model answer:** The first problem is leadership: ✅ Lenin was in exile in Switzerland and returned only in April, after a political amnesty, travelling through Germany in a sealed train; the party's most senior figures were not in the capital directing events. The second is affiliation: ✅ recent work has shown that many activists among the workers were not affiliated to any political group, and that the motivation recorded by Read, Koenker and Smith was economic - better conditions, higher wages and control over their own lives - rather than programmatic. The third is the verdict of the liberal historiography, which the account records plainly: none of the traditional liberal historians thought the revolutionary parties had played a significant role in organising the events. ⚠️ The strongest supportable version is the Soviet one in its restrained form: Bolshevik activists participated in organising strikes and demonstrations within a wave they did not originate and could not control, and *Pravda* had been giving direction to an already developing strike movement since 1912. That claim is defensible; "the Bolsheviks organised February" is not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because the Bolsheviks did not make February they were irrelevant in 1917. Lesson 7 shows how a party absent in February became the beneficiary of the four episodes that followed.

---

## Lesson 6 - Dual power: a government in office and a soviet with the soldiers

Progress: 6/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what a soviet was and when the Petrograd body was elected; the mechanism of dual power and the order that created it; the five structural problems the Provisional Government could not solve; and why "it was weak" is a description rather than an explanation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.2(b) and the first five items of §16.2(c), on the provisional government, the Petrograd soviet, and the failures over the war, elections, land and the economy
🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson
📰 CA Found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two bodies, one city, and the asymmetry between them

```text
   PETROGRAD, MARCH-OCTOBER 1917

   +---------------------------------+  +---------------------------------+
   | THE PROVISIONAL GOVERNMENT      |  | THE PETROGRAD SOVIET            |
   | set up by the Duma; mainly      |  | an elected committee of         |
   | liberal; Prince G. Lvov, then   |  | soldiers' and workers'          |
   | Kerensky from July              |  | representatives, elected at the |
   |                                 |  | END OF FEBRUARY - BEFORE the    |
   | HAS: legal office; ministries;  |  | Tsar's abdication               |
   | international recognition; the  |  |                                 |
   | obligations of an ally at war   |  | HAS: the loyalty of the armed   |
   |                                 |  | men in the capital; similar     |
   | LACKS: an electoral mandate;    |  | bodies appeared in Moscow and   |
   | a reliable armed force;         |  | all the provincial cities       |
   | a solution to the land question |  |                                 |
   | it is willing to implement now  |  | LACKS: legal authority;         |
   |                                 |  | responsibility for governing;   |
   |                                 |  | any obligation to the allies    |
   +---------------------------------+  +---------------------------------+
                  |                                    |
                  |     THE ORDER THAT DECIDED IT      |
                  +------------------+-----------------+
                                     |
        WHEN THE PETROGRAD SOVIET ORDERED ALL SOLDIERS TO OBEY ONLY
        THE SOVIET, IT MEANT THAT IN THE LAST RESORT THE PROVISIONAL
        GOVERNMENT COULD NOT RELY ON THE SUPPORT OF THE ARMY.

                                     |
        THE RESULTING STRUCTURE, STATED AS A MECHANISM:
        RESPONSIBILITY WITHOUT POWER   |   POWER WITHOUT RESPONSIBILITY
        the government must decide,    |   the soviet can refuse, demand
        and cannot enforce             |   and withhold, and need not govern
                                     |
        -> EVERY DECISION THE GOVERNMENT TAKES IS EITHER UNPOPULAR OR
           UNENFORCEABLE. THAT IS NOT WEAKNESS; IT IS A DESIGN FAULT.
```

*This is the diagram to reproduce in an answer on why the Provisional Government fell. ⚠️ It replaces six adjectives with one mechanism, and mechanisms score.*

### 1. What a soviet was, and when this one was elected

✅ **Fact:** the Petrograd soviet was an elected committee of soldiers' and workers' representatives which tried to govern the city; it had been elected at the end of February, before the Tsar's abdication. ✅ Other soviets appeared in Moscow and all the provincial cities.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the election date is the detail that carries the argument, and it is routinely omitted. ⚠️ The soviet was not created in opposition to the Provisional Government; it *predates* it. The Provisional Government was formed into a city that already had a functioning representative body with the confidence of the armed men in it. That is why dual power was structural from the first day rather than something that developed as the government lost support.

### 2. The order, and what it actually did

✅ **Fact:** when the Petrograd soviet ordered all soldiers to obey only the soviet, it meant that in the last resort the Provisional Government could not rely on the support of the army.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** state this as an instrument and not as an atmosphere. ⚠️ A government's capacity is ultimately the capacity to be obeyed when it is not agreed with. From March 1917 the Provisional Government had no such capacity in its own capital, because the soldiers had been instructed - by a body they had elected - to take their orders elsewhere. ⚠️ Every later failure in the list below is downstream of that one fact, which is why the strongest answers state it early and then use it to explain the rest rather than listing it fifth among seven causes.

### 3. The five structural problems, each traced to the mechanism

| Problem | What the record says | How dual power makes it insoluble |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **The war** | It took the unpopular decision to continue the war; the June offensive, Kerensky's idea, was another disastrous failure, causing the collapse of army morale and discipline and sending hundreds of thousands of deserting troops streaming home | ⚠️ Continuing the war required an army that obeyed the government; the government did not have one. Ending it required abandoning the allies and the government's own claim to legitimacy as a responsible successor state |
| ✅ **Elections** | It lost support because it delayed the promised elections for a Constituent Assembly, arguing that these were not possible in the middle of a war with several million troops away fighting | ⚠️ The argument was administratively reasonable and politically fatal, because the delay left the government without the mandate that would have let it override the soviet |
| ✅ **Land** | Another promise not kept was land reform - the redistribution of land from large estates among peasants. Tired of waiting, some peasants started to seize land from landlords; the Bolsheviks were able to use peasant discontent to win support | ⚠️ Redistribution before an elected assembly would have pre-empted the assembly; redistribution after it required the assembly the government kept postponing. Meanwhile the countryside solved the problem without permission |
| ✅ **The economy** | Increasing economic chaos, with inflation, rising bread prices, lagging wages and shortages of raw materials and fuel; industry severely handicapped by a shortage of investment | ⚠️ Recovery required either coercion or confidence, and the government could supply neither |
| ✅ **The alternative on offer** | Lenin and the Bolsheviks put forward what seemed a realistic and attractive policy: a separate peace with Germany to get Russia out of the war, all land to be given to the peasants, workers' control in the factories, and more food at cheaper prices | ⚠️ Every item on that list is a thing the Provisional Government could not do, for the reasons above. The programme was designed against the constraint |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the table to carry into any question on why the Provisional Government fell, because it converts a cause list into a causal architecture. ⚠️ **The government did not fail at five separate tasks. It failed at one task - governing without the power to enforce - and that single failure expressed itself in five places.** The basic owner states the point in exactly those terms: dual power meant that the government held office while the soviets held the loyalty of the soldiers and workers who could enforce anything, and that gap, not any single mistake, is why it fell.

### 4. The counter-consideration, stated fairly

⚠️ **Objection worth raising in an answer before the examiner does:** the government's choices were not all constrained. Continuing the war was a decision; the June offensive was, on the record, Kerensky's own idea; and the delay of elections was a judgement that could have gone the other way.

⚠️ **Reply:** correct, and the honest answer concedes it. ⚠️ Dual power explains why the menu was short; it does not explain why the worst item on it was chosen twice. The June offensive is the clearest instance: it was optional, it was catastrophic, and the recorded consequence - collapse of army morale and discipline, hundreds of thousands of deserters - transferred armed men from the front to the cities where the soviets were strongest. ⚠️ **A first-class answer therefore separates the structural verdict from the decisional one: the structure made success very difficult; the decisions made failure quick.**

### 5. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the Provisional Government collapse so quickly in 1917?
> ⚠️ How far was popular dissatisfaction with the Provisional Government responsible for its overthrow in October/November 1917?
> ⚠️ Explain the concept of dual power and its consequences.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Open with the mechanism in one sentence, give the order about obeying only the soviet as the evidence, and then run the five problems as expressions of it rather than as a list. ⚠️ Concede the decisional point about the June offensive in one clause; it demonstrates that the answer is weighing rather than reciting. ⚠️ For the second framing, note the trap in the stem: "popular dissatisfaction" invites an answer about mood, and the better answer shows that dissatisfaction became decisive only because an organised body existed that could convert it into armed action.

### Traps

- Writing that the Provisional Government "was weak" and leaving it there.
- Dating the Petrograd soviet after the abdication. ✅ It was elected at the end of February, before it.
- Treating the soviets as a Bolshevik institution in the spring of 1917, or as a Petrograd peculiarity. ✅ The Bolsheviks won majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets only by October, and soviets existed in Moscow and all the provincial cities, which is what made the model national rather than local.
- Blaming the government for not redistributing land without noticing the constitutional argument against doing so before the Assembly met.
- Omitting the June offensive, which is the decision that converted a structural problem into a collapse.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Petrograd soviet: an elected committee of soldiers' and workers' representatives, elected at the end of February 1917, before the abdication.
2. ✅ Similar bodies appeared in Moscow and all the provincial cities.
3. ✅ The soviet ordered all soldiers to obey only the soviet; in the last resort the government could not rely on the army.
4. ⚠️ The mechanism: responsibility without power against power without responsibility.
5. ✅ The war continued; the June offensive was Kerensky's idea and a disastrous failure; army morale and discipline collapsed; hundreds of thousands deserted.
6. ✅ Constituent Assembly elections delayed on the argument that they were impossible in wartime with millions at the front.
7. ✅ Land reform promised and not delivered; peasants began seizing land; the Bolsheviks used the discontent.
8. ✅ Economic chaos: inflation, rising bread prices, lagging wages, shortages of raw materials and fuel, and a shortage of investment in industry.
9. ✅ The Bolshevik counter-programme: separate peace, land to the peasants, workers' control, cheaper food.
10. ⚠️ The composite verdict: one failure - office without enforcement - expressed in five places, plus optional decisions that accelerated it.

### Concept check

**Question:** State in three sentences why "the Provisional Government was weak" is an unsatisfactory answer, and what should replace it.

**Model answer:** It is unsatisfactory because weakness is the phenomenon to be explained, not the explanation: the government had legal office, ministries, international recognition and the acquiescence of the old administrative apparatus, and none of that is what "weak" normally denotes. ✅ What it lacked was the capacity to be obeyed, and the source of that lack is identifiable and dated - a rival elected body in the same city, in existence before the government was formed, which instructed all soldiers to obey only itself, so that in the last resort the government could not rely on the army. ⚠️ The replacement formulation is therefore mechanical rather than evaluative: the Provisional Government held responsibility without power while the soviet held power without responsibility, which meant that every major decision open to it - on the war, on elections, on land, on the economy - was either unpopular or unenforceable, and the Bolshevik programme was constructed precisely out of the things it could not do.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating dual power as a temporary awkwardness resolved by October. It was the operative constitutional reality of Russia for eight months, and the October seizure was made *in the name of* the soviet precisely because that was where the enforceable authority already lay.

---

## Lesson 7 - April to September 1917: four episodes, and the transfer of advantage

Progress: 7/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Lenin's return and the April Theses; the June offensive; the July Days and the Bolshevik setback; the Kornilov affair and the reversal; the disputed authorship of July; and the arithmetic of soviet majorities by October

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.2(c), items 4 and 6 to 8, on the April Theses, the July Days, the Kornilov affair and the October soviet majorities
🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the advantage curve, with the four turning points marked

```text
   BOLSHEVIK POSITION, APRIL TO OCTOBER 1917
   (direction of movement, not a measured series)

   HIGH  |                                            ,---- OCTOBER
         |                                          ,'      majority in the
         |                                        ,'        Petrograd AND
         |                                      ,'          Moscow soviets;
         |         APRIL                      ,'            still a minority
         |         Lenin returns;           ,'              in the country
         |         April Theses           ,'                as a whole
         |            .----------.      ,'
         |          ,'            `.  ,'
         |        ,'                `'
         |      ,'                  JULY DAYS: sharp fall.
   LOW   |____,'                    Bolsheviks accused of an uprising;
         +--------------------------Lenin falsely reported a German spy;
           APR    JUN    JUL    AUG  he flees to Finland; leaders arrested
                   |             |
                   |             +-- KORNILOV, AUGUST: the reversal.
                   |                 The commander-in-chief moves troops
                   |                 on Petrograd; many of his soldiers
                   |                 mutiny; Kerensky orders his arrest;
                   |                 public opinion swings against the war
                   |                 and towards the only party openly
                   |                 talking about a separate peace
                   |
                   +-- JUNE OFFENSIVE: Kerensky's own idea; a disastrous
                       failure; army morale and discipline collapse;
                       hundreds of thousands of deserters stream home

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   THE PATTERN: the Bolsheviks gained twice by other people's actions
   (June, August) and lost once by an action that may not have been
   theirs (July). Advantage was transferred to them more often than
   it was seized by them - up to mid-October.
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*This curve is the corrective to two opposite errors: the account in which a masterful Lenin engineers everything, and the account in which the Bolsheviks merely drift into power. ⚠️ The record shows a party that was well placed to exploit other people's failures and nearly destroyed in July by a crisis it may not have started.*

### 1. April: the return, and the programme

✅ **Fact:** thanks to a new political amnesty, Lenin was able to return from exile in Switzerland in April; the Germans allowed him to travel through to Petrograd in a special sealed train, in the hope that he would cause further chaos in Russia. ✅ After a rapturous welcome he urged, in his April Theses, that the Bolsheviks should cease to support the provisional government, that all power should be taken by the soviets, and that Russia should withdraw from the war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three clauses of the April Theses are not three policies; they are one strategic decision with three faces. ⚠️ Refusing to support the government forgoes the influence that support would buy and buys something more valuable: freedom from responsibility for everything the government is about to fail at. Demanding all power to the soviets attaches the party to the institution that already holds the armed men. Demanding withdrawal from the war is the only position from which both of the others are credible. **Together they position a small party as the only opposition that is not implicated in the coming failure** - which is exactly what June, July and August then supplied.

⚠️ A further point of discipline: the German decision to let Lenin travel is a recorded fact with a recorded motive, and it should be reported as such and not inflated. ⚠️ It establishes that a belligerent power expected him to cause disruption. It does not establish the far stronger claim - made falsely and in bad faith in July - that he was an agent, and the account is explicit that the report was false.

### 2. June: the offensive that emptied the front

✅ **Fact:** the June offensive was Kerensky's idea and another disastrous failure; it caused the collapse of army morale and discipline and sent hundreds of thousands of deserting troops streaming home.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most under-used fact in the topic, and it does double work. First, it destroys the government's remaining military instrument. Second, and more importantly, it *relocates* armed men: hundreds of thousands of soldiers with rifles and grievances moved from the front into the countryside and the cities, where the soviets were organised and the land seizures were beginning. ⚠️ **The June offensive did not merely lose a battle; it redistributed armed force from the state to the localities.**

### 3. July: the crisis, and the authorship dispute

✅ **Fact:** the government lost popularity because of the July Days. ✅ On 3 July there was a huge demonstration of workers, soldiers and sailors who marched on the Tauride Palace where both the provisional government and the Petrograd soviet were meeting. ✅ They demanded that the soviet should take power, but the members refused to take the responsibility. ✅ The government brought loyal troops from the front to restore order and accused the Bolsheviks of trying to launch an uprising; it was reported, falsely, that Lenin was a German spy. ✅ At this, the popularity of the Bolsheviks declined rapidly; Lenin fled to Finland and other leaders were arrested. ✅ About 400 people had been killed during the violence, and Prince Lvov, deeply shocked by the July Days, resigned; he was replaced by Alexander Kerensky.

✅ **Fact:** it is still not absolutely clear who was responsible for the events of the July Days. ✅ Richard Pipes is convinced that Lenin planned the whole affair from the beginning; ✅ Robert Service argues that Lenin was improvising, "testing the waters" to discover how determined the provisional government was; ✅ the demonstration was probably spontaneous in origin, and Lenin soon decided that it was too early to launch a full-scale uprising.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two things in this episode are worth more than the narrative. The first is the refusal: ✅ the soviet was offered power by a crowd and declined to take the responsibility - which is the strongest available evidence that the soviets in July were not a Bolshevik instrument, since a Bolshevik-controlled soviet would have accepted. ⚠️ The second is the shape of the disagreement between Pipes and Service, which is a disagreement about *intention* rather than about events, and therefore one that cannot be settled by more facts about July. ⚠️ Displaying that structure - "the dispute is about what Lenin intended, and the evidence is compatible with both readings" - is a more sophisticated move than adjudicating it.

### 4. August: Kornilov, and the reversal

✅ **Fact:** the Kornilov affair embarrassed the government and increased the popularity of the Bolsheviks. ✅ General Kornilov, the army commander-in-chief, viewed the Bolsheviks as traitors; he decided it was time to move against the soviet and brought troops towards Petrograd in August. ✅ However, many of his soldiers mutinied and Kerensky ordered Kornilov's arrest. ✅ Army discipline seemed on the verge of collapse; public opinion swung against the war and in favour of the Bolsheviks, who were still the only party to talk openly about making a separate peace.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Kornilov is the hinge of the second half of 1917, and the mechanism is worth stating precisely because it is counter-intuitive. ⚠️ A move *against* the left rehabilitated the left. The government had to accept help in defending the capital from the forces it had been suppressing six weeks earlier, which simultaneously restored the Bolsheviks' legality and destroyed Kerensky's standing with the officer corps. ⚠️ **After August, Kerensky was a prime minister whom the army distrusted and the soviets no longer feared** - and there was no third constituency available to him.

### 5. The arithmetic by October, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** by October the Bolsheviks had won a majority over the Mensheviks and Social Revolutionaries in both the Petrograd and Moscow soviets, though they were in a minority in the country as a whole. ✅ Leon Trotsky, who had become a Bolshevik only in July, was elected Chairman of the Petrograd soviet.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this pair of facts is the entire October debate compressed into two lines, and the strongest answers state them together. ⚠️ A majority in the two decisive cities and a minority in the country is precisely the position from which a seizure of power is feasible and a democratic victory is not - which is why the Constituent Assembly result of November, examined in Lesson 9, surprised nobody in the leadership and changed nothing in their conduct. ⚠️ Note also Trotsky's July membership: the man who planned October had been in the party for three months, which is a useful corrective to accounts that treat the Bolsheviks as a monolith with a twenty-year plan.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** presenting the Bolsheviks as beneficiaries of other people's mistakes underplays their agency. They built a party, published a newspaper, produced a programme and made a decision to seize power; luck is what the losing side calls the winning side's judgement.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right that the account must not slide into accident, and the correct answer gives agency its due in the right place. ⚠️ Agency shows in three recorded things: the April Theses, which is a strategic choice with costs; the refusal to be drawn into a premature rising in July, which Service reads as deliberate caution; and the planning of October, which the account attributes to Trotsky and describes as going off without a hitch. ⚠️ What agency does *not* explain is the supply of opportunities, and those were produced by Kerensky's offensive and Kornilov's march. **The defensible formulation is that the Bolsheviks did not create the crisis of 1917 but were the only organised force with a programme built to exploit it** - which is both more accurate and more interesting than either caricature.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far was Lenin's leadership the main reason for the success of the Bolshevik Revolution in 1917?
> ⚠️ Why did the Bolsheviks gain popularity between April and October 1917?
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Kornilov affair.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the leadership demand, the structure that scores is a three-way allocation: Lenin's contribution (the April Theses and the strategic decision of mid-October), Trotsky's contribution (the planning and the chairmanship of the soviet), and the contributions of their opponents (the June offensive and the Kornilov march). ⚠️ Then state the margin: leadership was necessary but operated on opportunities it did not manufacture. ⚠️ Avoid the trap of the stem, which invites either a Lenin hagiography or a denial; the marks are in the allocation.

### Traps

- Writing that Lenin was smuggled into Russia by the Germans as an agent. ✅ The sealed-train passage is recorded with the German motive; the spy allegation of July is recorded as false.
- Treating the April Theses as a list of demands rather than a strategic position.
- Omitting the June offensive from an account of Bolshevik gains.
- Presenting the July Days as a failed Bolshevik coup. ✅ The authorship is disputed, the demonstration was probably spontaneous in origin, and the soviet itself refused power.
- Forgetting that about 400 people were killed in July and that Lvov resigned as a result.
- Saying the Bolsheviks had majority support in Russia by October. ✅ They had majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets and were a minority in the country.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ April: political amnesty; Lenin returns from Switzerland in a sealed German train, the Germans hoping for further chaos.
2. ✅ April Theses: cease supporting the provisional government; all power to the soviets; withdraw from the war.
3. ✅ June offensive: Kerensky's idea; disastrous; army morale and discipline collapse; hundreds of thousands desert.
4. ✅ 3 July: demonstration of workers, soldiers and sailors at the Tauride Palace; the soviet refuses to take power; about 400 killed; the false German-spy report; Lenin flees to Finland; Lvov resigns and Kerensky takes over.
5. ✅ Pipes: Lenin planned July from the beginning. Service: Lenin was improvising, "testing the waters". The demonstration was probably spontaneous in origin.
6. ✅ August: Kornilov, commander-in-chief, moves troops on Petrograd; his soldiers mutiny; Kerensky orders his arrest.
7. ✅ Result: opinion swings against the war and towards the Bolsheviks, the only party openly proposing a separate peace.
8. ✅ By October: Bolshevik majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets; minority in the country as a whole.
9. ✅ Trotsky joined the Bolsheviks in July and was elected Chairman of the Petrograd soviet.
10. ⚠️ The composite: two gains from opponents' actions, one near-fatal loss of disputed authorship, and a programme designed against the government's constraints.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Kornilov affair helped the Bolsheviks more than the July Days had harmed them.

**Model answer:** Because the two episodes affected different assets. ✅ July cost the Bolsheviks reputation and personnel - popularity declined rapidly, Lenin fled to Finland, other leaders were arrested and the party was publicly accused of attempting an uprising - but it left the underlying structural position untouched, since the government still could not end the war, deliver land or hold elections. ✅ August restored the reputation and destroyed the government's remaining assets in a single episode: the commander-in-chief moved on the capital, his own soldiers mutinied, Kerensky was reduced to ordering the arrest of his own army chief, army discipline seemed on the verge of collapse, and public opinion swung against the war and towards the only party openly proposing a separate peace. ⚠️ The asymmetry is therefore that July damaged a reputation that could be rebuilt in weeks, while August destroyed a relationship between a government and its officer corps that could not be rebuilt at all - and the measurable result is that by October the Bolsheviks held majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the recovery as proof of popular conversion to Bolshevism. ✅ The same account records that they remained a minority in the country as a whole; what changed was their position in the two cities where power was physically located.

## Lesson 8 - October/November 1917: the mechanics of the seizure and the argument about its nature

Progress: 8/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the mid-October decision and who took it; the operation of the night of 25-26 October; the walk-out that created a Bolshevik government; the three recorded interpretations of what October was; and the formulation that survives all three

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - Lowe, ch. 16 §16.2(c) item 8 and §16.2(d), on the seizure itself and the coup-or-insurrection debate
🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson
📰 CA Found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one night, four objectives, and the political consequence of a walk-out

```text
   THE DECISION
   mid-October: THE PETROGRAD SOVIET takes the crucial decision to
   attempt to seize power, urged on by Lenin and strongly supported by
   Joseph Stalin and Yakov Sverdlov, who had assumed the leadership
   while Lenin was absent in Finland.
   BUT THE PLANNING IS TROTSKY'S: "it was Leon Trotsky who made most
   of the plans, which went off without a hitch."
                                |
   THE NIGHT OF 25-26 OCTOBER (JULIAN) = 7-8 NOVEMBER (GREGORIAN)
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   | WHO ACTS: Bolshevik Red Guards and troops loyal to the       |
   | Petrograd Soviet                                             |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   | OBJECTIVE 1: TELEGRAPH OFFICES  -> communications            |
   | OBJECTIVE 2: THE RAILWAY STATION -> movement of troops       |
   | OBJECTIVE 3: SURROUND THE WINTER PALACE -> the government    |
   | OBJECTIVE 4: ARREST THE MINISTERS -> except Kerensky, who    |
   |              managed to escape                               |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   | CHARACTER: "almost a bloodless coup"                         |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
   THE POLITICAL STEP THAT MATTERED AS MUCH AS THE MILITARY ONE
   The Mensheviks and Social Revolutionaries WALKED OUT of the Second
   Congress of Soviets - still believing the revolution should wait
   until industrial workers were more numerous - LEAVING LENIN AND THE
   BOLSHEVIKS to set up a new Soviet government with Lenin in charge:
   the COUNCIL OF PEOPLE'S COMMISSARS (SOVNARKOM).
                                |
   -> A seizure carried out in the name of a multi-party institution
      became a single-party government because the other parties left
      the room. That is the sentence to remember.
```

*Two facts in this diagram do most of the work in an answer: the objectives were infrastructural rather than symbolic, and the one-party outcome was produced by a walk-out rather than by an exclusion.*

### 1. Who decided, and who planned

✅ **Fact:** in mid-October, urged on by Lenin, the Petrograd soviet took the crucial decision to attempt to seize power. ✅ He was strongly supported by Joseph Stalin and Yakov Sverdlov, who had assumed the leadership while Lenin was absent in Finland. ✅ But it was Leon Trotsky who made most of the plans, which went off without a hitch.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the distribution of credit here is examinable and is usually got wrong. ⚠️ Lenin supplied the decision and the pressure to take it; Stalin and Sverdlov supplied the party machinery in his absence; Trotsky supplied the operation. ⚠️ An answer that attributes October to Lenin alone has missed the recorded division of labour, and an answer that attributes it to Trotsky alone has missed that the decision was contested and required Lenin's insistence to be taken at all.

### 2. The operation

✅ **Fact:** during the night of 25-26 October, Bolshevik Red Guards and troops loyal to the Petrograd Soviet took over important buildings, including telegraph offices and the railway station, and surrounded the Winter Palace. ✅ Later the provisional government ministers were arrested, except Kerensky, who managed to escape. ✅ It was almost a bloodless coup, enabling Lenin to announce that the provisional government had been overthrown.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** compare this with February. February took eight days, killed at least 40 people in the first exchange alone, and depended on the behaviour of tens of thousands of people nobody controlled. October took one night, was almost bloodless, and depended on a few hundred people doing exactly what they had been told. ⚠️ **That contrast is the single most efficient answer to a "distinguish February and October" stem, because it is a contrast of method, not merely of outcome.** ⚠️ And it is also why the word "revolution" does different work in the two cases: the first was a collapse that nobody authored; the second was an operation with a planner.

### 3. Why the Bolsheviks were the ones who could do it

✅ **Fact:** the Bolsheviks knew exactly what they were aiming for, and were well disciplined and organised, whereas the other revolutionary groups were in disarray. ✅ The Mensheviks thought the next revolution should not take place until the industrial workers were in a majority in the country; Lenin and Trotsky believed that both revolutions could be combined into one, and so, after years of disagreement, they were able to work well together.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Menshevik position is not timidity; it is orthodoxy, and stating it as such is worth a mark. ⚠️ A party that believes history has a required sequence cannot seize power out of sequence without abandoning its own theory. The Bolsheviks' decisive advantage in October was therefore partly doctrinal: **they had already decided that the sequence could be compressed, which converted a theoretical position into an operational permission.**

### 4. The walk-out, and the birth of one-party government

✅ **Fact:** the Mensheviks and Social Revolutionaries still believed the revolution should have been delayed until the industrial workers were more numerous; they walked out of the Second Congress of Soviets, leaving Lenin and the Bolsheviks to set up a new Soviet government with Lenin in charge, to be called the Council of People's Commissars, or Sovnarkom.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most consequential procedural event in the topic and the most frequently omitted. ⚠️ The October seizure was carried out in the name of the soviets, which were multi-party bodies. Had the other socialist parties remained, the resulting government would have had to be a coalition of the soviet parties, and the whole subsequent history of one-party rule would have had a different starting point. ⚠️ They left, and the body that remained was Bolshevik. **A single-party state was not proclaimed in October; it was constituted by default, and then defended by force in January 1918.** That distinction is the beginning of the answer to the Leninism question in Lesson 21.

### 5. Three readings of October, and the evidence each rests on

| Reading | Core claim, as recorded | What it rests on | What it must explain away |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Official Soviet** | The takeover was the result of a mass movement: workers, peasants and most of the soldiers and sailors were attracted by Bolshevik politics - peace, land for the peasants, worker control, government by the soviets, self-determination for the nationalities; Lenin was a charismatic leader; and in only 16 out of 97 major centres did the Bolsheviks have to use force to assert their authority. It mattered because popular character gave the regime its legitimacy | ⚠️ The 16-of-97 figure is a real datum and is the strongest evidence for the reading | ⚠️ The Constituent Assembly result six weeks later, and the recorded minority status in the country as a whole |
| ✅ **Traditional liberal** | No significant popular support; a minority group of professional revolutionaries used the chaos to take power, succeeding because they were well organised and ruthless. Adam Ulam: "the Bolsheviks did not seize power in this year of revolutions. They picked it up. ... Any group of determined men could have done what the Bolsheviks did in Petrograd in October 1917: seize the few key points of the city and proclaim themselves the government." Richard Pipes: the revolution was due almost entirely to Lenin's overwhelming desire for power | ⚠️ The mechanics of the night, the minority status, and the dispersal of the Assembly | ⚠️ Why no other group of determined men did it, and why the programme of peace and land had the traction it demonstrably had |
| ✅ **Libertarian** | The October revolution was the result of a popular uprising which had very little to do with the Bolsheviks; the masses were responding to their own aspirations, not Bolshevik pressure. Alexander Berkman: "the shop and factory committees were the pioneers in labour control of industry, with the prospect of themselves, in the near future, managing the industries" | ⚠️ The independent radicalism of factory committees and land seizures, which are separately recorded | ⚠️ The centralised seizure in the capital, and the subsequent subordination of those same committees to the state |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three readings disagree about the *unit* they are describing, which is why they never meet. ⚠️ The Soviet reading describes the country; the liberal reading describes the night in Petrograd; the libertarian reading describes the workplaces. All three can be largely right about their own unit. ⚠️ **The advanced owner's formulation is the one to carry into an answer: October was both a disciplined seizure of strategic points and the culmination of a wider collapse of legitimacy around the Provisional Government.** That sentence concedes the liberal reading on method and the Soviet reading on context while refusing both as complete accounts.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the Ulam quotation settles the matter. If any group of determined men could have done it, the Bolsheviks' achievement is trivial and the event is a coup.

⚠️ **Reply:** the quotation is a claim about capability and is stated in hindsight, and the hypothetical it rests on was not tested. ⚠️ Three things were required simultaneously in October 1917: the will to seize power, the organisation to execute it, and a programme that made holding it conceivable. ✅ The record shows the Mensheviks and SRs possessed the second and lacked the first on doctrinal grounds - they walked out rather than participate - and that the Bolsheviks alone had combined all three. ⚠️ A capability that nobody else was willing to use is not a capability that was widely distributed. **The disciplined reply concedes that the seizure was technically easy and denies that this makes it accidental**, and it uses the walk-out as the evidence.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The Bolsheviks did not seize power, they picked it up." Discuss.
> ⚠️ Was the October Revolution a coup or a mass insurrection?
> ⚠️ Distinguish the February and October revolutions of 1917.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first two, refuse the binary in the opening sentence and then earn the refusal with evidence: the mechanics on the coup side, the collapse of the Provisional Government's legitimacy and the 16-of-97 figure on the insurrection side. ⚠️ Close with the compound formulation. ⚠️ For the third, build the answer on a four-axis table - trigger, decisive actors, degree of planning, outcome - which is the structure the basic owner's evidence bank already supplies.

### Traps

- Dating October by the Gregorian calendar without saying so.
- Attributing the planning to Lenin. ✅ It is recorded as Trotsky's.
- Forgetting Stalin and Sverdlov, who had assumed the leadership while Lenin was in Finland - a detail with obvious later significance.
- Describing the Winter Palace as stormed in a great battle. ✅ The record is "almost a bloodless coup".
- Omitting the walk-out of the Mensheviks and SRs, which is what made the new government single-party.
- Presenting the three interpretations as a list of opinions rather than as claims about three different units of analysis.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Mid-October: the Petrograd soviet decides to seize power, urged by Lenin and supported by Stalin and Sverdlov; Trotsky makes most of the plans.
2. ✅ Night of 25-26 October (Julian) = 7-8 November (Gregorian): Red Guards and pro-soviet troops take the telegraph offices and railway station, surround the Winter Palace and arrest the ministers except Kerensky, who escaped - "almost a bloodless coup".
3. ✅ Mensheviks and SRs walk out of the Second Congress of Soviets; Sovnarkom is formed with Lenin as chairman.
4. ✅ Soviet reading: a mass movement; force needed in only 16 of 97 major centres; legitimacy through popular character.
5. ✅ Traditional liberal reading: Ulam's "picked it up"; Pipes on Lenin's desire for power.
6. ✅ Libertarian reading: a popular uprising with little to do with the Bolsheviks; Berkman on the factory committees.
7. ✅ Menshevik doctrine required waiting for an industrial-worker majority; Lenin and Trotsky held that the two revolutions could be combined into one.
8. ⚠️ The formulation to carry: a disciplined seizure of strategic points **and** the culmination of a collapse of legitimacy around the Provisional Government.
9. ⚠️ February against October: eight days versus one night; unplanned versus planned; tens of thousands versus a few hundred; a collapse versus an operation.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the walk-out of the Mensheviks and Social Revolutionaries from the Second Congress of Soviets more important for the later history of the Soviet state than the capture of the Winter Palace?

**Model answer:** Because the capture settled who held the buildings and the walk-out settled who held the institution. ✅ The seizure was carried out in the name of the soviets, which were multi-party elected bodies of workers' and soldiers' representatives; had the Mensheviks and SRs remained in the Congress, the government formed out of it would have had to be a coalition of soviet parties. ✅ They left - still holding that the revolution should have waited until industrial workers were more numerous - and the Congress that remained was Bolshevik, which is how Sovnarkom came to be constituted as a single-party body with Lenin as its chairman. ⚠️ The consequence runs forward through the entire topic: the one-party state was not decreed in October but emerged by default, and the decision to *defend* that default against an adverse election in January 1918 is the point at which default became policy. An answer on the origins of one-party rule that starts at the dispersal of the Constituent Assembly has begun one step too late.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the walk-out as expulsion. The Bolsheviks did not exclude the other socialist parties from the Congress in October; they were left in possession of it, and the exclusion came later and by other means.

## Lesson 9 - The first four months: decrees, an election lost, a police force and a punitive peace

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the first decrees and what each one bought; the Left SR concession; the creation of the Cheka; the Constituent Assembly election result and its dispersal; the justification offered for it; and the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk with its territorial and economic price
