### Visual: four instruments answering four problems, in four months

```text
   THE PROBLEM, STATED BY THE ACCOUNT ITSELF:
   "The Bolsheviks had nothing like majority support in the country as
   a whole. One problem therefore was how to keep themselves in power
   and yet allow free elections."

   +---------------------+   +----------------------------------------+
   | PROBLEM 1           |   | INSTRUMENT 1: THE DECREES              |
   | no support base     |-->| land nationalised WITHOUT COMPENSATION |
   | outside two cities  |   | (crown and church estates included),   |
   |                     |   | for redistribution among the peasants; |
   |                     |   | workers' control over managers;        |
   |                     |   | the eight-hour day; self-determination |
   |                     |   | for every national group; banks, large |
   |                     |   | factories and mines nationalised; all  |
   |                     |   | tsarist and Provisional debts cancelled|
   |                     |   | PLUS: Left SRs admitted as junior      |
   |                     |   | partners - they had far more rural     |
   |                     |   | support than the Bolsheviks            |
   +---------------------+   +----------------------------------------+
   | PROBLEM 2           |   | INSTRUMENT 2: THE SECURITY POLICE      |
   | organised hostility |-->| right claimed to close hostile         |
   |                     |   | newspapers and journals; the Cheka     |
   |                     |   | created under Felix Dzierzynski        |
   +---------------------+   +----------------------------------------+
   | PROBLEM 3           |   | INSTRUMENT 3: DISPERSAL                |
   | an election that    |-->| Constituent Assembly, elected          |
   | could not be won    |   | mid-November: Bolsheviks 175 of about  |
   |                     |   | 700; SRs 370; Left SRs 40; nationality |
   |                     |   | groups 80; Kadets 17; Mensheviks 15.   |
   |                     |   | Met January 1918, was dispersed by     |
   |                     |   | Red Guards and never met again         |
   +---------------------+   +----------------------------------------+
   | PROBLEM 4           |   | INSTRUMENT 4: A PUNITIVE PEACE         |
   | a war that could    |-->| Brest-Litovsk, March 1918: Poland,     |
   | not be continued    |   | Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Ukraine,   |
   |                     |   | Georgia and Finland lost - a third of  |
   |                     |   | the farming land, a third of the       |
   |                     |   | population, two-thirds of the          |
   |                     |   | coalmines and half the heavy industry  |
   +---------------------+   +----------------------------------------+

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   READ THE FOUR ROWS TOGETHER AND THE PATTERN IS VISIBLE:
   deliver fast on the promises that buy time; acquire an instrument
   for the opposition that cannot be bought; refuse the verdict of the
   only national election; and pay any territorial price to stop
   fighting a war that would otherwise finish the government.
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*This is the period in which the character of the regime was set, which is why it does more work in the Leninism-Stalinism question than anything Stalin did.*

### 1. The decrees, and what each one purchased

✅ **Fact:** one of Lenin's first decrees nationalised all land, including former crown estates and land belonging to the church, without compensation, so that it could be redistributed among the peasants and, it was hoped, win their support. ✅ The decree on workers' control gave industrial workers authority over their managers and was intended to reduce unrest and strikes in factories. ✅ Another decree limited the working day in factories to eight hours. ✅ Other decrees granted self-determination to every national group, nationalised banks, large factories and mines, and cancelled all debts incurred by the tsarist and Provisional governments. ✅ One major concession Lenin and Trotsky were prepared to make was to allow some Left Social Revolutionaries to act as junior partners in the government, because they had far more support than the Bolsheviks in rural areas.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two features of this package deserve more than a list. First, the phrase *without compensation* is what distinguishes it from every land reform attempted before it, including Stolypin's, which required peasants to buy. ⚠️ Nationalisation without compensation is simultaneously the most attractive offer available to a land-hungry peasantry and the act that makes any future restoration impossible, because a restored landlord class would have to be compensated by a bankrupt state out of land already occupied. **The land decree did not only win support; it destroyed the alternative.**

⚠️ Second, the Left SR concession shows that the one-party outcome was not a settled plan in November 1917. ⚠️ A government that admits junior partners because they have rural support it lacks is a government still operating on coalition logic. That logic lasted until the Brest-Litovsk crisis, when the Left SRs walked out of Sovnarkom - which means the one-party state was consolidated by a foreign-policy dispute, not by a doctrinal decision.

### 2. The Cheka

✅ **Fact:** the government claimed the right to close down hostile newspapers and journals, and set up a new security police force: the Extraordinary Commission for Combating Sabotage and Counter-Revolution, usually known as the Cheka, under Felix Dzierzynski.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the date matters more than the name, and it is the pivot of the whole violence debate in Lesson 11. ⚠️ Pipes's argument rests precisely on it: a security police created in December 1917, at a point when, on his reading, there was no threat of organised opposition and no foreign intervention, indicates intention rather than reaction. ⚠️ The counter-argument is that a government with no majority and an armed opposition forming within four months was not obviously secure. ⚠️ **The disciplined move in an answer is to record that the timing is the evidence both sides use, and then say what would settle it - which is a question about what the leadership believed in December 1917, and is therefore a question about intention that no further fact about December will resolve.**

### 3. The election, with the numbers

✅ **Fact:** Lenin knew he would have to allow elections, having criticised Kerensky so bitterly for postponing them, but sensed that a Bolshevik majority was highly unlikely. ✅ Kerensky had arranged elections for mid-November and they went ahead as planned. ✅ The result: Bolsheviks 175 seats out of about 700; Social Revolutionaries 370; Mensheviks 15; Left Social Revolutionaries 40; various nationality groups 80; and Kadets - Constitutional Democrats who wanted genuine democracy - 17.

✅ **Fact:** under a genuine democratic system the SRs, with an overall majority, would have formed a government under their leader Viktor Chernov. ✅ Lenin was determined that the Bolsheviks were going to stay in power; there was no way he was going to hand it over to the SRs, or even share it, after the Bolsheviks had done all the hard work of getting rid of the Provisional Government. ✅ After some anti-Bolshevik speeches at the first meeting in January 1918, the Assembly was dispersed by Bolshevik Red Guards and not allowed to meet again. ✅ Lenin's justification was that this was really the highest form of democracy: since the Bolsheviks knew what the workers wanted, they had no need of an elected parliament to tell them; the Assembly must take second place to the Congress of Soviets and Sovnarkom.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things are worth extracting. First, the scale: the largest party had more than twice the Bolshevik total, so this was not a narrow defeat that could be argued away. ⚠️ Second, the identity of the winner: the SRs were the peasant party, which means the election measured exactly the constituency the land decree had been designed to win - and the decree had not worked fast enough, or had worked to the benefit of the party that had wanted land redistribution first. ⚠️ Third, the justification. It is not a claim of emergency; it is a claim about *representation itself* - that a party which knows the interests of a class does not require the class's vote to be authorised to act for it. ⚠️ **That doctrine, once stated, has no internal stopping point, and it is the single most important fact in the entire Lenin-to-Stalin argument.** The basic owner marks it in exactly those terms: this is the fact that answers the "was Stalin a betrayal of Lenin?" question.

### 4. Brest-Litovsk

✅ **Fact:** an armistice between Russia and the Central Powers was agreed in December 1917, followed by long negotiations during which Trotsky tried without success to persuade the Germans to moderate their demands. ✅ The Treaty of Brest-Litovsk of March 1918 was cruel: Russia lost Poland, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, the Ukraine, Georgia and Finland, which included a third of Russia's farming land, a third of her population, two-thirds of her coalmines and half her heavy industry. ✅ All the other parties condemned it, and the Left Socialist Revolutionaries walked out of Sovnarkom. ✅ Lenin insisted it was worth it, pointing out that Russia needed to sacrifice space in order to gain time to recover, and he probably expected Russia to get the land back when, as he hoped, the revolution spread to Germany and other countries.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the treaty is usually taught as a humiliation and is better taught as a calculation with a stated premise and a stated risk. ⚠️ The premise was that revolution would spread to central and western Europe, making the cession temporary. The risk was that it would not - and Lesson 11 records that it did not, leaving the new government isolated and facing a capitalist Europe deeply suspicious of it. ⚠️ **Brest-Litovsk is therefore the first and largest instance of a pattern that runs through the whole topic: a decision justified by an expected international development that did not occur.** The same premise underlies Trotsky's permanent revolution in Lesson 13 and its defeat.

⚠️ One further consequence is often missed and is worth a clause: the territories surrendered included the country's main coal and heavy-industrial base, which means the economic catastrophe of 1918-21 in Lesson 11 begins with a treaty and not only with a civil war.

### 5. Comparison: four ways a new government can handle an adverse election

| Option | What it requires | Taken? |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Accept the result and leave office | A belief that the mandate is binding | ✅ Rejected explicitly: Lenin would not hand power to the SRs "after the Bolsheviks had done all the hard work" |
| ⚠️ Form a coalition with the largest party | A willingness to share programme control | ⚠️ Partially attempted earlier through the Left SR junior partnership, and abandoned at Brest-Litovsk |
| ⚠️ Rerun or annul the election on procedural grounds | A plausible procedural complaint | ⚠️ Not used; the elections went ahead as arranged by the previous government |
| ✅ Disperse the assembly and relocate sovereignty | An alternative theory of legitimacy | ✅ Taken, with the Congress of Soviets and Sovnarkom named as the superior bodies |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** setting out the options shows that the fourth was a choice among available alternatives rather than the only possible response - which is what makes it evidence about the regime's character rather than about its circumstances.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** judging the dispersal by liberal-democratic standards is anachronistic. The Bolsheviks never claimed to be parliamentary democrats; they claimed to represent a class, and they said so openly.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is sound as a warning against smuggling in a standard, and it fails as a defence, for a reason internal to the record. ✅ The account notes that Lenin knew he would have to allow elections *because he had criticised Kerensky so bitterly for postponing them* - that is, the Bolsheviks had themselves invoked the electoral standard when it was useful against their opponents. ⚠️ A party that campaigns on the illegitimacy of a postponed election cannot afterwards claim that elections were never its standard. ⚠️ The correct analytical statement is therefore not that the Bolsheviks violated a norm they had never accepted, but that they used a norm instrumentally and discarded it on contact with an adverse result - which is a more precise and more damaging observation, and one made from inside the evidence rather than from outside it.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ In what ways, and with what success, did Lenin's policies attempt to solve the problems facing Russia at the beginning of 1918?
> ⚠️ How did Lenin and the Bolsheviks consolidate power between 1917 and 1924?
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the dispersal of the Constituent Assembly.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Use the four-problem, four-instrument structure; it converts a list of measures into an argument about a government solving a defined set of problems. ⚠️ Give the Assembly figures exactly - 175 of about 700 against 370 - because precise numbers in a political argument read as mastery. ⚠️ Close on the justification rather than the dispersal: the sentence about not needing an elected parliament is the examinable item, and it is the one that links forward to Lesson 21.

### Traps

- Saying the Bolsheviks lost the election narrowly. ✅ The SRs had more than twice their seats and an overall majority.
- Confusing the Social Revolutionaries with the Left Social Revolutionaries, who took 40 seats and served as junior partners.
- Omitting "without compensation" from the land decree, which is the clause that does the political work.
- Dating the Cheka vaguely. ✅ Its creation in December 1917 is the pivot of the violence debate.
- Treating Brest-Litovsk as merely humiliating rather than as a calculation with a stated premise about European revolution.
- Forgetting that the Left SRs left Sovnarkom over Brest-Litovsk, which is how the government became single-party in practice.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ First decrees: land nationalised without compensation including crown and church estates; workers' control over managers; eight-hour day; self-determination for national groups; banks, large factories and mines nationalised; tsarist and Provisional debts cancelled.
2. ✅ Left SRs admitted as junior partners because they had far more rural support than the Bolsheviks.
3. ✅ The right to close hostile newspapers claimed; the Cheka created under Felix Dzierzynski.
4. ✅ Constituent Assembly, elected mid-November 1917: Bolsheviks 175 of about 700; SRs 370; nationality groups 80; Left SRs 40; Kadets 17; Mensheviks 15.
5. ✅ Viktor Chernov led the SRs and would have formed the government under a genuine democratic system.
6. ✅ January 1918: the Assembly met, made anti-Bolshevik speeches, was dispersed by Red Guards and never met again.
7. ✅ The justification: the highest form of democracy, since the Bolsheviks knew what the workers wanted; the Assembly to take second place to the Congress of Soviets and Sovnarkom.
8. ✅ Armistice December 1917; Treaty of Brest-Litovsk March 1918: Poland, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Ukraine, Georgia and Finland lost.
9. ✅ The price: a third of the farming land, a third of the population, two-thirds of the coalmines, half the heavy industry; the Left SRs walked out of Sovnarkom.
10. ✅ Lenin's defence: sacrifice space to gain time, on the expectation that revolution would spread to Germany and elsewhere.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the dispersal of the Constituent Assembly, rather than the October seizure, is the decisive fact for anyone arguing about the relationship between Leninism and Stalinism.

**Model answer:** Because the seizure can be defended on emergency grounds and the dispersal cannot. ✅ October removed a government that had no electoral mandate of its own, had postponed the promised elections, and had lost the loyalty of the armed force in its capital; a seizure in those circumstances is an act against an unelected administration. ✅ January 1918 removed something different: an assembly elected in a national poll organised by the previous government and conducted as arranged, in which the Bolsheviks took 175 of about 700 seats against 370 for the Social Revolutionaries. ✅ The stated justification was not that the election had been unfair or that an emergency required postponement, but that an elected parliament was unnecessary because the party already knew what the workers wanted, and that the Congress of Soviets and Sovnarkom ranked above it. ⚠️ That is a general doctrine rather than an emergency measure, and it establishes before Stalin appears that the regime's legitimacy claim did not run through elections at all - which is why the basic owner records this, and not any later event, as the fact that answers the betrayal question.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the dispersal makes Stalinism simply Leninism continued. It establishes the framework; Lesson 21 shows what it does not establish, namely the scale, the bureaucratic penetration and the systematic reach of the terror that came later.

---

## Lesson 10 - The civil war, 1918-21: the coalitions, the intervention, and why the Reds won

Progress: 10/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: who the Whites were and what they actually wanted; the slogan that expressed the grievance against Bolshevik local rule; the theatres and the commanders; the foreign contingents and their objective; the recorded death toll and its comparison with the war; and the four recorded reasons for the Red victory

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one war, four fronts, and the grievance that was not about the Tsar

```text
   WHO OPPOSED THE BOLSHEVIKS, AND WHY

   THE WHITES WERE "A MIXED BAG": Social Revolutionaries, Mensheviks,
   ex-tsarist officers, and any other group that did not like what it
   had seen of the Bolsheviks.
   THEIR GENERAL AIM WAS NOT TO RESTORE THE TSAR. It was to set up a
   democratic government on Western lines.

   THE GRIEVANCE, IN THE REGIME'S OWN VOCABULARY
   Bolshevik slogan, 1917 : "ALL POWER TO THE SOVIETS"
        |
        | what people expected: every town would have its own soviet,
        | running the town's affairs and local industry
        |
        | what arrived: officials called COMMISSARS, appointed by the
        | government and supported by Red Guards, who threw SR and
        | Menshevik members out of the soviets, leaving Bolshevik
        | members in control -> dictatorship from the centre instead
        | of local control
        v
   OPPONENTS' SLOGAN: "LONG LIVE THE SOVIETS AND DOWN WITH THE
   COMMISSARS"

   THE THEATRES
   SIBERIA            | Admiral Kolchak, former Black Sea Fleet
                      | commander, sets up a White government
   THE CAUCASUS       | General Denikin with a large White army,
                      | advancing on Moscow
   TRANS-SIBERIAN     | the Czechoslovak Legion, about 40 000 men,
   RAILWAY, near Omsk | seizing long stretches of the railway
   THE NORTH AND EAST | British, American and French contingents from
                      | 1918, aiming to link up with Kolchak's army;
                      | Murmansk in the north; Japanese at Vladivostok
   THE WEST           | Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania take independence

   THE COST, AS RECORDED
   at least 8 MILLION DEATHS - more than four times the number of
   Russian deaths in the First World War (1.7 million) - counting
   combat, White anti-Jewish pogroms, starvation, dysentery, and the
   typhus and typhoid epidemics.
   The economy was in ruins; the rouble was worth ONE PER CENT of its
   October 1917 value.
```

*The second block is the one to carry into an answer. ⚠️ The civil war was not fought between monarchists and revolutionaries; it was fought largely between rival claimants to the revolution, and the loudest opposition slogan demanded the soviets against the party.*

### 1. Who the Whites were, and what follows from it

✅ **Fact:** by April 1918 armed opposition to the Bolsheviks was breaking out in many areas, leading to civil war. ✅ The opposition, known as the Whites, was a mixed bag consisting of Social Revolutionaries, Mensheviks, ex-tsarist officers and any other groups which did not like what they had seen of the Bolsheviks. ✅ There was great discontent in the countryside, where peasants hated the food-procurement policies of the government; even the soldiers and workers who had supported the Bolsheviks in 1917 resented the high-handed way in which the Bolsheviks treated the soviets all over Russia. ✅ Their general aim was not to restore the Tsar, but simply to set up a democratic government on Western lines.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this changes the meaning of the whole conflict and is the highest-value correction available in this lesson. ⚠️ If the Whites had been monarchists, the civil war would have been a contest between the old order and the new, and the Red victory would demonstrate the popularity of the revolution. ⚠️ Because the Whites included the party that had just won the national election, the war was substantially a contest *within* the revolutionary coalition of 1917 about who owned its result. **The Red victory therefore proves that the Bolsheviks were better at winning a civil war; it does not prove that the country preferred their programme.** That distinction is what a good answer states and a mediocre one elides.

### 2. The commissar grievance

✅ **Fact:** one of the Bolshevik slogans had been "ALL POWER TO THE SOVIETS", and people had naturally expected that every town would have its own soviet running the town's affairs and local industry. ✅ Instead, officials known as commissars, appointed by the government and supported by Red Guards, threw Social Revolutionary and Menshevik members out of the soviets, leaving Bolshevik members in control; it soon turned into dictatorship from the centre instead of local control. ✅ The slogan of the government's opponents became "LONG LIVE THE SOVIETS AND DOWN WITH THE COMMISSARS".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the opposition slogan is the most economical evidence in the topic for a claim that is otherwise hard to prove: that the centralisation of 1918 was experienced as a betrayal of 1917 by people who had supported 1917. ⚠️ It also prefigures Kronstadt in 1921 exactly, and an answer that uses the slogan here and the mutiny there has drawn a line through three years with two pieces of evidence.

### 3. The fronts, the commanders and the intervention

✅ **Fact:** in Siberia Admiral Kolchak, former Black Sea Fleet commander, set up a White government; General Denikin was in the Caucasus with a large White army and advanced on Moscow; and the Czechoslovak Legion of about 40 000 men had seized long stretches of the Trans-Siberian Railway in the region of Omsk. ✅ British, American and French contingents arrived from 1918 aiming to link up with Kolchak's White army, with Murmansk among the northern points and Japanese forces at Vladivostok. ✅ Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania took their independence.

⚠️ **A note on the evidence:** this material comes from a badly degraded page of Lowe's chapter, whose map captions are only partly legible here. ⚠️ Accordingly: the commanders, the Legion's approximate strength, the intervening states and the stated objective of linking up with Kolchak are used because they are legible and are corroborated by the canonical owner's account of foreign intervention; **no date of arrival, troop strength, or account of any operation by any intervening force is asserted anywhere in this session**, because this copy does not legibly supply them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the strategically important feature of the White position is visible on any map and is worth one sentence: the Whites were on the periphery and the Bolsheviks held the centre. ✅ The basic owner records exactly this - that the Bolsheviks controlled the central core of Russia - which gave them the railway hub, the industrial base that remained and interior lines, while their opponents were separated from each other by thousands of kilometres and could not co-ordinate.

### 4. The four recorded reasons for the Red victory

| Reason | As recorded | ⚠️ How to use it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Leadership** | Lenin and Trotsky supplied firmer leadership than the divided Whites | The comparison is relational: the claim is not that Red leadership was brilliant but that White leadership was divided |
| ✅ **Geography** | The Bolsheviks controlled the central core of Russia | Interior lines, the railway hub, the population centres and what remained of industry |
| ✅ **White disunity** | The Whites lacked unity of aim | A coalition containing SRs, Mensheviks and ex-tsarist officers could not agree what victory would mean |
| ✅ **The nationalist card** | Foreign intervention against the Bolsheviks helped the Reds portray themselves as defenders of Russia | The most interesting of the four, because it converts a military liability into a political asset |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fourth reason deserves emphasis because it is counter-intuitive and because it recurs. ⚠️ Intervention gave the government a patriotic argument it could not otherwise have made: a party that had signed away a third of the country's population at Brest-Litovsk could now present itself as the national defence against foreign armies. ⚠️ **The same conversion - external hostility into domestic legitimacy - reappears in Stalin's justification for forced industrialisation in Lesson 15 and in the wartime settlement with the churches in Lesson 18.** Identifying a recurring mechanism across a topic is precisely what distinguishes a 15-mark answer from a 10-mark one.

### 5. The cost, stated with the evidence rules applied

✅ **Fact:** counting combat deaths, deaths in the White anti-Jewish pogroms, those who died from starvation and those who perished from dysentery and in the typhus and typhoid epidemics, the total number of deaths was at least 8 million - more than four times the number of Russian deaths in the First World War, which the same account gives as 1.7 million. ✅ The economy was in ruins and the rouble was worth only one per cent of its value in October 1917.

⚠️ **Sensitive-event control applied here:** the figure is reported as the account states it, with its composition specified and its floor character preserved - "at least" - and it is not broken down by cause, region or party, because Lowe does not break it down. ⚠️ **No apportionment of that total between the sides is offered anywhere in this session**, and any answer that offers one is asserting a distribution that the evidence does not contain.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison with 1.7 million is the most useful thing in the paragraph, because it reframes the whole period. ⚠️ The Russian experience of 1914-21 was not a war followed by a revolution; it was a war whose domestic sequel killed more than four times as many people as the war itself. That fact explains the exhaustion, the industrial standstill, the peasant withdrawal into subsistence and the naval mutiny that together produced the New Economic Policy.

### 6. What the war did to the regime that won it

✅ **Fact:** at the end of the war important changes had taken place in the communist regime: economically it became more centralised, as state control was extended over all areas of the economy; politically the regime became militarised and even brutalised.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the bridge to Lesson 11 and should be stated as a question rather than a conclusion, because that is how the literature states it: were those changes forced on the government by the crisis of the civil war, or would they have taken place anyway because of the nature of communism? ⚠️ The answer to that question is the subject of the next lesson, and the honest position is that the evidence is compatible with more than one reading.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** emphasising that the Whites were not monarchists is a technicality. They included ex-tsarist officers, they were supported by foreign powers hostile to the revolution, and their victory would have destroyed the new order whatever their stated aims.

⚠️ **Reply:** the composition point is a technicality; the *aim* point is not, and they should be separated. ⚠️ Nobody denies that ex-tsarist officers fought for the Whites or that a White victory would have ended Bolshevik rule. ✅ What the record denies is that the objective was restoration; it states that the general aim was to set up a democratic government on Western lines. ⚠️ That matters for one specific reason: it means the political alternative defeated in 1921 was not the ancien régime but a parliamentary republic of the sort the Constituent Assembly result had pointed towards - which is the counterfactual Lesson 21 has to weigh. ⚠️ Treating the question as settled by the presence of ex-tsarist officers is treating a coalition's most reactionary component as its programme, which is exactly the error the attribution discipline of this session forbids in the other direction.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Assess the reasons why the Bolsheviks were victorious in the civil war by 1921.
> ⚠️ Why did the Whites fail to destroy the Bolshevik regime?
> ⚠️ Examine the effects of foreign intervention on the Russian civil war.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ Correct the composition of the opposition in the opening two clauses - a mixed bag of Social Revolutionaries, Mensheviks and ex-tsarist officers whose general aim was a democratic government on Western lines - because everything else in the answer depends on it. ⚠️ Then run the four recorded reasons as a ranked structure rather than a list, with control of the central core doing the heaviest work and White disunity of aim explaining why the peripheral positions could not be combined. ⚠️ Score intervention twice: a military liability that became a political asset, since it let a government that had signed Brest-Litovsk present itself as the defender of Russia. ⚠️ Give the cost with its comparator - at least 8 million against 1.7 million Russian deaths in the First World War - and never apportion it. ⚠️ Close on the distinction the stem is really testing: the war measured organisation, not consent, which is why the commissar grievance and the Kronstadt rising of 1921 belong in the final paragraph.

### Traps

- Writing that the Whites wanted to restore the Tsar. ✅ The recorded general aim was a democratic government on Western lines.
- Treating the Whites as a single force. ✅ They were a mixed bag of SRs, Mensheviks, ex-tsarist officers and others, lacking unity of aim.
- Omitting the commissar grievance, which shows opposition coming from inside the revolutionary coalition.
- Apportioning the 8 million deaths between the sides, or dropping the "at least".
- Comparing the civil-war toll to the First World War without giving the 1.7 million figure that makes the comparison meaningful.
- Presenting foreign intervention only as a military threat and missing its political value to the Bolsheviks.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Armed opposition breaking out in many areas by April 1918; the Whites a mixed bag of SRs, Mensheviks, ex-tsarist officers and others.
2. ✅ Their general aim: not to restore the Tsar but to set up a democratic government on Western lines.
3. ✅ The commissar grievance: appointed officials with Red Guards expelling SR and Menshevik soviet members; dictatorship from the centre instead of local control.
4. ✅ Opposition slogan: "Long live the soviets and down with the commissars."
5. ✅ Kolchak in Siberia; Denikin in the Caucasus advancing on Moscow; the Czechoslovak Legion of about 40 000 on the Trans-Siberian Railway near Omsk.
6. ✅ British, American and French contingents from 1918 aiming to link up with Kolchak; Japanese at Vladivostok; Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania independent.
7. ✅ Four reasons for the Red victory: firmer leadership, control of the central core, White disunity of aim, and the patriotic dividend of foreign intervention.
8. ✅ At least 8 million deaths, including pogrom victims, starvation, dysentery, typhus and typhoid - more than four times the 1.7 million Russian deaths of the First World War.
9. ✅ The economy in ruins; the rouble worth one per cent of its October 1917 value.
10. ✅ The regime that emerged was economically more centralised and politically militarised and brutalised - and whether the war caused that is the question of Lesson 11.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it misleading to treat the Red victory in the civil war as evidence that the Bolshevik programme was popular?

**Model answer:** Because the war did not test popularity; it tested the capacity to win a war. ✅ The opposing coalition was not the old order seeking restoration but a mixed bag of Social Revolutionaries, Mensheviks and ex-tsarist officers whose general aim was a democratic government on Western lines, and it included the party that had just taken 370 of about 700 seats in the only national election. ✅ The four recorded reasons for the Red victory are also all capability factors rather than support factors: firmer leadership against divided opponents, control of the central core of Russia, White disunity of aim, and the ability to use foreign intervention to portray themselves as defenders of Russia. ⚠️ Popularity would need to be shown by something the war did not measure, and two items of contrary evidence sit in the same record - the countryside's hatred of food procurement, and the resentment even of 1917 supporters at the replacement of local soviets by appointed commissars. ⚠️ The defensible statement is therefore that the Bolsheviks won because they were the most effective organised force in a fragmented field, and that the Kronstadt mutiny of March 1921 is what popular opinion looked like once the war was over.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring the opposite - that the Reds were unpopular and won only by force. The 16-of-97 figure from October, the appeal of the land decree, and the patriotic effect of intervention are all real, and the honest verdict is that the war measured organisation rather than consent in either direction.

---

## Lesson 11 - War communism and the Red Terror: forced by circumstance or intended from the start?

Progress: 11/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the two isolations that framed every decision after October; the recorded Cheka figures and the composition of the terror; the killing of the imperial family; the four positions on whether Lenin intended violence; the parallel debate about nationalisation; and the discipline for writing about contested intention

### Visual: one question, four answers, and the evidence each one owns

```text
   THE QUESTION: DID LENIN HAVE VIOLENT INTENTIONS FROM THE BEGINNING,
   OR WAS HE PUSHED INTO THESE POLICIES BY THE CIRCUMSTANCES?

   | ISOLATION 1 (internal): the industrial proletariat had not    |

   | most self-interested class - the peasants                     |
   | ISOLATION 2 (external): Lenin expected revolution to follow   |
   | quickly in central and western Europe. None of it happened.   |
   | Russia was left isolated, facing a deeply suspicious          |
   | capitalist Europe                                             |
   | RESULT: law and order breaking down; local soviets ignoring   |
   | the government's decrees                                      |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
        |                                             |
   ANSWER A: FORCED                             ANSWER B: INTENDED
   Soviet and Marxist historians:               Traditional liberals;
   the Bolsheviks had no choice, given          Pipes: terror was a vital
   the uncompromising attitude of their         element, used even
   enemies                                      preventively. THE EVIDENCE
                                                HE CITES: why else create
   ANSWER C: BRUTALISED BY THE WAR              the Cheka in early December
   Tucker: the civil war made                   1917, when there was no
   centralisation, strict discipline            threat of opposition and no
   and mobilisation integral; it gave           foreign intervention?
   the Party a siege mentality it found         PLUS Lenin's 1908 essay on
   hard to break. AND he notes Lenin's          the French revolutionaries'
   "liberal" thinking at the height of          "excessive generosity", and
   the war - the May 1919 pamphlet on           "how can you make a
   backwardness, to be cured by                 revolution without
   education, not force                         executions?"

   ANSWER D: INHERITED FROM THE TSARIST ENVIRONMENT
   Read: the Bolsheviks were products of an extremely authoritarian
   environment; tsarist governments never hesitated to use extreme
   methods, and Stolypin had executed around 4000 opponents only a few
   years earlier. "In the prevailing circumstances, it is hard to see
   why opposition should be tolerated when the Russian tradition was to
   eradicate it as heresy."
   AND Ulam, from the older liberal generation: violence and terror were
   an integral part of communism, and Lenin actually WELCOMED the civil
   war because it gave him an excuse to use more violence.

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   NOTE WHAT THE FOUR ANSWERS SHARE: they all accept the same facts.
   They differ about what those facts show about intention - which is
   why no additional fact about 1918 will settle the argument.
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The last box is the teaching point. ⚠️ A candidate who explains why the disagreement is unresolvable has demonstrated more understanding than one who picks a side confidently.*

### 1. The two isolations

✅ **Fact:** the industrial workers had not become a large enough class to sustain the Bolsheviks, which left them a minority government uncomfortably dependent on the largest but most self-interested class in Russian society, the peasants. ✅ Lenin had expected a successful revolution in Russia to occur as part of a European or even worldwide socialist revolution, and was convinced that revolutions would quickly follow in central and western Europe so that the new Soviet government would be supported by sympathetic neighbouring governments; none of this happened, so Russia was left isolated, facing a capitalist Europe deeply suspicious of the new regime. ✅ Both internally and externally the regime was under pressure from the forces of counter-revolution; law and order seemed to be breaking down and local soviets simply ignored the government's decrees.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two facts are the premises of everything that follows, including Stalin. ⚠️ A revolutionary government whose own theory says it should be governing an industrial majority, and which finds itself governing a peasant majority; and whose survival strategy assumes foreign revolutions that do not arrive. **Both of the regime's founding assumptions failed within eighteen months, and every policy from war communism to the Five-Year Plans can be read as an attempt to manufacture the conditions the theory had assumed.** That reading is the most useful single framework in the topic, and it is available from the record without speculation.

### 2. The Red Terror: what is recorded, and what is not

✅ **Fact:** whatever the intentions of the Bolsheviks, there is no doubt that violence and terror became widespread. ✅ The Red Army was used to enforce the procurement of grain from peasants thought to have surpluses. ✅ During 1918 the Cheka suppressed 245 peasant uprisings and 99 in the first seven months of 1919. ✅ Official Cheka figures show that during the course of these operations over 3000 peasants were killed and 6300 executed; in 1919 there were over 3000 more executions, but the actual death toll was probably much higher. ✅ Social Revolutionaries and other political opponents were rounded up and shot.

✅ **Fact:** one of the most disturbing features of this Red Terror was that many of those arrested and executed were not guilty of any particular offence, but were accused of being "bourgeois" - a term of abuse applied to landowners, priests, businessmen, employers, army officers and professional people, all labelled "enemies of the people" as part of the government's campaign of class war.

⚠️ **Sensitive-event control applied here:** the figures above are the only ones Lowe supplies for this episode, they are *official Cheka* figures, and the account itself records that the actual toll was probably much higher. ⚠️ **They are therefore reported as a floor derived from the perpetrating agency's own records, not as a measurement**, and no total for the Red Terror is stated anywhere in this session.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the category point matters more than the arithmetic. ⚠️ The distinguishing feature of this terror is not its scale but its *criterion*: people were selected by social classification rather than by act. ⚠️ That is a structural innovation, and it recurs with the kulak category in 1929-33 and with the "former people" of the 1936 Constitution. **Once a state accepts that membership of a category is itself an offence, the size of the resulting operation becomes an administrative decision rather than a judicial one** - and that is the mechanism that makes both the dekulakisation of Lesson 14 and the mass operations of Lesson 16 possible.

### 3. The killing of the imperial family

✅ **Fact:** in the summer of 1918 the ex-Tsar Nicholas and his family were being kept under guard in a house in Ekaterinburg in the Ural Mountains. ✅ The civil war was in full swing and the Bolsheviks were afraid that White forces advancing towards Ekaterinburg might rescue the royal family, who would then become a focus for all the anti-Bolshevik forces. ✅ Lenin himself gave the order for them to be killed, and in July 1918 the entire family, together with members of their household, were shot by members of the local Cheka. ✅ Their graves were discovered only after the collapse of the Soviet Empire; in 1992 some of the bones were subjected to DNA analysis, which proved that they were indeed the remains of the Romanovs.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the examinable feature of this episode is the chain of authorisation, because it is unusually well recorded. ⚠️ An order given personally by the head of government, executed by a local branch of the security police, against non-combatants including household staff, on a *preventive* rationale - that the victims might become a rallying point - is precisely the pattern that Pipes generalises into his reading of the regime. ⚠️ Treating it as a lurid detail wastes it; treating it as evidence about how decisions of this kind were taken and justified does not.

### 4. The four positions, and how to handle them

The four positions are set out in the visual and are not restated here. ⚠️ What matters for an answer is the shape of the disagreement:

| Position | The claim | The kind of evidence it would take to refute it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Forced** (Soviet and Marxist) | No choice, given the enemies' uncompromising attitude | Evidence of violence preceding any organised opposition |
| ✅ **Intended** (Pipes) | Terror was a vital element, used preventively, before active opposition existed | Evidence that the December 1917 security decision was a response to a real threat |
| ✅ **Brutalised** (Tucker) | The civil war made centralisation, discipline and mobilisation integral and produced a siege mentality | Evidence that the same practices existed before the war and survived it unchanged |
| ✅ **Inherited** (Read); ✅ **Integral** (Ulam) | Read: the Bolsheviks were products of an authoritarian tsarist environment in which the tradition was to eradicate opposition as heresy. Ulam: violence was integral to communism and Lenin welcomed the civil war as an excuse | Read's claim would need comparative evidence from other post-imperial revolutions; Ulam's would need evidence that Lenin sought accommodation when he had the option |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Tucker's position contains the detail that makes the whole debate interesting, and it is routinely missed. ✅ He points out that already at the height of the civil war there were signs of Lenin's more "liberal" thinking - in May 1919 Lenin wrote a pamphlet explaining that the main obstacle to socialism in Russia was the culture of backwardness left over from centuries of tsarist rule, and that the best way to change this was not by forcible means but by education, which would unfortunately take a long time. ⚠️ That is evidence *against* a simple intentionalist reading, produced at the moment when the intentionalist reading should be strongest. ⚠️ **An answer that cites the May 1919 pamphlet alongside the 1908 essay has displayed the evidence on both sides of the argument from inside the same man's writings, which is the most convincing form the argument can take.**

### 5. The parallel debate about the economy

✅ **Fact:** the same debate runs over the economic features of war communism: were nationalisation and state control of the economy central to communist aims and ideals, or were they forced on the government by the need to harness the economy to the war effort? ✅ Even Soviet historians differ: some believe the Party had a basic plan for nationalising the major industries as soon as possible, hence the nationalisation of banks, railways, shipping and hundreds of large factories by June 1918; others believe Lenin really hoped for a mixed economy in which some capitalist activity would be allowed.

✅ **Fact:** Alec Nove's conclusion is recorded as the sensible one: "Lenin and his colleagues were playing it by ear. ... We must allow for the interaction of Bolshevik ideas with the desperate situation in which they found themselves."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Nove's formulation is the most useful sentence in this lesson for examination purposes, because it names a third possibility that most candidates never consider. ⚠️ The choice is not between doctrine and circumstance; it is that doctrine supplied a *repertoire* of available responses and circumstance selected from it. ⚠️ A government with a nationalising ideology facing a supply crisis nationalises; a government with a different ideology facing the same crisis rations, requisitions or inflates. **Ideology does not determine the action, but it determines which actions are thinkable** - and that formulation can be reused in Lesson 14 on collectivisation and Lesson 15 on the plans.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** presenting four positions and declining to choose is evasion. Examiners reward judgement, and the evidence of the Cheka's creation, the class-war criterion and the Ekaterinburg order points one way.

⚠️ **Reply:** it would be evasion if the answer stopped at the survey, and it should not. ⚠️ The judgement available on this evidence is a *graded* one, and it can be stated with precision: the instruments of coercion were created early, before large-scale organised opposition, which is a point for the intentionalists; the *scale* on which they were used tracks the civil war closely, which is a point for Tucker; the criterion of selection was ideological from the outset, which is a point for Ulam and Read; and the same leader was writing in 1919 that backwardness should be cured by education rather than force, which is a point against any simple intentionalism. ⚠️ **The defensible verdict is that Lenin solved survival problems by institutionalising party monopoly and coercion** - which is the advanced owner's formulation, and which is a judgement, not a refusal to judge. ⚠️ What cannot be defended is the confident claim that he either always intended terror or never did.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Lenin - evil genius?" Discuss.
> ⚠️ To what extent was Bolshevik violence a response to circumstances rather than a matter of principle?
> ⚠️ Assess the nature and consequences of war communism.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The Lenin-verdict demand has a fixed and reliable structure: achievements in survival and state creation; coercion, the Cheka and party monopoly; and a conclusion framed around the Lenin-to-Stalin problem. ⚠️ The technique that raises the mark is to argue both sides from inside the same evidence - the 1908 essay and the 1919 pamphlet, the Cheka's creation date and its pattern of use - rather than by quoting two historians at each other.

### Traps

- Stating a total for the Red Terror. The base supplies official Cheka figures and says the real toll was probably much higher.
- Omitting the "bourgeois" category point, which is the structural innovation.
- Treating war communism as a considered economic programme, or the intention debate as settled. ✅ The literature is divided and Nove's verdict is that they were playing it by ear; and Tucker's May 1919 pamphlet is the strongest recorded evidence against simple intentionalism.
- Making Ekaterinburg a set-piece narrative rather than evidence about authorisation and rationale.
- Attributing the terror to "the Bolsheviks" as a collective without naming the Cheka, the Red Army, Dzierzynski or Lenin where the record names them.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Two isolations: an industrial working class too small to sustain the regime, and the expected European revolutions that never came.
2. ✅ Law and order breaking down; local soviets ignoring government decrees.
3. ✅ The Red Army enforced grain procurement; the Cheka suppressed 245 peasant uprisings in 1918 and 99 in the first seven months of 1919.
4. ✅ Official Cheka figures: over 3000 peasants killed and 6300 executed in those operations; over 3000 more executions in 1919; the actual toll probably much higher.
5. ✅ Selection by category: "bourgeois" applied to landowners, priests, businessmen, employers, army officers and professional people, labelled "enemies of the people".
6. ✅ July 1918: Lenin ordered the killing of the imperial family at Ekaterinburg, executed by the local Cheka; graves found after the Soviet collapse; DNA analysis in 1992.
7. ✅ Four positions: forced (Soviet and Marxist); intended (Pipes, with the December 1917 Cheka, the 1908 essay and the execution remark); brutalised (Tucker, with the May 1919 pamphlet as a qualification); inherited (Read) and integral (Ulam).
8. ✅ The economic parallel: a basic nationalisation plan against a hoped-for mixed economy; banks, railways, shipping and hundreds of large factories nationalised by June 1918.
9. ✅ Nove: "playing it by ear", with Bolshevik ideas interacting with a desperate situation.
10. ⚠️ The graded verdict: Lenin solved survival problems by institutionalising party monopoly and coercion.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the date of the Cheka's creation carries so much argumentative weight, and state what it does and does not establish.

**Model answer:** It carries weight because it is the only piece of evidence in the debate that is both precisely dated and prior to the events that the "forced by circumstance" reading uses as its explanation. ✅ Pipes's argument is exactly that: why else set up a security police in early December 1917, at a time when there was no threat of organised opposition and no foreign intervention - armed opposition broke out only by April 1918 - and he supports the inference with Lenin's 1908 essay condemning the French revolutionaries' "excessive generosity" and his recorded retort that a revolution cannot be made without executions. ⚠️ What the date establishes is that the *instrument* preceded the *emergency*, which is genuinely damaging to the strong version of the "no choice" case. ⚠️ What it does not establish is the scale or the character of what the instrument was later used for, both of which track the civil war closely, nor does it dispose of Tucker's evidence that Lenin was simultaneously arguing in May 1919 that Russian backwardness should be cured by education rather than force. ⚠️ The precise conclusion available is therefore that coercion was an accepted instrument from the start and that its expansion into mass terror was a product of the war - a graded finding that neither side of the debate would regard as a surrender.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "he created a secret police" as proof of everything that followed. Most states of the period had political police; what has to be argued is the criterion of selection and the absence of judicial constraint, not the existence of the institution.

## Lesson 12 - The New Economic Policy, 1921-28: retreat, alternative road, or trap?

Progress: 12/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the collapse that forced the change and the Kronstadt mutiny; the exact contents of the NEP and what remained under state control; the recorded results on both sides; the argument about Lenin's intentions with his own words on both sides; the ban on factions and the purge of 1921; and the political settlement Lenin left behind

### Visual: the line drawn through the economy in 1921

```text
   WHAT FORCED THE CHANGE
   war communism unpopular with the peasants, who saw no point in
   working hard to produce food that was taken away without
   compensation, and simply produced enough for their own needs
        -> severe food shortages, aggravated by droughts in 1920-1
        -> industry almost at a standstill
        -> MARCH 1921: A SERIOUS NAVAL MUTINY AT KRONSTADT, the island
           base just off St Petersburg, suppressed only by prompt
           action from Trotsky, who sent troops across the ice on the
           frozen sea

   THE NEW ECONOMIC POLICY: WHERE THE LINE WAS DRAWN
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | RELEASED TO PRIVATE ACTIVITY  | RETAINED BY THE STATE         |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | peasants keep surplus produce | HEAVY INDUSTRY: coal, iron    |
   | after paying a tax on a       | and steel                     |
   | proportion of the surplus     |                               |
   |                               | POWER                         |
   | private trade reintroduced    |                               |
   |                               | TRANSPORT                     |
   | small industries and trade in |                               |
   | their products restored to    | BANKING                       |
   | private ownership             |                               |
   |                               | AND THE WHOLE POLITICAL       |
   | old managers brought back;    | SYSTEM: one party, no other   |
   | bonuses and piece-rates;      | parties permitted             |
   | foreign investment encouraged |                               |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+

   THE RESULT, BOTH COLUMNS
   UP:   production levels improving, in most commodities not far off
         1913 levels; great progress in electrification; by late 1927
         the ordinary Russian probably better off than at any time
         since 1914; employed industrial workers paid real wages, with
         an eight-hour day, two weeks' paid holiday, sick and
         unemployment pay and healthcare; peasants with a higher
         standard of living than 1913
   DOWN: unemployment higher than before; still frequent food shortages

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   THE POLITICAL COUNTERPART, SAME MONTH: MARCH 1921
   Lenin bans "factionalism" within the Party. Discussion is allowed,
   but once a decision is taken all sections must stick to it; anyone
   persisting in a different view is expelled. During the rest of 1921
   ABOUT ONE-THIRD OF THE PARTY'S MEMBERS WERE PURGED - expelled, with
   Cheka help - and many more resigned, mainly because they opposed NEP.
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The two halves of March 1921 are the lesson. ⚠️ Economic liberalisation and political closure were introduced in the same month by the same leader, and treating either in isolation misdescribes the policy.*

### 1. The crisis of 1920-21

✅ **Fact:** from early 1921 Lenin faced the formidable task of rebuilding an economy shattered by the First World War and then by civil war. ✅ War communism had been unpopular with the peasants, who, seeing no point in working hard to produce food which was taken away from them without compensation, simply produced enough for their own needs; this caused severe food shortages aggravated by droughts in 1920-1, and industry was almost at a standstill. ✅ In March 1921 a serious naval mutiny occurred at Kronstadt, the island naval base just off St Petersburg, suppressed only through prompt action by Trotsky, who sent troops across the ice on the frozen sea.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the peasant response described here is the single most important economic fact in the topic, because it is the constraint that every subsequent policy runs into. ⚠️ A peasantry that can withdraw into subsistence cannot be coerced into surplus production by requisition alone; it can only be induced, or dispossessed. ⚠️ **NEP is the induction answer and collectivisation is the dispossession answer, and they are answers to the same question.** Stating that in one sentence links Lessons 12 and 14 and is worth more than either lesson separately.

⚠️ Kronstadt deserves one further clause, and it concerns signal rather than scale. ✅ The recorded fact that the mutiny convinced Lenin a new approach was needed shows that the leadership read an armed rising at a naval base as a political message rather than as a disorder to be put down and forgotten. ⚠️ It is therefore the endpoint of the trajectory that began with the commissar grievance of 1918: opposition arising inside the revolution's own constituency, and the last such signal the leadership was willing to answer with a change of policy rather than with force alone.

### 2. The contents, exactly

✅ **Fact:** peasants were now allowed to keep surplus produce after payment of a tax representing a certain proportion of the surplus. ✅ This, plus the reintroduction of private trade, revived incentive and food production increased. ✅ Small industries and trade in their products were also restored to private ownership, though heavy industry such as coal, iron and steel, together with power, transport and banking, remained under state control. ✅ Lenin also found that the old managers often had to be brought back, as well as such capitalist incentives as bonuses and piece-rates, and foreign investment was encouraged to help develop and modernise Russian industry.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice which activities were released and which retained, because the line is not arbitrary. ⚠️ Everything released was small-scale, local and labour-intensive; everything retained was large-scale, capital-intensive and strategically decisive. ⚠️ **The state kept exactly what it would need to industrialise later and released exactly what it could not administer in 1921.** Whether that was foresight or necessity is not recoverable from the record, but the shape of the compromise is, and describing it accurately is what separates an answer that has understood NEP from one that has summarised it as "a return to capitalism".

### 3. The argument about what NEP was

✅ **Fact:** some Bolsheviks claimed that the Kronstadt mutiny and peasant unrest had no bearing on the decision, and that they had been on the point of introducing an earlier version of NEP when the civil war prevented it. ✅ Other communist leaders, especially Kamenev and Zinoviev, disapproved of NEP because they thought it encouraged the development of kulaks who would turn out to be the enemies of communism; they saw it as a retreat from true socialism.

✅ **Fact:** the central question - whether Lenin intended NEP as a temporary compromise until recovery was assured, or as a return to something like the correct road to socialism from which the civil war had diverted them - is recorded as difficult to settle. ✅ What is clear is that Lenin defended NEP vigorously: he said they needed the experience of the capitalists to get the economy blooming again; in May 1921 he told the Party that NEP must be pursued "seriously and for a long time - not less than a decade and probably more"; and he argued that instead of introducing socialism in a country dominated by industrial workers they were working in a backward, peasant-dominated society, so that **NEP was not a retreat but an attempt to find an alternative road to socialism in less than ideal circumstances**, requiring a long campaign of educating the peasants in the benefits of agrarian co-operatives so that force would not be necessary. ✅ Roy Medvedev, a dissident Soviet historian, was convinced these were Lenin's genuine intentions, and that if he had lived another twenty years - to the same age as Stalin - the future of the USSR would have been very different.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the "not less than a decade and probably more" formulation is the most examinable phrase in the lesson, because it is datable, quotable and decisive against the weakest version of the retreat reading. ⚠️ A tactical expedient is not announced with a minimum duration of ten years. ⚠️ But it is equally important not to over-read it: a policy can be intended to last a decade and still be a transitional stage, and Lenin's own justification - educating the peasants into co-operatives so that force would not be necessary - names an *end state* that is not NEP. ⚠️ **The disciplined formulation is that NEP was neither a retreat nor a settled destination but a declared long transition, whose end point was co-operative agriculture arrived at without coercion** - which is precisely the element that 1929 removed.

### 4. The results, both columns

✅ **Fact:** NEP was moderately successful: the economy began to recover and production levels were improving, in most commodities not far off the 1913 levels - a considerable achievement given the territorial losses at the end of the First World War and the war with Poland. ✅ Great progress was made with the electrification of industry, one of Lenin's pet schemes. ✅ Towards the end of 1927, when NEP began to be abandoned, the ordinary Russian was probably better off than at any time since 1914; industrial workers who had a job were being paid real wages and had the benefits of NEP's new social legislation - an eight-hour working day, two weeks' holiday with pay, sick and unemployment pay and healthcare - and the peasants were enjoying a higher standard of living than in 1913. ✅ The downside was that unemployment was higher than before and there were still frequent food shortages.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1927 finding is the sharpest weapon available against a teleological account of Soviet economic history, and it should be used in any answer on collectivisation or the plans. ⚠️ **The policy that was abandoned in 1928-29 had, on the record, delivered the highest living standards the country had seen since 1914.** That does not settle whether abandoning it was wrong - the abandonment case rests on food supply to the cities, on strategic vulnerability and on the pace of industrial growth, all of which Lesson 15 sets out - but it means the abandonment has to be *argued for*, and an answer that treats 1929 as an obvious necessity has skipped the argument.

### 5. The political settlement of March 1921

✅ **Fact:** Russia was now the world's first communist state, the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics; power was held by the Communist Party and no other parties were allowed. ✅ The main political problem for Lenin was now disagreement and criticism within the Communist Party, and in March 1921 he banned factionalism: discussion would be allowed, but once a decision had been taken all sections of the Party had to stick to it, and anybody who persisted in holding a view different from the official party line would be expelled. ✅ During the rest of 1921 about one-third of the Party's members were purged with the help of the Cheka, and many more resigned, mainly because they were against NEP. ✅ Lenin also rejected the claim of the trade unions that they should run industry: they had to do as the government told them, and their main function was to increase production.

✅ **Fact:** the governing body in the Party was the Politburo; during the civil war, when quick decisions were required, it had got into the habit of acting as the government and continued to do so when the war was over. ✅ The "dictatorship of the proletariat" was nowhere in evidence, nor was there any prospect of the state withering away; Lenin defended this on the grounds that the working class were exhausted and weak, so the most advanced workers and their leaders - the Communist Party - must rule the country for them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the ban on factions is the most consequential single institutional act of Lenin's career after the dispersal of the Assembly, and it is usually reduced to a footnote. ⚠️ It does two things at once: it makes intra-party opposition an expellable offence, and - because expulsion is administered by the party apparatus - it transfers the power to define orthodoxy to whoever controls that apparatus. ⚠️ **In March 1921 nobody controlled it in particular; from April 1922 one man did.** Lesson 13 is the story of what the ban on factions became in the hands of a General Secretary, and the causal link should be stated explicitly in any answer on Stalin's rise.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** NEP proves that Lenin had abandoned communism, and the later Soviet claim that it was a stage on the road to socialism is retrospective rationalisation.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection reads the economic measures and ignores the political ones taken in the same month, which is where the test lies. ⚠️ A leader abandoning communism does not simultaneously ban factions inside the ruling party, expel a third of its membership, subordinate the trade unions to the government and defend one-party rule on the ground that the working class is too weak to rule for itself. ✅ The basic owner's formulation is the accurate one: NEP restored limited market mechanisms **under communist political control**, and the advanced owner adds that it was tactical, pragmatic and still within that control. ⚠️ The strongest version of the objection is not that Lenin abandoned communism but that he abandoned *forced* transition - and the evidence for that is real, is in Lenin's own words about education and co-operatives, and is exactly what Stalin reversed.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Was the New Economic Policy a retreat from socialism?
> ⚠️ Assess the success of Lenin's economic policies, 1921-24.
> ⚠️ How far did Lenin's political settlement make Stalin's dictatorship possible?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, refuse the binary and use the three-way distinction: retreat, destination, or declared long transition with a named end state. ⚠️ Quote the "not less than a decade and probably more" formulation once. ⚠️ For the third, run the causal chain from the ban on factions through the apparatus to the General Secretaryship; this is the point at which Lessons 12 and 13 fuse, and the fusion is what the question is testing.

### Traps

- Writing that NEP restored capitalism. ✅ Heavy industry, power, transport and banking remained state-controlled, and the political monopoly was unchanged.
- Treating Kronstadt as an ordinary disorder. ✅ It convinced Lenin that a new approach was needed.
- Omitting the tax-in-kind mechanism and describing NEP only as "private trade allowed".
- Forgetting the one-third purge of party membership in 1921, which is what the ban on factions produced immediately.
- Missing that most of those who resigned did so because they *opposed* NEP, which shows the policy was contested from the left.
- Asserting that Lenin intended NEP to be temporary, or permanent, as a settled fact.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ War communism made peasants produce only for their own needs; severe shortages aggravated by droughts in 1920-1; industry almost at a standstill.
2. ✅ March 1921: naval mutiny at Kronstadt, suppressed by Trotsky sending troops across the ice; it convinced Lenin a new approach was needed.
3. ✅ NEP: peasants keep surplus after a proportional tax; private trade reintroduced; small industry and trade privatised; old managers, bonuses and piece-rates returned; foreign investment encouraged - while coal, iron and steel, power, transport and banking stayed under state control.
4. ✅ Kamenev and Zinoviev opposed NEP as encouraging kulaks and retreating from true socialism.
5. ✅ Lenin, May 1921: NEP to be pursued "seriously and for a long time - not less than a decade and probably more"; not a retreat but an alternative road to socialism in less than ideal circumstances, with peasants educated into co-operatives so that force would not be necessary.
6. ✅ Medvedev: these were Lenin's genuine intentions, and twenty more years would have produced a very different USSR.
7. ✅ Results: production near 1913 levels in most commodities; electrification; by late 1927 the ordinary Russian probably better off than at any time since 1914; eight-hour day, two weeks' paid holiday, sick and unemployment pay, healthcare; higher peasant living standards than 1913; but higher unemployment and frequent food shortages.
8. ✅ March 1921: factionalism banned; about a third of party members purged during the rest of the year with Cheka help; trade unions subordinated; the Politburo acting as the government.
9. ⚠️ The link to carry forward: an expulsion power administered by the apparatus becomes decisive once one person controls the apparatus.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it a mistake to describe the New Economic Policy simply as "a retreat", and what is the more precise description the evidence supports?

**Model answer:** It is a mistake because "retreat" describes a direction without naming a destination, and Lenin's own defence named one. ✅ He told the Party in May 1921 that NEP must be pursued seriously and for a long time, not less than a decade and probably more; he argued that because they were working in a backward, peasant-dominated society rather than one dominated by industrial workers, NEP was not a retreat but an attempt to find an alternative road to socialism in less than ideal circumstances; and he specified the route - a long campaign of educating the peasants in the benefits of agrarian co-operatives so that force would not be necessary. ⚠️ The more precise description is therefore a *declared long transition with a named and non-coercive end state*, adopted under duress after Kronstadt and the food crisis, and paired in the same month with political closure - the ban on factions, the purge of about a third of the membership, and the subordination of the trade unions. ⚠️ That description also explains why the policy is so contested: its economic content pointed one way, its political content the other, and the two were never reconciled before Lenin's strokes removed him from government.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the results settle the intentions. ✅ NEP delivered the best living standards since 1914 by 1927 and was abandoned anyway, which shows that the decision of 1928-29 turned on criteria - grain deliveries to the cities, the pace of heavy industry, and strategic fear - that consumption figures do not capture.

## Lesson 13 - 1922-29: the machine, the doctrines, and four oppositions defeated one at a time

Progress: 13/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Lenin's incapacity and the succession vacuum; the candidates and how they were seen; Lenin's recorded judgement on Stalin; the four mechanisms of Stalin's rise; socialism in one country against permanent revolution; the sequence of defeats; and the argument about whether the 1928 turn was cynical or genuine
