━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the terror as a machine, with the archive gap marked

```text
   THE POLITICAL SITUATION IT GREW OUT OF
   By the summer of 1930 the government's popularity with the general
   public had fallen sharply because of collectivisation and the
   hardships of the First Five Year Plan. There was growing opposition
   to Stalin in the Party; a document known as the RYUTIN PLATFORM -
   after one of the Moscow party leaders - was circulated.
   [⚠️ EVIDENCE GAP: the pages of Lowe's chapter that carry the
    narrative from the Ryutin episode through the assassination of
    1934, the show trials and the army purge are blank or illegible in
    the copy available here. The connecting chronology below is
    supplied as standard verified fact and is marked as such; the
    interpretation and the figures that follow are from legible pages.]

   THE CHRONOLOGY, AS STANDARD VERIFIED FACT
   1 Dec 1934  | Sergei Kirov, the Leningrad party leader, assassinated
               | in Leningrad - the event conventionally treated as the
               | occasion for the escalation
   19-24 Aug   | FIRST MOSCOW SHOW TRIAL: Zinoviev, Kamenev and others
   1936        |
   23-30 Jan   | SECOND MOSCOW SHOW TRIAL: Pyatakov, Radek and others
   1937        |
   11 Jun 1937 | Marshal Tukhachevsky and seven other senior commanders
               | tried and executed - the decapitation of the army
   30 Jul 1937 | NKVD Order No. 00447 authorises mass operations with
               | numerical quotas by region
   2-13 Mar    | THIRD MOSCOW SHOW TRIAL: Bukharin, Rykov, Yagoda and
   1938        | others
   25 Nov 1938 | Yezhov removed as head of the NKVD and replaced by
               | Beria - the terror is wound down

   THE MACHINERY, AS THE SOURCE RECORDS IT
   IT WAS STALIN who gave the orders to Nikolai YEZHOV, head of the
   NKVD as the secret police were now called, ABOUT THE SCALE of the
   repressions, and it was STALIN WHO PERSONALLY APPROVED LONG LISTS
   OF PEOPLE TO BE EXECUTED.
   After announcing the end of the Terror, STALIN MADE YEZHOV THE
   SCAPEGOAT, accusing him and his subordinates of going too far;
   Yezhov was a "scoundrel" guilty of great excesses, and he and most
   of his staff were arrested and shot. In this way Stalin diverted
   responsibility away from himself and kept some of his popularity.
   ALSO RECORDED: Stalin always made sure that other members of the
   Politburo signed death warrants as well as himself; huge numbers of
   people from the top down to interrogators, torturers, guards and
   executioners were willing to carry out the orders; and LOCAL PARTY
   BOSSES - "little Stalins" - often initiated their own terrors from
   below.
```

*The last three lines of the machinery box are the ones that make the historiographical argument in section 6 intelligible: a system with co-signed warrants, willing executants at every level and locally initiated terror is not a simple chain of command from one desk.*

### 1. The political background

✅ **Fact:** during the 1930s Stalin and his closest allies gradually tightened their grip on the Party, the government and the local party organisations, until by 1938 all criticism and disagreement had been driven underground. ✅ Although his personal dictatorship was complete, Stalin did not feel secure; he became increasingly suspicious, trusted nobody and seemed to see plots everywhere. ✅ By the summer of 1930 the government's popularity with the general public had fallen sharply because of collectivisation and the hardships of the First Five Year Plan; there was growing opposition to Stalin in the Party, and a document known as the Ryutin Platform, after one of the Moscow party leaders, was circulated.

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** ⚠️ The pages of Lowe's chapter that carry the narrative from the Ryutin episode through to the revisionist historiography are blank or illegible in the copy available here. ⚠️ Accordingly the connecting chronology in the visual is supplied as standard verified fact and is flagged in the coverage matrix; ⚠️ **no detail of the contents of the Ryutin Platform, of the investigation into the 1934 assassination, of the conduct or charges of any individual trial, or of any confession is asserted anywhere in this session**, because neither Lowe's legible pages nor the canonical owners supply them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal link that *is* fully supported by the legible record is the one between the social upheaval of 1929-33 and the political terror that followed. ⚠️ Collectivisation and the First Plan cost the leadership its public standing and generated opposition inside the party; the terror removed the people who could have acted on that opposition. **The Purges are therefore not a separate phenomenon from the economic transformation; they are its political sequel**, and that connection is the strongest structural claim available in this lesson.

### 2. What the Purges achieved, from Stalin's point of view

✅ **Fact:** the Purges were successful in eliminating possible alternative leaders and in terrorising the masses into obedience. ✅ The central and local government, the government in the republics, the army and navy and the economic structures of the country had all been violently subdued. ✅ Stalin ruled unchallenged with the help of his supporting clique - Molotov, Kaganovich, Mikoyan, Zhdanov, Voroshilov, Bulganin, Beria, Malenkov and Khrushchev - until his death in 1953.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the list of names is worth reproducing because of what happens to it later. ⚠️ Those nine men formed the leadership that survived; several of them appear again in Lesson 20 on the list of people Stalin was reportedly preparing to move against in 1952-53, and several of them governed the USSR after his death. ⚠️ **A terror that consumes the leadership of a revolution and then stabilises around a fixed group for fifteen years is a different thing from a permanent revolution devouring its children** - and the stability of that group after 1938 is a fact that any account of the Purges has to explain.

### 3. The four recorded consequences

| Consequence | As recorded | ⚠️ Analytical weight |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Human cost** | Historians are still arguing about how many people fell victim; but whichever statistics are accepted, the cost in human lives and suffering is almost beyond belief | The figures are in section 4 and are handled under the sensitive-event rules |
| ✅ **The old party destroyed** | Lenin's old Bolshevik Party was the main victim; the power of the Bolshevik elite had been broken and eliminated | ⚠️ This is the strongest single argument against the simple "Stalinism = Leninism continued" thesis: the continuity claim has to account for the physical destruction of the people who made the revolution |
| ✅ **Expertise destroyed** | Many of the best brains in government and industry had disappeared; in a country where the numbers of highly educated people were still relatively small, this was bound to hinder progress | ⚠️ Sets directly against the achievement case of Lesson 15: the same regime expanded technical education and then killed the technically educated |
| ✅ **The army decapitated** | The purge of the army disrupted the USSR's defence policies at a time of great international tension and contributed to the disasters of 1941-2 | ⚠️ The qualification that must accompany Nove's 1942 counterfactual |

### 4. The figures, under the sensitive-event rules

✅ **Fact:** the estimates held in this base are these, and they are reported with their estimators attached and their objects specified.

| Source | What is counted | Figure as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Robert Conquest | Arrests, executions and camp deaths, **for the years 1937-8 only** | About 7 million arrests; about 1 million executions; about 2 million deaths in the labour camps; and of those in the camps, no more than 10 per cent survived |
| ✅ Official KGB figures released in the early 1990s | Executions and camp population, **same period** | 700 000 executions; and at the end of the 1930s, 3.6 million people in labour camps and prisons |
| ✅ Ronald Suny | Famine deaths **plus** those executed or exiled during the 1930s | Adding the 4 million to 5 million who perished in the famine of 1932-3 to the totals for the decade, "the total number of lives destroyed runs from ten to eleven million" |
| ✅ Alexander Yakovlev | **Victims of communism after 1917**, across the whole period | Between 60 million and 70 million |

⚠️ **Controls applied, stated once.**
1. ⚠️ These four rows are **not four estimates of the same thing**. Conquest and the KGB figures cover 1937-8; Suny covers the decade and includes famine deaths; Yakovlev covers thirty-five years and a wider definition of victimhood. ⚠️ **They must never be averaged, reconciled or used interchangeably**, and an answer that produces a single number from them has manufactured a figure no historian holds.
2. ⚠️ The largest divergence - about a million executions against 700 000 - is between an estimate and the KGB figures released for the same span, and this session displays it rather than resolving it. ⚠️ The account itself records that many vital records have been destroyed, probably deliberately, which is why the divergence is not closable from what survives.
3. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed as the record attributes it: to Stalin, who gave the orders about scale and personally approved execution lists; to Yezhov and the NKVD; to the Politburo members who co-signed; to the interrogators, guards and executioners recorded as willing; and to the local party bosses who initiated terrors from below.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the most useful thing a candidate can do with this table is not to use any figure in it, but to describe its structure. ⚠️ **A range that runs from 700 000 to 70 million is not an imprecise measurement of one quantity; it is four measurements of four quantities, and saying so demonstrates exactly the source-criticism that a bare number cannot.** One sentence - "estimates vary enormously because they count different things over different periods, from Conquest's figures for 1937-8 to Yakovlev's for the whole post-1917 era" - is worth more than any total.

### 5. Everyday effects, and the evidence for reach

✅ **Fact:** Robert Service records in *Comrades* that "the USSR was a listening state with an insatiable curiosity, in which maids, porters and drivers were routinely employed to file reports". ✅ It seems clear that many people, perhaps even a majority of the population, lived a kind of double existence: at work and in public they were careful to mouth all the correct opinions and on no account to make the slightest criticism of the regime, and only at home with family or among the most trustworthy friends would anybody express their private thoughts.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the double-existence finding is what makes every source about popular opinion in this period difficult, and saying so is a mark of a careful answer. ⚠️ If public expression was uniformly compliant and private expression was confined to the household, then neither the enthusiasm recorded by McCauley nor the resentment recorded elsewhere can be measured directly from public evidence. ⚠️ **The Purges did not merely repress opinion; they corrupted the record of opinion**, which is a substantive obstacle for the revisionist argument in Lesson 19 and should be noted there.

### 6. The three-way argument about whether this was a plan

| Position | As recorded | ⚠️ What it explains well, and badly |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Traditional, and the archival support for it** | Dmitri Volkogonov concluded from the Soviet archives that Stalin simply had an evil mind and lacked any moral sense; it was Stalin who gave Yezhov the orders about scale and who personally approved long lists for execution, and who then made Yezhov the scapegoat | ⚠️ Explains the direction and the scale, and the recorded scapegoating. Explains less well the willingness of thousands of executants and the locally initiated terrors |
| ✅ **Getty** | The Purges were a form of political infighting at the top; he plays down Stalin's role and claims it was the obsessive fears of all the leaders which generated the Terror. Getty maintains Stalin had no overall plan for terror: "Their fears of losing control, even of losing power, led them into a series of steps to protect their position: building a unifying cult around Stalin." For Getty, Stalin was not the master criminal but one among the elite taking the measures necessary to stay in power | ⚠️ Explains the co-signed warrants, the "little Stalins" and the chaotic administrative pattern. Explains badly the recorded personal approval of execution lists |
| ✅ **Fitzpatrick** | The Purges must be seen in the context of continuing revolution; the circumstances were abnormal - all revolutions are faced by constant conspiracies designed to destroy them, so abnormal responses can be expected | ⚠️ Explains the leadership's stated beliefs and the atmosphere. Explains badly the specific targeting of the party's own founders |

✅ **Fact:** the account also records the contemporaneous disagreement about Stalin's psychology: Khrushchev seemed to think he was paranoid and psychologically unbalanced, claiming Stalin was a "very distrustful man, sickly suspicious"; while Roy Medvedev believes Stalin was perfectly sane but coolly ruthless, one of the greatest criminals in human history, whose main motives were inordinate vanity and lust for power.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Khrushchev-Medvedev disagreement is more useful than it looks, because it bears on responsibility. ⚠️ A paranoia explanation is partly exculpatory in the way that a medical explanation always is; a vanity-and-power explanation is not. ⚠️ **Noting that the choice between them is a choice about culpability rather than about psychology is the kind of observation that lifts an answer**, and it can be made in one clause without adjudicating either.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** Getty's position should be rejected outright. An archive that records a head of government personally approving execution lists settles the question, and treating his reading as a live option is false balance.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection would hold if Getty were denying Stalin's participation, and he is not; he is making a claim about *sufficiency*. ⚠️ The legible record contains three facts that a purely top-down account has to accommodate: Politburo members co-signed the warrants; huge numbers of people at every level down to guards and executioners were willing to carry out the orders; and local party bosses often initiated their own terrors from below. ⚠️ None of those is explained by one man's intentions, and a terror of the recorded scale is not administratively possible without them. ⚠️ **The defensible synthesis is that direction came from the top and was personally exercised, and that the volume was produced by an apparatus and a wider participation that the direction did not have to specify** - which concedes Volkogonov's evidence on authorship and Getty's on mechanism without adopting either as a complete account. ⚠️ The account itself notes that the totalitarian writers accused Getty and his colleagues of trying to whitewash Stalin, and that they in turn accused the totalitarians of Cold War prejudice; reporting that exchange and then supplying the synthesis is better than joining one side of it.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Stalin's power during the 1930s was based almost entirely on terror." How far would you agree?
> ⚠️ Examine the causes and consequences of the Great Purges.
> ⚠️ Why did the Soviet leadership turn on its own party after 1934?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ On the first framing, "almost entirely" is the target and the answer must concede terror's reality and centrality before limiting it; this lesson supplies the terror half, and Lessons 17, 18 and 19 supply the beneficiary stratum, the provision, the propaganda apparatus and the plebiscitary device that make up the rest. ⚠️ On the second, open with the causal link the record supports - the collapse of popularity after collectivisation and the First Plan, the growing party opposition and the Ryutin Platform - so that the Purges appear as the political sequel to the economic transformation rather than as an outbreak of paranoia. ⚠️ Handle the figures by naming the estimator, the object and the span in a single sentence, and never average them. ⚠️ Reserve two sentences for the machinery: the orders about scale, the personally approved lists, the co-signed warrants, the willing executants and the locally initiated terrors, because that is what makes the Volkogonov and Getty readings testable rather than rhetorical. ⚠️ End on consequences that bear on other questions - the destruction of the old party, the loss of expertise, and the damage to the army command.

### Traps

- Stating a single total for the Purges, or averaging the estimates.
- Presenting Conquest and the KGB figures as a disagreement about the same quantity without saying that they are and are not - same period, different methods - while Suny and Yakovlev count different things entirely.
- Asserting details of the show trials, the 1934 investigation or the Ryutin Platform's contents, none of which is legible in the text used here.
- Treating Getty as a defender of Stalin. ✅ His claim is about the absence of an overall plan and the role of elite anxiety.
- Forgetting the scapegoating of Yezhov, which is the clearest recorded evidence about how responsibility was managed politically.
- Omitting the army purge from an assessment of Soviet war-readiness.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ By 1938 all criticism and disagreement driven underground; Stalin's personal dictatorship complete but insecure.
2. ✅ Summer 1930: popularity fallen sharply because of collectivisation and First Plan hardships; growing party opposition; the Ryutin Platform circulated.
3. ✅ Standard chronology: Kirov assassinated 1 December 1934; show trials in August 1936, January 1937 and March 1938; Tukhachevsky and seven other commanders executed 11 June 1937; NKVD Order No. 00447 of 30 July 1937; Yezhov replaced by Beria on 25 November 1938.
4. ✅ Stalin gave Yezhov the orders about scale and personally approved long execution lists, then made Yezhov the scapegoat; Yezhov and most of his staff were arrested and shot.
5. ✅ Politburo members co-signed death warrants; executants at every level were willing; local party bosses initiated terrors from below.
6. ✅ Achievements for Stalin: alternative leaders eliminated, the masses terrorised into obedience, government, republics, armed forces and economy subdued; rule with Molotov, Kaganovich, Mikoyan, Zhdanov, Voroshilov, Bulganin, Beria, Malenkov and Khrushchev until 1953.
7. ✅ Four consequences: the human cost; the destruction of Lenin's old Bolshevik Party; the loss of many of the best brains in government and industry; the disruption of defence policy contributing to the disasters of 1941-2.
8. ✅ Four estimates, four objects: Conquest for 1937-8; the official KGB figures released in the early 1990s for the same span; Suny's ten to eleven million destroyed lives for the decade including famine; Yakovlev's sixty to seventy million for the whole post-1917 period.
9. ✅ Service: a listening state in which maids, porters and drivers filed reports; a population living a double existence.
10. ✅ Three readings: Volkogonov's archival traditionalism; Getty's elite-anxiety revisionism with no overall plan; Fitzpatrick's continuing-revolution context - plus the Khrushchev-Medvedev disagreement about paranoia against vanity and lust for power.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script writes: "The Purges killed 20 million people." State what is wrong with the sentence and write the version that would earn marks.

**Model answer:** Three things are wrong. ✅ The figure appears in none of the four estimates the evidence holds: Conquest gives about a million executions and about two million camp deaths for 1937-8; the official KGB figures released in the early 1990s give 700 000 executions for that span with 3.6 million in camps and prisons at the end of the decade; Suny reaches ten to eleven million *destroyed lives* for the decade only by adding four to five million famine deaths; and Yakovlev's sixty to seventy million is for victims of communism across the whole period after 1917. ⚠️ Second, "the Purges" is left undefined, so the sentence does not say whether it means the two years of mass operations, the decade, or the whole Stalin era, and the answer differs by an order of magnitude in each case. ⚠️ Third, "killed" collapses executions, camp deaths, deportation deaths and famine deaths into one category, which is precisely the conflation that produces impossible totals. ⚠️ The version that earns marks is: *estimates of the human cost vary enormously because they count different categories over different periods - from about a million executions in 1937-8 on Conquest's figures, and 700 000 on the KGB figures for the same years, to Suny's ten to eleven million destroyed lives across the decade once the famine is included - and the record is further limited by the deliberate destruction of many vital documents.*

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that refusing to give a total is evasive. Naming the estimators, the periods and the categories is a stronger demonstration of command than any number, and it is the only form in which the claim is defensible.

---

## Lesson 17 - Nationality, federalism, women, the family and social mobility

Progress: 17/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the non-Russian inheritance and what happened to it between 1917 and 1925; the two-handed nationality bargain and where it stopped; the purges of national communist parties; women in the industrialisation drive; the reversal of family policy in the mid-1930s; the Wives' Movement; and the everyday conditions that the social record has to include

🔍 CA Search: "official government statement November 2025 Holodomor Remembrance Day 1932-1933 famine commemoration" - the one topic-level current-affairs query run for this session; no separate query was run for this lesson
📰 CA Found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the two halves of the nationality bargain, and the line neither half crossed

```text
   THE INHERITANCE, 1914
   The tsarist empire included Poland, Finland, Ukraine, Belorussia,
   Georgia, Armenia, Azerbaijan, Kazakhstan, Kirghizia, Uzbekistan,
   Turkmenistan, Tajikistan, and the three Baltic states of Estonia,
   Latvia and Lithuania.

   WHAT HAPPENED, 1917-25
   Nov 1917 | Lenin gives FINLAND independence
   Mar 1918 | Poland and the three Baltic republics given independence
            | BY THE TREATY OF BREST-LITOVSK
   Mar 1918 | Ukraine, Georgia, Armenia and Azerbaijan declare
            | themselves independent and soon show themselves
            | ANTI-BOLSHEVIK
   Civil war| Stalin, appointed commissar for nationalities by Lenin,
            | decides these hostile states surrounding Russia are too
            | much of a threat; DURING THE CIVIL WAR THEY WERE ALL
            | FORCED TO BECOME PART OF RUSSIA AGAIN
   By 1925  | SIX SOVIET REPUBLICS: Russia itself; Transcaucasia
            | (Georgia, Armenia and Azerbaijan); Ukraine; Belorussia;
            | Uzbekistan; Turkmenistan

   THE PROBLEM, STATED AS A NUMBER
   47 PER CENT OF THE POPULATION OF THE USSR WERE NON-RUSSIAN, and it
   would be difficult to hold them together if they were bitterly
   resentful of rule from Moscow.

   THE TWO-HANDED APPROACH, WHICH WORKED UNTIL 1985
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | HAND ONE: CONCESSION          | HAND TWO: THE LIMIT           |
   | national cultures and         | Moscow had the final say in   |
   | languages ENCOURAGED;         | ALL IMPORTANT DECISIONS.      |
   | the republics had a certain   | If necessary, FORCE would be  |
   | amount of independence -      | used to preserve control by   |
   | MUCH MORE LIBERAL than the    | Moscow.                       |
   | tsarist regime, which had     |                               |
   | tried to "Russianize" the     | 1932: the Ukrainian Communist |
   | empire                        | Party steps out of line by    |
   |                               | admitting collectivisation    |
   |                               | had failed -> a ruthless      |
   |                               | purge of what Stalin called   |
   |                               | "bourgeois nationalist        |
   |                               | deviationists"; similar       |
   |                               | campaigns in Belorussia,      |
   |                               | Transcaucasia and Central     |
   |                               | Asia                          |
   |                               | 1951: Georgian communist      |
   |                               | leaders try to take Georgia   |
   |                               | out of the USSR -> removed    |
   |                               | and shot                      |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+

   THE RULE THE BARGAIN ENCODES:
   CULTURAL AUTONOMY WAS PERMITTED; POLITICAL AUTONOMY WAS NOT.
   And the 1932 case shows where the line actually ran - a statement
   about agricultural policy was treated as nationalist deviation.
```

*This lesson exists because "effect on society" is the half of the syllabus clause that most candidates leave out, and because nationality, gender and mobility are where the Soviet record is most genuinely two-sided.*

### 1. The nationality settlement

✅ **Fact:** the inheritance, the sequence of 1917-25, the 47 per cent figure and the two-handed approach are as set out in the visual. ✅ The approach worked successfully until Gorbachev came to power in 1985. ✅ When the Ukrainian Communist Party stepped out of line in 1932 by admitting that collectivisation had been a failure, Moscow carried out a ruthless purge of what Stalin called "bourgeois nationalist deviationists", and similar campaigns followed in Belorussia, Transcaucasia and Central Asia. ✅ Later, in 1951, when the Georgian communist leaders tried to take Georgia out of the USSR, Stalin had them removed and shot.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three observations do most of the analytical work here.

⚠️ First, the *comparison* is the concession's whole content and it should be stated as the record states it: the policy was much more liberal than the tsarist regime's attempt at Russification. ⚠️ That is a real difference and an answer that denies it is not reading the evidence. But "more liberal than an assimilationist empire" is a low bar, and the second hand of the bargain shows where the bar was set.

⚠️ Second, the 1932 Ukrainian case is the most revealing single episode, because of what was punished. ⚠️ The offence was an admission that an agricultural policy had failed - a statement of economic fact, made in the year of the famine - and it was classified as nationalist deviation. **A federal system in which a republic's leadership cannot report a policy failure without being charged with nationalism is one in which the federalism is administrative rather than political**, and that sentence is the analytical yield of the episode.

⚠️ Third, and most important for the decree of Lesson 9: self-determination was granted to every national group by decree in 1917, and the record then shows Finland released, the Baltic states and Poland released by a treaty signed under duress, and four declared republics forced back during the civil war. ⚠️ **The promise was real, the release was real where the state could not prevent it, and the reconquest was real where it could.** An answer that reports only the decree has reported a policy; one that reports the sequence has reported what happened.

### 2. Women, work and the industrialisation drive

✅ **Fact:** the 1930s were a difficult time for many families because of the disappearance of so many men during collectivisation, the famine and the Purges; there was a high desertion and divorce rate, and millions of women were left as the sole breadwinner in the family. ✅ During the rapid industrialisation of the 1930s more than 10 million women became wage earners for the first time; the percentage of women at work rose from 24 per cent to 39 per cent of the total paid workforce. ✅ By 1940 about two-thirds of the workforce in light industry were women, and many were engaged in heavier jobs such as construction, lumbering and machine-building, which were traditionally thought of as men's work.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal ordering here is uncomfortable and must be stated accurately rather than smoothed. ⚠️ The record puts the entry of millions of women into paid work in the same paragraph as the disappearance of men through collectivisation, famine and the Purges, and alongside a labour demand that the plans generated. ⚠️ **Female employment expanded because the state needed labour and because households had lost their male earners** - which is a different claim from the emancipation narrative and a different claim from a dismissal of it. ⚠️ Both are partly true: entry into construction, lumbering and machine-building was a genuine widening of occupational access, and it occurred under conditions no one had chosen.

### 3. The dilemma, and the reversal of family policy

✅ **Fact:** the government faced the dilemma that it needed women to provide much of the workforce for the industrialisation drive while at the same time it wanted to encourage and strengthen the family unit. ✅ One way of coping was to build more day-care centres and nurseries, and the number of places doubled in the two years 1929-30. ✅ In the mid-1930s new laws were passed encouraging women to have as many children as possible: abortion was made illegal except where the mother's life was in danger; maternity leave of up to sixteen weeks was allowed; and there were to be various subsidies and other benefits for pregnant women. ✅ Even so, this placed a heavy burden on working-class and peasant women, who were expected to produce children, take jobs, increase output and look after the household and family.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the clearest instances in the topic of policy reversing under the pressure of demography and war-preparation, and it is a high-value example precisely because it cuts against the expectation. ⚠️ A regime with a radical rhetoric on the family legislated in the mid-1930s to restrict abortion and to maximise births. ⚠️ **The instrumental reading is the accurate one: women's roles were defined by what the state needed at a given moment - labour first, then labour and children together - and the rhetoric adjusted around the requirement.** That formulation is also usable in comparative questions, because it names a mechanism rather than a doctrine.

### 4. The Wives' Movement

✅ **Fact:** things were different for wives of the elite and for educated women with professional jobs, who were seen by the state as part of its campaign to "civilize" the masses. ✅ The Wives' Movement began in 1936; its aim was to raise the culturedness of the people the wives came into contact with, particularly those in their husbands' workplaces, and their main duty was to make a comfortable home life for their husbands and families. ✅ Towards the end of the 1930s, as war began to seem more likely, the Wives' Movement encouraged women to learn to drive lorries, shoot and even fly planes, so that they would be ready to take men's places if they had to go to war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Wives' Movement is a small subject with a large use, because it demonstrates stratification. ⚠️ A single state was simultaneously conscripting working-class and peasant women into construction and lumbering while organising elite wives to raise domestic standards - and then, within three years, training the second group to drive, shoot and fly. ⚠️ **The Soviet 1930s did not have one policy for women; it had different policies for different strata, and all of them tracked state requirements rather than a doctrine of emancipation.**

### 5. Everyday conditions, which belong in the social answer

✅ **Fact:** in 1933 the average married worker in Moscow consumed less than half the amount of bread and flour consumed by his counterpart around 1900, and in 1937 average real wages were only about three-fifths of what they had been in 1928. ✅ The rapid growth of the urban population - which increased by 31 million between 1926 and 1939 - caused serious housing shortages: local soviets controlled all the housing in a town and had power to evict residents and move new residents into already occupied houses, so that middle-class families in large houses commonly found their home transformed into a communal apartment with two or three other families moved in, sharing kitchens, bathrooms and toilets, and most large houses had people living in corridors and under staircases. ✅ Less fortunate were workers who lived in barracks: in the new industrial city of Magnitogorsk in 1938, half the housing consisted of barracks, the usual accommodation for unmarried workers and students. ✅ City conditions generally were poor, most lacking efficient sewage systems, running water, electric light and street lights; Moscow was the exception, where the government made a real effort.

✅ **Fact:** one of the most annoying aspects of life for ordinary people was the existence of special elite groups - party members, government officials in the bureaucracy known as *nomenklatura*, successful members of the intelligentsia, engineers, experts and Stakhanovites - who escaped the worst of the hardships and enjoyed many privileges: bread delivered to their homes instead of queueing for hours, lower prices, better accommodation and the use of *dachas*. ✅ This resulted in a "them and us" attitude, and ordinary people felt aggrieved that they were still the underdogs.

✅ **Fact, on the other side:** in a speech in November 1935 Stalin told an audience of Stakhanovites that "life has become better, life has become more joyous", and this was not entirely wishful thinking - food supplies improved and all rationing was abolished in 1936; cheap meals in factory canteens and free work clothes were a great help; education and healthcare were free and the number of schools and medical centres was increasing; the state provided more leisure facilities, with close on 30 000 cinemas by the end of the 1930s, sports facilities, public gardens and culture parks, the largest and most famous being Gorky Park in Moscow, and most towns of any size had a theatre and a library.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two paragraphs above are the evidential heart of the argument in Lesson 19 and should be held together rather than used selectively. ⚠️ **Consumption fell and provision rose at the same time.** Real wages at three-fifths of the 1928 level and bread consumption at less than half the 1900 level are not compatible with a simple improvement narrative; free healthcare, free education, abolished rationing, canteens, cinemas and parks are not compatible with a simple immiseration narrative. ⚠️ The accurate statement is that the state substituted collective provision for private consumption, and that the substitution was experienced very differently by those inside and outside the privileged strata - which is exactly why a "them and us" attitude is recorded.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** describing the nationality policy as more liberal than tsarist Russification dignifies a system that deported populations and shot republican leaderships. The concession was cosmetic.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right to refuse the concession as a verdict and wrong to refuse it as a fact, and both halves matter for accuracy. ✅ The record holds both: national cultures and languages were encouraged and the republics had a certain amount of independence, and Moscow had the final say in all important decisions with force available to preserve it; ✅ the 1932 Ukrainian purge and the 1951 Georgian executions show the second hand in operation. ⚠️ Calling the first hand cosmetic, however, cannot explain why the two-handed approach is recorded as working successfully until 1985, since a purely coercive policy over 47 per cent of a population would have required a level of permanent repression the record does not describe outside specific episodes. ⚠️ **The precise statement is that cultural recognition was substantive and political autonomy was not, and that the system was stable for six decades because the first bought enough consent to make the second enforceable at acceptable cost.** That is a harder and more defensible claim than either "cosmetic" or "liberal".

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ To what extent did the lives of ordinary people in the USSR improve or worsen as a result of Stalin's policies, 1928-41?
> ⚠️ Examine the effects of the Russian Revolution on the position of women.
> ⚠️ How did the Soviet state manage its national minorities?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the structure that scores is by *group* rather than by theme: the new managerial and technical strata, skilled industrial workers, unskilled and barrack workers, collectivised peasants, women, and the nationalities. ⚠️ The answer then produces a differentiated verdict, which is what "to what extent" invites. ⚠️ Use the two consumption figures and the two provision lists as the paired evidence, and use the *nomenklatura* privileges to explain why aggregate statements are unsafe.

### Traps

- Writing that the Bolsheviks granted self-determination, full stop. ✅ The decree was real and the civil-war reconquest of four declared republics is also on the record.
- Omitting the 47 per cent figure, which is what makes the nationality question structural rather than incidental.
- Describing Soviet policy on women as straightforwardly emancipatory, which the mid-1930s abortion law and the birth-maximisation drive contradict.
- Using the entry of 10 million women into wage work without noting the loss of male earners that partly produced it.
- Giving the Stalin quotation about life becoming more joyous as pure propaganda. ✅ The account records that it was "not entirely wishful thinking" and lists the provisions that improved.
- Producing an aggregate verdict on living standards. The *nomenklatura* evidence makes aggregates misleading.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1914 inheritance: a dozen and more non-Russian territories, including the three Baltic states.
2. ✅ Finland independent November 1917; Poland and the Baltic republics by Brest-Litovsk, March 1918; Ukraine, Georgia, Armenia and Azerbaijan declared independence in March 1918 and were forced back during the civil war; six Soviet republics by 1925.
3. ✅ 47 per cent of the USSR's population was non-Russian.
4. ✅ Two-handed policy: cultures and languages encouraged and some republican independence, more liberal than tsarist Russification; but Moscow decided all important matters, with force available. It worked until 1985.
5. ✅ 1932: Ukrainian Communist Party purged as "bourgeois nationalist deviationists" for admitting collectivisation had failed; similar campaigns in Belorussia, Transcaucasia and Central Asia; 1951, Georgian leaders removed and shot.
6. ✅ More than 10 million women became wage earners in the 1930s; women rose from 24 to 39 per cent of the paid workforce; about two-thirds of light-industry workers by 1940; also construction, lumbering and machine-building.
7. ✅ Day-care and nursery places doubled in 1929-30; mid-1930s laws made abortion illegal except to save the mother's life, allowed maternity leave up to sixteen weeks, and gave subsidies to pregnant women.
8. ✅ The Wives' Movement from 1936: raising culturedness and home comfort; by the late 1930s, learning to drive lorries, shoot and fly planes.
9. ✅ 1933 bread and flour consumption for a married Moscow worker less than half that of 1900; 1937 real wages about three-fifths of 1928; urban population up 31 million between 1926 and 1939; communal apartments; Magnitogorsk half barracks in 1938; Moscow the exception.
10. ✅ Against that: rationing abolished 1936; canteens and free work clothes; free education and healthcare; close on 30 000 cinemas; Gorky Park; and the *nomenklatura* privileges that produced a "them and us" attitude.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why can no single verdict be given on whether Soviet policy in the 1930s improved the position of women?

**Model answer:** Because the record contains three different movements at once and they point in different directions. ✅ Occupationally the change was large and real: more than 10 million women entered wage earning for the first time, the female share of the paid workforce rose from 24 to 39 per cent, about two-thirds of light-industry workers were women by 1940, and women entered construction, lumbering and machine-building, work traditionally reserved to men. ✅ Legally the movement in the mid-1930s was restrictive rather than liberating: abortion was made illegal except where the mother's life was in danger and new laws encouraged the maximum number of births, with maternity leave and subsidies as inducements. ✅ Socially the burden compounded rather than shifted: working-class and peasant women were expected to produce children, take jobs, increase output and run the household, while elite and professional women were organised from 1936 into a Wives' Movement whose main duty was domestic comfort. ⚠️ And the context of entry into work included the disappearance of so many men during collectivisation, the famine and the Purges, leaving millions of women as sole breadwinners. ⚠️ The defensible conclusion is therefore that occupational access widened sharply while reproductive autonomy narrowed and domestic burdens grew, and that all three changes tracked state requirements rather than any consistent doctrine about women.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the doubling of nursery places proves a welfare motive. The same record attaches it to the state's need for female labour during the industrialisation drive, and the mid-1930s family legislation shows the same instrument used for a different demographic objective.

### Visual: one mission, six instruments, and one recorded reversal

```text
   THE MISSION, IN THE REGIME'S OWN TERMS
   However much they might try, ordinary people could not avoid contact
   with the state - in education, jobs, promotion, marriage, child-
   rearing, housing, shopping, travel, sport, literature, theatre,
   concerts, the visual arts, religion, news and radio.
   WHY: the communists had a mission TO ERADICATE "BACKWARDNESS".
   The state must become modernised and socialist, and the new Soviet
   citizen must be educated and "cultured".
   ARTISTS, MUSICIANS AND WRITERS were to attack "bourgeois" values by
   producing works of SOCIALIST REALISM glorifying the Soviet system.
   STALIN: they were to be "ENGINEERS OF THE HUMAN SOUL".
   (Even the Moscow Dinamo football team was run by the NKVD.)

   INSTRUMENT 1  EDUCATION      | 1917: under half the population
                                | literate. Jan 1930: all children
                                | aged 8-11 to be enrolled by the end
                                | of the summer. 1929-31: pupils from
                                | 14 million to around 20 million,
                                | most of the increase in rural areas.
                                | By 1940: 199 000 schools. 1939
                                | census: 94% of town-dwellers and 86%
                                | of rural people aged 9-49 literate;
                                | by 1959, 99% and 98%.
                                | AND: exams and punishments restored,
                                | teachers given authority, more time
                                | on mathematics and science
   INSTRUMENT 2  RELIGION       | working priests fall from about
                                | 60 000 in 1925 to under 6000 by 1941;
                                | by 1941 only one church building in
                                | forty still in use for worship;
                                | hundreds of Muslim and Jewish leaders
                                | also killed
                                | >>> REVERSED IN 1942 <<<
   INSTRUMENT 3  LITERATURE     | RAPP -> AUW dissolved -> AUSW ->
                                | all replaced by the Union of Soviet
                                | Writers under Maxim Gorky; Zhdanov,
                                | 1934: the guiding principle is "the
                                | ideological remoulding and re-
                                | education of the toiling people in
                                | the spirit of socialism"
   INSTRUMENT 4  ART & MUSIC    | abstract art rejected; photographic
                                | style required; RAPM condemns
                                | "modernism"; relaxation by the
                                | mid-1930s for jazz, dance and light
                                | music
   INSTRUMENT 5  CINEMA         | films "intelligible to the millions";
                                | over 300 Soviet films made 1933-40;
                                | cinemas from about 7000 to around
                                | 30 000
   INSTRUMENT 6  KUL'TURNOST'   | "culturedness": appearance, hygiene,
                                | sheets, knife and fork, no
                                | drunkenness or bad language, not
                                | beating your wife and children;
                                | 13 000 new bread shops from late 1934
                                | with white smocks, politeness lessons
                                | and wrapped loaves
```

*Two features of this list should be noticed before any detail: the reach is total in intention, and the one recorded reversal is the religious one - which is why it is the best available test of how instrumental the whole programme was.*

### 1. Education: the largest uncontested achievement, with its motive attached

✅ **Fact:** one of the greatest achievements of the Stalinist regime was the expansion of free, mass education. ✅ In 1917 under half the population could be described as literate. ✅ In January 1930 the government announced that by the end of the summer all children aged 8 to 11 must be enrolled in schools; between 1929 and 1931 the number of pupils increased from 14 million to around 20 million, and it was in rural areas, where education had been patchy, that most of the increase took place. ✅ By 1940 there were 199 000 schools and even the most remote areas were well provided, and many new training colleges were set up to train a new generation of teachers and lecturers. ✅ According to the 1939 census, of people aged between 9 and 49, 94 per cent in the towns and 86 per cent in rural areas were literate, and by 1959 these had increased to 99 and 98 per cent.

✅ **Fact:** the regime had an ulterior motive - education was the way by which it could turn the younger generation into good, orthodox Soviet citizens, and religion and other "bourgeois" practices were presented as superstitious and backward. ✅ Ironically, the education experts decided that a return to traditional teaching methods would be better than the experimental, more relaxed techniques tried in the 1920s, which had included the abolition of examinations and punishments and an emphasis on project work; this was now reversed, with teachers given more authority and strict discipline, examinations brought back, and more teaching time spent on mathematics and science.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two points, both usable. ⚠️ First, this is the achievement that is hardest to argue away and easiest to under-use, and it belongs in any answer on effect on society: a rise from under half the population literate to 94 and 86 per cent in a generation, concentrated in the rural areas where provision had been worst. ⚠️ Second, the pedagogical reversal of the 1930s is a miniature of the whole cultural policy: experiment in the 1920s, conservatism in the 1930s, and the conservatism justified by output. **The same state that abolished examinations in the name of revolution restored them in the name of engineers** - and the mathematics-and-science emphasis names exactly what the plans of Lesson 15 needed.

### 2. Religion: the campaign and the reversal

✅ **Fact:** Lenin, Stalin and the other Bolshevik leaders were atheists who accepted Marx's claim that religion was an invention of the ruling classes to keep the people docile - the "opium of the masses". ✅ Lenin had launched a savage attack on the Orthodox Church, seizing all its lands, schools and church buildings and having hundreds of priests arrested. ✅ After Lenin's death the regime became more tolerant towards religious groups; many priests were sympathetic towards communist ideals, and there seemed a good chance of complete reconciliation between Church and State, with careful handling making the Church useful in helping to control the peasants - but many militant young communists continued to believe that religion was a harmful superstition, and a "League of Militant Godless" was formed to persecute the clergy and eliminate religion as far as possible.

✅ **Fact:** relations deteriorated disastrously during Stalin's regime. ✅ Many priests courageously opposed collectivisation, so Stalin secretly instructed local party organisations to attack churches and priests; hundreds of churches and cemeteries were vandalised and literally thousands of priests were killed. ✅ The number of working priests fell from about 60 000 in 1925 to under 6000 by 1941; the slaughter was not confined to Christians, and hundreds of Muslim and Jewish leaders also fell victim; by 1941 only one in forty church buildings was still functioning as a place of worship. ✅ The anti-religious campaign caused outrage, especially in rural areas where priests, mullahs, shamans and rabbis were popular and respected members of the local communities.

✅ **Fact:** during the Second World War, State and Church were to some extent reconciled. ✅ In 1942, with the war going badly and both Leningrad and Moscow under attack, Stalin decided that religion had a role to play after all, as a force for patriotism; an understanding was reached with Christians, Jews and Muslims that past differences would be forgotten in their joint struggle against the invader; churches, mosques and synagogues were allowed to reopen; and by most accounts the religious groups played a vital role in maintaining morale among the general public.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1942 reversal is the single most analytically valuable episode in this lesson, and it should be used rather than merely reported. ⚠️ A regime that had reduced the working clergy from about 60 000 to under 6000 in sixteen years restored religious institutions within a year of invasion because they were useful. ⚠️ **That demonstrates that the anti-religious campaign was subordinate to state requirement rather than doctrinally absolute - and by extension it supports the instrumental reading of the whole cultural programme.** It also connects to the same mechanism seen in Lesson 10, where foreign intervention was converted into patriotic legitimacy, and in Lesson 17, where family policy reversed under demographic pressure.

⚠️ One timing point worth a clause: the intensification of the campaign is recorded as a response to clerical opposition to collectivisation. ⚠️ The clergy were attacked hardest at the moment they became an obstacle to the agrarian policy, which is another instance of the pattern and a useful cross-reference to Lesson 14.

### 3. Literature and theatre

✅ **Fact:** the years 1928 to 1931 became known as "the Cultural Revolution", when the regime began to mobilise writers, artists and musicians to wage a cultural war against "bourgeois intellectuals". ✅ There were at first two rival groups: the dedicated communists of the All-Russian Association of Proletarian Writers (RAPP), committed to socialist realism, and the non-communists who wanted to keep politics out of literature, dismissively labelled "fellow-travellers", members of the All-Russian Union of Writers (AUW), which included most of the leading writers who had made their names before the revolution. ✅ RAPP accused some AUW members of publishing anti-Soviet works abroad; they were found guilty and the government dissolved the AUW, replacing it with the All-Russian Union of Soviet Writers (AUSW), to which about half the former members were refused admission - a serious blow, since only union members were allowed to publish.

✅ **Fact:** this left RAPP dominant, but it soon fell foul of Stalin: its members believed in portraying society as it really was, with all its faults, whereas Stalin wanted it portrayed as he would like it to be. ✅ In 1930 Stalin announced that nothing could be published which went against the party line or showed the Party in a poor light, and when some RAPP members failed to respond he disbanded both RAPP and the AUSW, replacing them with one organisation, the Union of Soviet Writers, chaired by Maxim Gorky. ✅ Andrei Zhdanov emerged as the politician most involved in the arts, announcing at the first Congress of Soviet Writers in 1934 that the guiding principle must be "the ideological remoulding and re-education of the toiling people in the spirit of socialism".

✅ **Fact:** among the most popular new works were Nikolai Ostrovsky's *How the Steel was Tempered* (1934) and Mikhail Sholokov's *Virgin Soil Upturned*, which dealt with collectivisation; there were other works of lesser quality, sometimes known as "five-year plan" novels, whose heroes were ordinary people bravely achieving their targets against obstacles - not great literature, but arguably serving a purpose, since they were easily understood, raised morale and inspired greater efforts.

✅ **Fact:** writers who did not produce the right kind of socialist realism ran the risk of arrest; Stalin himself sometimes read novels in typescript and added comments and suggested changes which authors were expected to note. ✅ Among the best-known victims were the poet Osip Mandelstam, who had written a poem criticising Stalin and was sent to a labour camp where he died, and Evgenia Ginsburg, who spent eighteen years in prison and labour camps after being accused of organising a writers' terrorist group; some of the best writers, such as the poet Anna Akhmatova and the novelist Boris Pasternak, either stopped work altogether or kept their new work locked away, and Pasternak's *Dr Zhivago* and Mikhail Bulgakov's *The Master and Margarita* were published only after Stalin's death. ✅ Soon after Khrushchev came to power in 1956 the authorities announced that at least 600 writers had perished in prisons or labour camps during Stalin's rule. ✅ Theatre people also came under attack; the most famous victim was the experimental director Vsevolod Meyerhold, whose Moscow theatre was closed in 1938 as "alien to Soviet art" and who was arrested, tortured and later shot, his wife, a well-known actress, being found stabbed to death in their flat. ✅ Ironically, after the first flush of the Cultural Revolution the regime reinstated nineteenth-century classical Russian literature - Pushkin, Tolstoy, Gogol, Turgenev and Chekhov - having decided that these were "revolutionary democrats".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence RAPP-AUW-AUSW-Union of Soviet Writers is not bureaucratic detail; it is the mechanism of control made visible in four steps. ⚠️ Publication was restricted to union members; the union was dissolved and reconstituted twice, each time excluding people; and the final body was chaired by a writer Stalin admired. ⚠️ **Control over literature was exercised through control of the right to publish, not primarily through censorship of texts** - which is why membership rules matter more than any individual banning. ⚠️ Note also that RAPP, the most committed communist body, was destroyed for wanting to depict society with its faults: **ideological enthusiasm was no protection, which is the same finding as the Purges produce in the political sphere.**

### 4. Art, music and the Shostakovich episode

✅ **Fact:** artists, sculptors and musicians were all expected to play their part in socialist realism; abstract art was rejected and paintings were expected to portray workers straining every muscle to fulfil their targets, scenes from the revolution or the civil war, or revolutionary leaders, in a photographic and finely detailed style; there was a steady flow of paintings of Lenin and Stalin and worker scenes with titles like *The Steelworker* and *The Milkmaids*; sculptors were limited to busts of Lenin and Stalin; and architecture deteriorated into the uninspiring and dull, with grandiose neoclassical façades and featureless tower blocks. ✅ The Russian Association of Proletarian Musicians (RAPM) condemned the "modernism" of western music, including the atonal twelve-note music of Schoenberg, Webern and Berg, but also jazz, music-hall light music and even the foxtrot; in the mid-1930s the regime relaxed and jazz, dance and light music were permitted.

✅ **Fact:** the USSR had two outstanding classical composers of international reputation by the 1930s - Sergei Prokofiev, who had left Russia soon after the Revolution but returned in 1933 and was especially successful at producing high-quality music that ordinary people could appreciate, his ballet *Romeo and Juliet* and his musical story *Peter and the Wolf* being highly popular with audiences and authorities; and Dmitri Shostakovich, who was not so successful. ✅ Shostakovich's first opera, *The Nose*, based on a Gogol story, was condemned and banned by RAPM in 1930; his second, *Lady Macbeth of Mtsensk*, was well received by audiences and critics in 1934 and ran for over 80 performances in Leningrad and over 90 in Moscow; but in January 1936 Stalin attended a performance in Moscow and walked out before the end, and two days later a devastating article in *Pravda*, thought to have been written by Stalin himself, dismissed the opera as "a cacophony, crude and vulgar" and Shostakovich's work was banned. ✅ Badly shaken, he expected to be arrested; for some reason he was spared, probably because Maxim Gorky defended him, pointing out that some of his music was much more tuneful than the opera. ✅ The American ambassador in Moscow noted afterwards that "half the artists and musicians in Moscow are having nervous prostration, and the others are trying to imagine how to write and compose in a manner to please Stalin". ✅ Shostakovich redeemed himself with his Fifth Symphony in 1937.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Shostakovich episode is the best single case study in the topic of how cultural control actually operated, and its value lies in what it shows about arbitrariness. ⚠️ A work approved by audiences and critics for two years was destroyed by one man walking out of one performance; the artist expected arrest and was spared through a personal intercession; and the observable effect on everyone else, as the ambassador recorded, was not compliance with a rule but an attempt to guess at a taste. ⚠️ **A system in which the standard is a private preference rather than a published criterion produces anticipatory self-censorship, which is more efficient than censorship and leaves fewer records** - and that observation applies well beyond music.

### 5. Cinema, and the culturedness campaign

✅ **Fact:** Stalin, like Lenin, considered film probably the most important form of communication; he loved films, had a private cinema in the Kremlin and one in his *dacha*, and demanded that Soviet films be "intelligible to the millions", telling a simple but powerful story. ✅ Boris Shumyatsky was given the job of modernising the film industry in 1930 and aimed at films that were genuinely entertaining as well as full of socialist realism, but was hampered by the arrival of sound films, which were more expensive and raised a language problem in a country of many languages, and by the regime's almost impossible and sometimes contradictory demands - proletarian values, classless Soviet nationalism, the problems of ordinary people, the heroic exploits of the revolutionaries and the glorious communist future. ✅ In 1935 Shumyatsky went to Hollywood for ideas and proposed a Soviet equivalent in the Crimea, but the government refused the finance and the project never got off the ground; Stalin was not satisfied with his progress and in 1938 he was arrested and shot. ✅ In spite of all this, over 300 Soviet films were made between 1933 and 1940, some of high quality, and the number of cinemas rose from about 7000 to around 30 000 in the same period. ✅ Stalin became so obsessed that he vetted many scripts himself, needing to be satisfied that they put over the message that life in the USSR was better and happier in every way than anywhere else. ✅ Sergei Eisenstein failed to repeat his great masterpieces of the 1920s - *Strike*, *Battleship Potemkin* and *October* - until in 1938 he salvaged his reputation with the patriotic *Alexander Nevsky*, which told of the medieval invasion of Russia by Teutonic knights and their defeat, and which, given the international situation, hit exactly the right note with the censors.

✅ **Fact:** the state also encouraged what the Russians called *kul'turnost'* - culturedness - involving care over appearance and personal hygiene; some industrial enterprises ordered that engineers and managers be clean-shaven with neatly cut hair; conditions in barracks were improved by partitions giving each person his own space; and other signs of culture were sleeping on sheets, eating with a knife and fork, avoiding drunkenness and bad language, and not beating your wife and children. ✅ According to Stephen Kotkin, the cultured person was one who had learned to "speak Bolshevik": he knew how to conduct himself in the workplace, stopped spitting on the floor, could make a speech and propose a motion, and could understand the basic ideas of Marxism. ✅ Culturedness extended to shopping: at the end of 1934 over 13 000 new bread shops opened across the country, the assistants wearing white smocks and caps and taking lessons in politeness, with strict new sanitary regulations and loaves required to be wrapped, and the campaign for "cultured trade" spread to every shop in the country.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the *Alexander Nevsky* case and the culturedness campaign together make the point that the cultural programme had two distinct functions. ⚠️ One was mobilisational and increasingly national: a film about repelling a Germanic invasion, approved in 1938 for reasons the account attaches to the international situation, is a long way from proletarian internationalism. ⚠️ The other was civilising in the most literal sense - hygiene, table manners, politeness, and the prohibition of domestic violence taught as a marker of culture. ⚠️ **Kotkin's "speak Bolshevik" is the sharpest formulation of what was actually required: not belief but competence in a public idiom**, which is precisely what the double existence of Lesson 16 describes from the other side.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** grouping education with censorship under a single heading of "manufacturing a citizen" is tendentious. Literacy is an unambiguous good and does not belong in the same analysis as the shooting of a theatre director.

⚠️ **Reply:** they belong in the same analysis because the record puts them there, and separating them would misdescribe the policy. ✅ The account states the ulterior motive explicitly: education was the way by which the regime could turn the younger generation into good, orthodox Soviet citizens, with religion and other "bourgeois" practices presented as superstitious and backward. ⚠️ That does not diminish the achievement - a rise to 94 and 86 per cent literacy is a fact whatever the motive - but it does mean that schooling, publishing rules, film scripts and hygiene campaigns were parts of one programme with one stated objective, the eradication of backwardness and the production of a cultured Soviet citizen. ⚠️ **The correct analytical move is to keep the evaluation of each item separate while keeping the description of the programme unified**, and an answer that does both is demonstrably reading the evidence rather than sorting it into approved and disapproved piles.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ To what extent did the lives of ordinary people in the USSR improve or worsen as a result of Stalin's policies?
> ⚠️ Examine the effects of Stalinist rule on education, religion and culture.
> ⚠️ "The Soviet state set out to manufacture a new kind of citizen." Discuss.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ The effect-on-society demand is the half of the syllabus clause most candidates omit, so structure by domain and not by chronology: education, religion, the arts and everyday conduct, each scored separately. ⚠️ Lead with the literacy record, because it is the strongest thing the regime can be credited with and conceding it early makes the criticism that follows look weighed rather than reflexive - and attach its stated motive in the same sentence. ⚠️ Use the reconciliation of 1942 as the analytical hinge: a campaign that had reduced the working clergy from about 60 000 to under 6000 was reversed within a year of invasion because religion had become useful, which is the clearest available proof that the cultural programme was subordinate to state requirement. ⚠️ Name the mechanism of literary control precisely - publication restricted to union members, with the union twice dissolved and reconstituted - rather than calling it censorship. ⚠️ Keep the Shostakovich episode to three sentences and use it for what it shows about arbitrariness and anticipatory self-censorship. ⚠️ Close on Kotkin's formulation: what the state actually required was not belief but competence in a public idiom.

### Traps

- Treating the literacy achievement as uncontested propaganda, or as politically neutral. It is real and it had a stated instrumental purpose.
- Omitting the 1942 religious reversal, which is the best evidence for the instrumental character of the whole programme.
- Describing socialist realism only in literature. ✅ It governed painting, sculpture, architecture, music and film.
- Presenting censorship as the main mechanism in literature. ✅ Control of the right to publish through union membership was the primary instrument.
- Forgetting that RAPP, the most committed communist body, was itself disbanded.
- Treating the culturedness campaign as comic. ✅ Kotkin's formulation makes it the acquisition of a public competence, which is central to how the system worked.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The mission: eradicate backwardness; artists, musicians and writers as "engineers of the human soul" producing socialist realism.
2. ✅ Education: under half literate in 1917; enrolment order of January 1930 for ages 8-11; pupils from 14 to around 20 million, 1929-31; 199 000 schools by 1940; 1939 census literacy 94 per cent urban and 86 per cent rural for ages 9-49; 99 and 98 per cent by 1959.
3. ✅ Pedagogical reversal: exams and punishments restored, teachers' authority increased, more mathematics and science.
4. ✅ Religion: working priests from about 60 000 in 1925 to under 6000 by 1941; one church building in forty in use by 1941; Muslim and Jewish leaders also killed; intensification linked to clerical opposition to collectivisation.
5. ✅ 1942 reversal: religion as a force for patriotism; churches, mosques and synagogues reopened; religious groups credited with maintaining morale.
6. ✅ Literature: the Cultural Revolution of 1928-31; RAPP against the AUW; the AUW dissolved and the AUSW formed, excluding about half; only union members could publish; both bodies disbanded and replaced by the Union of Soviet Writers under Gorky; Zhdanov's 1934 formula.
7. ✅ Victims and refusals: Mandelstam died in a camp; Ginsburg served eighteen years; Akhmatova and Pasternak withheld work; *Dr Zhivago* and *The Master and Margarita* published only after Stalin's death; at least 600 writers announced as having perished; Meyerhold's theatre closed in 1938 and Meyerhold arrested, tortured and shot.
8. ✅ Art and music: abstract art rejected; photographic style; RAPM against modernism and jazz; relaxation by the mid-1930s; Prokofiev's return in 1933; the *Lady Macbeth* episode of January 1936 and the Fifth Symphony of 1937.
9. ✅ Cinema: "intelligible to the millions"; Shumyatsky appointed 1930, arrested and shot 1938; over 300 films 1933-40; cinemas from about 7000 to around 30 000; Eisenstein's *Alexander Nevsky*, 1938.
10. ✅ *Kul'turnost'*: hygiene, sheets, cutlery, sobriety and not beating your wife and children; Kotkin's "speak Bolshevik"; over 13 000 new bread shops from late 1934 with politeness lessons and wrapped loaves.

### Concept check

**Question:** What does the reconciliation with the churches in 1942 show about the nature of Soviet cultural and ideological policy?

**Model answer:** ✅ It shows that the policy was subordinate to state requirement rather than doctrinally fixed. The anti-religious campaign had been severe and sustained: Lenin had seized the Orthodox Church's lands, schools and buildings and had hundreds of priests arrested; Stalin secretly instructed local party organisations to attack churches and priests after many opposed collectivisation; hundreds of churches and cemeteries were vandalised and thousands of priests killed; working priests fell from about 60 000 in 1925 to under 6000 by 1941; and by 1941 only one church building in forty was still in use for worship. ✅ Yet in 1942, with the war going badly and Leningrad and Moscow under attack, Stalin decided religion had a role after all as a force for patriotism, an understanding was reached with Christians, Jews and Muslims that past differences would be forgotten, and places of worship were allowed to reopen, with the religious groups credited by most accounts with maintaining public morale. ⚠️ The inference is not that the atheism was insincere but that it ranked below survival, and the same ranking appears elsewhere in the record - capitalist piecework inside a socialist plan, a restrictive family law from a revolutionary party, and a medieval patriotic epic approved in 1938. ⚠️ **Doctrine supplied the default; requirement supplied the exception**, and identifying that as the operating rule is what converts a list of cultural policies into an argument.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the reversal was permanent or generous. It is recorded as a wartime accommodation reached under extreme military pressure, and Lesson 20 shows the intellectual sphere being tightened again from August 1946.

---

## Lesson 19 - The 1936 Constitution and the totalitarian debate: a text, a practice, and three schools

Progress: 19/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: what the 1936 Constitution said and what made it inoperative; the definition of a totalitarian regime and the evidence for it; the social-revisionist challenge and what it rests on; the Getty challenge and what it rests on; the exchange of accusations between the schools; and the balanced conclusion the record supports
