# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - The old order and its collapse, Lessons 1 to 5

### Cumulative check A1 - assembling the collapse

**Question:** Assemble the explanation of the collapse of tsarism in four ranked components, and state precisely what the war contributed that the other three could not.

**Model answer:** The first component is constitutional. ✅ The October Manifesto promised an elected Duma, freedom of conscience, speech, assembly and association, universal suffrage for Duma elections and a rule that no law could operate without the Duma's approval; the First Duma was dispersed by troops after ten weeks, the Second met the same fate, and the franchise was then altered to deprive peasants and urban workers of the vote, with Nicholas recorded as remarking that a democratic republic would be senseless and criminal. The second is agrarian. ✅ Redemption payments dating from the emancipation of 1861 had reduced over half the rural population to dire poverty; Stolypin abolished them and encouraged land purchase, with about 2 million peasants buying land by 1916 and 3.5 million emigrating to Siberia, but by 1911 the reforms were failing because the peasant population was growing at 1.5 million a year and farming methods were too inefficient. The third is social and political alienation. ✅ Strikes rose from over 2000 in 1912 to 2400 in 1913 and over 4000 in the first seven months of 1914 after the shooting of 270 miners at the Lena goldfields; the secret police alienated students, lecturers and deported groups; and the dynasty was discredited by Rasputin and by the manner of Stolypin's murder. ⚠️ What the war contributed was none of these: it destroyed the state's *capacity*. Trains carrying troops left food in the countryside while bread became scarce in the capital; Nicholas's assumption of supreme command in August 1915 attached the defeats to him personally; and by January 1917 the aristocracy, the Duma, industrialists and the army had turned against him to forestall a worse revolution. ⚠️ The first three explain why upheaval was probable; the war explains why the instruments of repression were not available when it came.

**Misconception to avoid:** Adding the war to a list of grievances as a seventh item. It belongs in a different causal category - a converting variable acting on pre-existing structures - and treating it as one more grievance makes the ranking impossible.

### Cumulative check A2 - the historiography of inevitability, used rather than listed

**Question:** Show how the recorded positions on inevitability sort into two distinct claims, and state which claim the evidence supports more strongly.

**Model answer:** The first claim concerns the *probability of upheaval*. ✅ Kennan and Haimson held that the regime would have collapsed sooner or later even without the war; Fitzpatrick that it was so vulnerable to any jolt or setback that it is hard to imagine it surviving long; and Soviet historiography that the unfinished revolution of 1905-06 could not long be delayed, with the war actually postponing it. Against them, Read held that the overthrow was by no means inevitable and that the pre-1914 situation could have continued indefinitely provided there was no war. The second claim concerns the *type* of upheaval. ✅ Service made this explicit: Russia was in a condition of general brittleness and a vulnerable plant, but it was not doomed to the root-and-branch revolution of 1917, and what made that kind of revolution possible was the protracted, exhausting conflict of the war. ⚠️ On the first claim the evidence is genuinely balanced, because the same decade contains both a rising strike series culminating in a Bolshevik-organised general strike in the capital in July 1914 and a real record of recovery - 50 000 additional primary schools by 1914, a 1912 insurance scheme, factory inspection, and land purchases by about 2 million peasants. ⚠️ On the second the evidence is one-sided: the party that produced the eventual outcome had been short of money, split and led from exile only two years before the war, and a revolution producing a one-party state was not a foreseeable extension of the 1914 situation. ⚠️ The supportable verdict is therefore that upheaval was probable and that the form it took was contingent on the war.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the debate as a list of historians with opinions. Sorting it into two claims converts recall into analysis and is what the "how far" directive is asking for.

## Block B - Seizure and consolidation, Lessons 6 to 12

### Cumulative check B1 - what the Bolsheviks actually inherited and what they built

**Question:** Explain how a party that was a minority in the country in October 1917 was still in undisputed control by 1924, using instruments rather than adjectives.

**Model answer:** Four instruments, each answering a defined problem. ✅ Against the absence of support, the decrees: land nationalised without compensation including crown and church estates, workers' control over managers, the eight-hour day, self-determination for national groups, the nationalisation of banks, large factories and mines, the cancellation of tsarist and Provisional debts, and the admission of Left Social Revolutionaries as junior partners because they had far more rural support. ✅ Against organised hostility, the security apparatus: the claimed right to close hostile newspapers and the Cheka under Felix Dzierzynski, which in 1918 suppressed 245 peasant uprisings and 99 in the first seven months of 1919, with official figures of over 3000 peasants killed and 6300 executed and a real toll the account says was probably much higher. ✅ Against an election that could not be won, dispersal: the Constituent Assembly, in which the Bolsheviks took 175 seats of about 700 against 370 for the Social Revolutionaries, was dissolved by Red Guards in January 1918 on the doctrine that a party which knew what the workers wanted needed no elected parliament. ✅ Against a war that could not be continued, Brest-Litovsk in March 1918, surrendering Poland, the Baltic states, Ukraine, Georgia and Finland with a third of the farming land, a third of the population, two-thirds of the coalmines and half the heavy industry. ⚠️ Two further instruments completed the settlement: victory in a civil war won on firmer leadership, control of the central core, White disunity and the patriotic dividend of foreign intervention; and the March 1921 combination of NEP with the ban on factions, which expelled about a third of the party's members during that year. ⚠️ The composite is that the regime bought the countryside, removed the electoral route, acquired a coercive instrument, ended the external war, won the internal one, and then closed the last channel of legitimate internal dissent.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the civil war as the explanation. It was necessary but not sufficient; the decisive instruments in January and March 1918 preceded it, and the decisive political instrument of March 1921 followed it.

### Cumulative check B2 - NEP as the hinge of the whole topic

**Question:** Explain why the New Economic Policy is the pivot on which both the Lenin question and the Stalin question turn.

**Model answer:** Because it is the one point at which the regime chose inducement over coercion, and both later arguments run through that choice. ✅ For the Lenin question, NEP is the strongest evidence against the strong intentionalist reading: the peasants kept surplus produce after a proportional tax, private trade and small industry were restored, old managers, bonuses and piece-rates returned and foreign investment was encouraged, while Lenin told the Party in May 1921 that it must be pursued seriously and for a long time, not less than a decade and probably more, and described it not as a retreat but as an alternative road to socialism to be completed by educating peasants into co-operatives so that force would not be necessary. ⚠️ Set beside Tucker's evidence of Lenin's May 1919 pamphlet on curing backwardness by education, that makes a coercion-by-necessity reading defensible - though the ban on factions of the same month, the expulsion of about a third of the membership, and the subordination of the trade unions show that the political monopoly was never on the table. ✅ For the Stalin question, NEP is the benchmark that the 1929 turn is measured against: by late 1927, when it began to be abandoned, the ordinary Russian was probably better off than at any time since 1914, with an eight-hour day, paid holidays, sick and unemployment pay and healthcare for employed workers and peasant living standards above 1913. ⚠️ That means the abandonment has to be argued for rather than assumed, and the argument available - the faltering of food deliveries, the strategic fear, and the party's genuine belief that kulaks were blocking progress - is exactly the argument Lesson 14 tests against the harvest and procurement series.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating NEP as an economic episode. Its political counterpart, adopted in the same month, is what makes it evidence about the regime rather than about the economy.

## Block C - The transformation and its cost, Lessons 13 to 16

### Cumulative check C1 - the two revolutions from above, connected

**Question:** Show how collectivisation and industrialisation were a single decision, and how the Purges follow from them.

**Model answer:** They were a single decision because each supplied what the other required. ✅ Industrialisation needed a reliable grain supply for the growing cities and a way of financing imported technology, and collectivisation delivered exactly that: state procurement rose from 10.8 million tons in 1928 to 22.6 in 1933 and exports from 0.029 million tons in 1927-8 to 4.76 in 1930 and 5.06 in 1931, while the harvest itself did not rise - 73.3 in 1928, 68.4 in 1933, lower in 1934 than in 1928. ✅ Collectivisation in turn needed an industrial sector to supply the tractors that justified it, and the account records that the government did not at first provide sufficient tractors while peasants had slaughtered their horses, so the substantial production increase attributed to mechanisation came only in 1937. ⚠️ The Purges follow because the social cost of that double transformation generated political danger: by the summer of 1930 the government's popularity had fallen sharply because of collectivisation and the hardships of the First Five Year Plan, there was growing opposition inside the Party, and the Ryutin Platform was circulating. ✅ The terror then removed the people capable of acting on that opposition - Lenin's old Bolshevik Party was its main victim, many of the best brains in government and industry disappeared, and the purge of the army disrupted defence policy at a time of great international tension and contributed to the disasters of 1941-2. ⚠️ The sequence is therefore economic decision, social cost, political danger, and terror as the political sequel - which is why an answer that treats the Purges as an unrelated outbreak of paranoia has missed the causal chain.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the Purges primarily by Stalin's psychology. Khrushchev's "sickly suspicious" and Medvedev's "coolly ruthless" are both on the record, but the recorded political background - collapsed popularity, party opposition, a circulated platform - is evidence, and psychology is inference.

### Cumulative check C2 - the achievement and the cost, weighed without a rate of exchange

**Question:** State the case for and against the Stalinist economic transformation, and explain how a verdict can be reached without implying that output justified deaths.

**Model answer:** ✅ The case for is specific: in 1929 France, not a leading industrial power, produced more coal and steel than Russia, and Stalin held that rapid heavy industrialisation was essential against an attack he expected from the western capitalist powers; McCauley records genuine enthusiasm and prodigious production achievements in the First Plan, with impossible targets galvanising people into action; Nove holds that something drastic was needed given tsarist backwardness, that a great industry was built, and asks where the Russian army would have been in 1942 without a Urals-Siberian metallurgical base; and the education, literacy and health provisions of the same years were real. ✅ The case against is equally specific: Freeborn's judgement that the First Plan was a declaration of war by the state machine against workers and peasants, supported by the 1933 Moscow consumption figure of less than half the 1900 level and the 1937 real wage at about three-fifths of 1928; the grain and livestock series; the famine of 1932-3; the destruction of expertise and the army command; and the 1952 harvest at three-quarters of the 1940 level. ⚠️ A verdict is reached without any rate of exchange by asking a causal question instead of a moral one: were the costs incidental to the achievement or constitutive of it? ⚠️ On this record they were constitutive - the same targets produced the output and the wrecking charges, and the grain that fed the cities and paid for imported technology is the grain recorded in the export series - which supports the conclusion that the achievement was real, that no cheaper version of it was attempted, and that the regime treated the cost as a price rather than as a problem.

**Misconception to avoid:** Producing a two-column list and calling it balance. Balance is a relation between the columns, and the relation here is causal, not arithmetical.

## Block D - System, society and judgement, Lessons 17 to 21

### Cumulative check D1 - why no single verdict on Soviet society is available

**Question:** Explain why aggregate statements about whether Soviet society improved under Stalin are unsafe, and give the disaggregation that replaces them.

**Model answer:** Because the same years produced sharply divergent outcomes for different groups, and the record identifies the groups. ✅ Consumption fell - bread and flour for a married Moscow worker in 1933 were less than half the 1900 level and real wages in 1937 about three-fifths of 1928 - while collective provision rose: rationing abolished in 1936, factory canteens, free work clothes, free education and healthcare, close on 30 000 cinemas, parks and libraries. ✅ A privileged stratum existed and was visible: party members, the *nomenklatura*, successful intelligentsia, engineers, experts and Stakhanovites had bread delivered rather than queued for, lower prices, better accommodation and *dachas*, producing a recorded "them and us" attitude. ✅ For women, occupational access widened enormously - over 10 million entering wage work, the female share rising from 24 to 39 per cent, two-thirds of light industry by 1940 - while abortion was criminalised except to save the mother's life and household burdens compounded. ✅ For the nationalities, cultures and languages were encouraged and the republics held some independence, more liberal than tsarist Russification, while Moscow decided everything important and purged the Ukrainian party in 1932 for admitting a policy failure. ✅ For peasants the direction was uniformly downward until the war and beyond. ⚠️ The disaggregation that replaces the aggregate is therefore by group - new managerial and technical strata, skilled workers, unskilled and barrack workers, collectivised peasants, women, nationalities - with a separate verdict for each and a statement that the regime's gains in provision were financed by losses in consumption.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the double existence recorded by Service as evidence of universal hostility. It makes public evidence about opinion unreliable in both directions, which is a reason for caution rather than for a contrary conclusion.

### Cumulative check D2 - assembling the seven demand families

**Question:** Given a stem you have not seen before on this topic, describe the procedure that produces a correct structure, and illustrate it on the stem "How accurate is it to talk about a Stalin Revolution in economic and political affairs during the period 1928 to 1941?"

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The procedure has five steps: identify which of the three subjects the stem names; identify the directive and the answer shape it requires; select two pieces of evidence that a weaker script will not have; decide the concession; and write the graded verdict before the introduction. ⚠️ Applied to this stem: the subject is the transformation, so the 1917 material is excluded except for one clause on inherited instruments. The directive is "how accurate", which tests a *description* rather than a policy, so the answer must interrogate the word "revolution" - whether a change imposed from above, by a state, on a society, using existing institutions, qualifies as one. ✅ The two pieces of evidence are the harvest-against-procurement contrast, which shows a transformation of control rather than of output, and the continuity of the political instruments, since the one-party state, the security police and the ban on factions all predate 1928. ⚠️ The concession is that in scale the change was revolutionary: over 90 per cent of farmland collectivised by 1937, a country that produced less coal and steel than France in 1929 moving decisively past France and Britain on several measures, literacy rising towards the 94 and 86 per cent of the 1939 census, and an entire new managerial stratum created. ⚠️ The graded verdict is that "revolution" is accurate for the economy and the social structure and misleading for the political system, where 1928-41 radicalised an order established between 1917 and 1922 rather than replacing it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Answering an "how accurate is this description" stem as though it were "what happened". The stem is about a word, and the answer must test the word against the evidence.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ These seven questions are this session's own. **None is a previous-year question**, none is represented as one, and no owned verified question exists for this topic. Each model answer is written inside the stated word ceiling and carries a scoring note identifying the single feature that earns the marks.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Why did the Provisional Government of 1917 fail to survive the year? Examine.

### Model answer

It failed because it held office without the power to enforce a decision. The Petrograd Soviet, elected at the end of February before the abdication, instructed soldiers to obey only itself, so the government could not rely on the army in the last resort. Every major choice then became unpopular or unenforceable. It continued the war, and Kerensky's June offensive collapsed army discipline and sent hundreds of thousands of deserters home. It delayed Constituent Assembly elections, arguing that wartime made them impossible, and so never acquired a mandate. It postponed land redistribution while peasants seized estates. Inflation and shortages continued. The Bolshevik programme - separate peace, land, workers' control, cheaper food - was constructed precisely from the things it could not do, and Kornilov's march in August destroyed its standing with the officer corps. Dual power, not incompetence, explains the fall; the June offensive merely made it quick.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from replacing the adjective "weak" with a named mechanism and then deriving each failure from it, and from the final sentence separating the structural verdict from the decisional one.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ "Collectivisation achieved what it was designed to achieve." Comment.

### Model answer

Only if the design is read correctly. Judged by its stated objective, output, it failed: total grain production did not increase at all except in 1930, standing at 73.3 million tons in 1928 and 68.4 in 1933, and lower in 1934 than in 1928, while cattle fell from 70.5 to 38.4 million and livestock production recovered to the 1928 figure only in 1953. Judged by the objective it actually delivered, control, it succeeded: state procurement rose from 10.8 million tons in 1928 to 22.6 in 1933, and exports from 0.029 million tons in 1927-8 to 5.06 in 1931. The government could now obtain grain without bargaining, and the risk of a poor harvest shifted from the cities to the villages, where famine followed in 1932-3 while 1.75 million tons were exported. The design contained a contradiction: eliminating the most productive farmers while promising higher yields.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from splitting the objective before answering, and from using four paired figures rather than adjectives to score each half.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ How far did the First World War cause the collapse of the tsarist regime in Russia? Examine.

### Model answer

The war was the necessary converting variable acting on weaknesses it did not create. Three of those weaknesses were structural. The October Manifesto of 1905 promised a Duma, four civil liberties, universal suffrage and a legislative veto; the First Duma was dispersed by troops after ten weeks, the Second met the same fate, and the franchise was then narrowed against peasants and urban workers, Nicholas remarking that a democratic republic would be senseless and criminal. Agrarian reform was attempted and lost: redemption payments dating from 1861 had impoverished over half the rural population, and though Stolypin abolished them and two million peasants bought land by 1916, population growth of 1.5 million a year outran the scheme by 1911. Repression alienated peasants, workers and intelligentsia together, and strikes rose from over 2000 in 1912 to more than 4000 by mid-1914.

Against this, real recovery is recorded: factory inspection, a 1912 insurance scheme, 50 000 new primary schools by 1914. That recovery makes the war decisive rather than incidental. It destroyed capacity rather than adding grievance: military monopolisation of the railways left food in the countryside and bread scarce in the capital; Nicholas's assumption of supreme command in August 1915 attached defeat to him personally; and by January 1917 the aristocracy, Duma, industrialists and army had turned against him to forestall a worse revolution.

Read and Service deny that collapse was inevitable without war; Fitzpatrick doubts survival either way. The defensible verdict is that upheaval was probable and its form contingent.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from conceding the recovery evidence and then using the concession as the proof of the war's decisiveness, and from separating the probability of upheaval from its form.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Assess the view that the October Revolution was a coup rather than a mass insurrection.

### Model answer

The description is accurate about method and incomplete about cause. On method the evidence supports it. The Petrograd Soviet decided in mid-October at Lenin's urging, with Stalin and Sverdlov supporting and Trotsky planning an operation that went off without a hitch. On the night of 25-26 October Red Guards and pro-soviet troops seized telegraph offices and the railway station, surrounded the Winter Palace and arrested the ministers; Kerensky escaped. It was almost bloodless. Ulam's formulation follows: the Bolsheviks did not seize power, they picked it up, and any group of determined men could have taken a few key points and proclaimed themselves the government. Pipes attributes the outcome almost entirely to Lenin's desire for power.

Three considerations qualify this. First, the Bolsheviks held majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets by October while remaining a national minority, so the seizure came from inside an elected institution. Second, Soviet historiography records that force was needed in only sixteen of ninety-seven major centres, which is evidence about the country and not merely the capital. Third, the government removed had no mandate, had postponed the elections it promised, and had lost the armed force in its capital.

The capability claim is also weaker than it sounds: the Mensheviks and Social Revolutionaries had the organisation and refused the opportunity on doctrinal grounds, walking out of the Second Congress of Soviets. The supportable verdict is that October was a disciplined seizure of strategic points and the culmination of a wider collapse of legitimacy.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from meeting the strongest version of the case first, then limiting it with three distinct kinds of evidence, and from noticing that the walk-out undermines Ulam's capability claim.

## Question 5 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "Stalin's power in the 1930s rested on terror." Evaluate this statement.

### Model answer

Terror was necessary to Stalin's power and not sufficient to explain it. Its reality is not in doubt. Stalin instructed Yezhov on the scale of repression and personally approved execution lists, then made him the scapegoat and had him shot; alternative leaders were eliminated, government, republics, armed forces and economy violently subdued, Lenin's old Bolshevik Party the principal victim, and the army purge contributed to the disasters of 1941-2. Estimates of the cost differ because they count different things: Conquest gives about a million executions and two million camp deaths for 1937-8, official KGB figures released in the early 1990s give 700 000 executions, and Suny reaches ten to eleven million destroyed lives for the decade including famine.

Four other supports operated. Plans and purges together created a beneficiary stratum - managers, engineers, technicians, Stakhanovites, the *nomenklatura* - with delivered bread, lower prices, better housing and dachas, whose solid support and bargaining Fitzpatrick's social historians record. Provision expanded: rationing abolished in 1936, free education and healthcare, canteens, cinemas. Literacy rose from under half the population in 1917 to 94 per cent of urban and 86 per cent of rural adults by the 1939 census. And the 1936 Constitution's single-candidate elections, aimed at almost unanimous turnouts, show a regime seeking demonstrated popularity rather than indifferent to it.

The system was, on the record, over-centralised, inefficient and widely evaded - except in terror, where it was frighteningly effective. Power rested on terror's certainty and on the visible rewards of compliance.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from testing the word "rested", from handling the estimates by naming what each counts, and from using the Constitution's plebiscitary purpose as evidence about the regime's appetite for consent.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Critically examine Stalin's economic transformation of the USSR, with reference to both its achievements and its social effects.

### Model answer

The diagnosis was real. In 1929 France, not a leading industrial power, produced more coal and steel than Russia, and Stalin held that rapid heavy industrialisation was essential against an attack he expected from capitalist powers, and would enlarge the regime's industrial class. The instruments were foreign technicians, technical education at every level, the ploughing-back of surpluses, piecework and differentials, and the Stakhanovite system, named after a miner who cut 102 tons in a single shift in August 1935 with a well-organised team behind him.

Achievement and cost were produced by the same mechanism. McCauley records genuine enthusiasm and prodigious production; Nove holds that something drastic was needed and asks where the Soviet army would have been in 1942 without a Urals-Siberian metallurgical base, while listing Stalin's errors. Freeborn calls the First Plan a declaration of war by the state machine against workers and peasants, and the evidence supports him: Moscow bread and flour consumption in 1933 below half the 1900 level, real wages in 1937 at three-fifths of 1928, barracks housing half of Magnitogorsk in 1938, and an urban population up 31 million between 1926 and 1939 crammed into communal apartments.

Socially the results were divergent. Literacy rose towards 94 and 86 per cent by 1939; over ten million women entered wage work as the female share rose from 24 to 39 per cent, while abortion was criminalised; a privileged stratum enjoyed dachas and delivered bread. Agriculture never recovered: the 1952 harvest reached only three-quarters of 1940.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from the claim that the same mechanism produced both ledgers, from disaggregating the social effects by group, and from carrying the verdict past 1941 to the 1952 harvest.

## Question 7 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ To what extent was Stalinism a continuation of Leninism? Discuss with reference to institutions, policies and the scale of coercion.

### Model answer

Leninism names an ideology, a style of government and a programme, and each yields a different verdict. On ideology continuity is near-complete: the dictatorship of the proletariat guided by a disciplined party, the soviets treated as the highest form of democracy because party and soviets were mainly proletarian, and a late Lenin who thought the New Economic Policy would make permanent revolution unnecessary, moving towards socialism in one country.

On institutions the framework is demonstrably Lenin's. The Constituent Assembly, in which the Bolsheviks held 175 seats of about 700 against 370 for the Social Revolutionaries, was dispersed in January 1918 on the doctrine that a party which knew what workers wanted needed no elected parliament. The Cheka was created in December 1917. Factionalism was banned in March 1921, and about a third of the party's members were expelled that year. Stalin inherited a one-party state with political police and an expulsion mechanism.

On policy there is rupture. Lenin announced the New Economic Policy in May 1921 as lasting not less than a decade and probably more, an alternative road reaching co-operative agriculture without force; Stalin reversed it in 1928-29 and collectivised by brute force.

On scale the difference arguably becomes one of kind. Volkogonov records both as violent, Lenin in the civil war and Stalin over the kulaks and in the Great Terror; but the Terror's principal victim was Lenin's own Bolshevik Party. Stalinism grew from Leninist structures and radicalised one-party rule into a far wider terror-state.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from decomposing the term before answering, from producing four separate verdicts on four components, and from the closing observation that the Purges destroyed the party that built the framework.

---

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

⚠️ Each row is an error observed in scripts on this topic, the correction with its evidence, and the lesson that repairs it.

| # | The error | ✅ The correction | Repair |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "The Russian Revolution of 1917" | Two revolutions: February/March removed the Tsar; October/November brought the Bolsheviks to power | L1, L5, L8 |
| 2 | "The October Revolution took place in November - a contradiction" | Two calendars. Julian 25 October = Gregorian 7 November; Russia adopted the Gregorian calendar in 1918 | L1 §4 |
| 3 | "Russia was a backward country and so revolution was inevitable" | Both halves are contested. The same decade holds 50 000 new primary schools, an insurance scheme and two million land purchases | L3 |
| 4 | "Nicholas refused all reform" | He issued the October Manifesto with four specific promises, then hollowed them out | L2 |
| 5 | "The Duma was abolished" | Two were dispersed; the Third and Fourth sat full terms without power | L2 §4 |
| 6 | "Stolypin was a reactionary" / "a liberal" | Both: roughly 4000 executions in three years, and the abolition of redemption payments | L3 §1 |
| 7 | "The peasants wanted land" as an actor-claim | Attribute to identifiable groups; the peasantry was stratified and acted differently by stratum | L3, L14 |
| 8 | "Russia was starving in February 1917" | There was plenty of food; the military monopolised the trains | L4 §2 |
| 9 | "The Russian army was hopeless" | Stone records it acquitted itself reasonably well; Brusilov's 1916 offensive was an impressive success | L4 §3 |
| 10 | Omitting August 1915 | Nicholas took supreme command and thereby personalised subsequent defeat | L4 §3 |
| 11 | "The elite supported the revolution" | They turned on Nicholas to forestall a worse revolution | L4 §4 |
| 12 | "The Tsar was deposed" | He abdicated, in favour of the Grand Duke Michael, who declined | L5 §1 |
| 13 | "He abdicated in favour of his son" | His brother | L5 §1 |
| 14 | "The Bolsheviks organised February" | Lenin was in Switzerland; liberal historians record no significant party role | L5 §3 |
| 15 | "February was spontaneous" stated flatly | Divide the event: unorganised origin, decisive mutiny, elite completion | L5 §3 |
| 16 | "The Provisional Government was weak" | Dual power: office without enforcement, created by the order to obey only the soviet | L6 |
| 17 | Dating the Petrograd Soviet after the abdication | It was elected at the end of February, before it | L6 §1 |
| 18 | Omitting the June offensive | Kerensky's own idea; it collapsed army discipline and produced mass desertion | L7 §2 |
| 19 | "Lenin was a German agent" | The sealed train and the German motive are recorded; the July spy report is recorded as false | L7 §1, §3 |
| 20 | "The July Days were a failed Bolshevik coup" | Authorship disputed - Pipes against Service - and the soviet itself refused power | L7 §3 |
| 21 | Omitting Kornilov | It is the reversal that produced the Bolshevik soviet majorities of October | L7 §4 |
| 22 | "The Bolsheviks had majority support by October" | Majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets; a minority in the country | L7 §5 |
| 23 | "Lenin planned October" | Lenin pressed the decision; Trotsky made the plans | L8 §1 |
| 24 | "The Winter Palace was stormed in heavy fighting" | "Almost a bloodless coup" | L8 §2 |
| 25 | Omitting the walk-out | The Mensheviks and SRs left the Second Congress; that is what made the government single-party | L8 §4 |
| 26 | Treating the three October interpretations as opinions | They describe three different units - the country, the capital, the workplaces | L8 §5 |
| 27 | "Land was redistributed" without "without compensation" | The no-compensation clause is what foreclosed restoration | L9 §1 |
| 28 | "The Bolsheviks narrowly lost the election" | 175 of about 700 against 370 for the SRs | L9 §3 |
| 29 | Treating the dispersal as an emergency measure | The recorded justification is doctrinal, not emergency | L9 §3 |
| 30 | "Brest-Litovsk was a humiliation" and nothing more | A calculation with a stated premise - European revolution - and a stated risk | L9 §4 |
| 31 | "The Whites wanted the Tsar back" | Their general aim was a democratic government on Western lines | L10 §1 |
| 32 | Apportioning the 8 million civil-war deaths | Not apportioned in the base and not apportioned here; and never without "at least" | L10 §5 |
| 33 | Omitting the commissar grievance | "Long live the soviets and down with the commissars" shows opposition from inside the revolution | L10 §2 |
| 34 | Giving a total for the Red Terror | Lowe gives official Cheka figures and says the real toll was probably much higher | L11 §2 |
| 35 | Deciding the Lenin-intention debate confidently | Four recorded positions; the 1908 essay and the May 1919 pamphlet cut opposite ways | L11 §4 |
| 36 | "NEP restored capitalism" | Heavy industry, power, transport and banking stayed state-owned; the political monopoly was untouched | L12 §2 |
| 37 | "NEP was a temporary retreat" stated as fact | Lenin: not less than a decade and probably more; an alternative road, not a retreat | L12 §3 |
| 38 | Omitting the ban on factions | Same month as NEP; about a third of the party expelled that year | L12 §5 |
| 39 | "Stalin defeated Trotsky by ideology" | Organisation, patronage, timing and factional splits | L13 §3 |
| 40 | Dating the General Secretaryship to 1924 | April 1922 | L13 §3 |
| 41 | "Socialism in one country was Stalin's doctrine" | Recorded as Bukharin's position, which Stalin supported | L13 §4 |
| 42 | "Trotsky was exiled in 1927" | Expelled from the Party in 1927; later exiled from the USSR to Istanbul | L13 §5 |
| 43 | "Collectivisation raised grain output" | It did not, except in 1930; lower in 1934 than in 1928 | L14 §3 |
| 44 | Citing the 1937 harvest without 1936 | 97.4 follows 56.1 | L14 §4 |
| 45 | Treating "kulak" as a real social actor | All property-owning peasants were hostile, kulak or not | L14 §2 |
| 46 | Supplying a famine total other than the one Lowe gives | Over 5 million on Lowe's estimate, contested; no other figure | L14 §5 |
| 47 | "The exported grain would have prevented the deaths" | The base records both facts; it performs no such calculation | L14 §5 |
| 48 | Adopting the genocide characterisation as the session's verdict | Attribute it to the body that stated it | L14 §6 |
| 49 | Dating the comparative production table | Its year is not legible in the copy used here | L15 §2 |
| 50 | Treating Stakhanov's shift as typical | Achieved with a well-organised team | L15 §3 |
| 51 | "Industrialisation justified the methods" | The advanced owner names this as the trap; the tension is the examined thing | L15 §6 |
| 52 | Averaging the repression estimates | Four estimators, four objects, four spans | L16 §4 |
| 53 | Asserting show-trial detail | The copy used here does not legibly supply it | L16 §1 |
| 54 | "Getty defends Stalin" | His claim is about the absence of an overall plan and the role of elite anxiety | L16 §6 |
| 55 | "Self-determination was granted" and nothing more | Four declared republics were forced back during the civil war | L17 §1 |
| 56 | "Soviet policy emancipated women" | Occupational access widened; abortion was criminalised and burdens compounded | L17 §2-3 |
| 57 | An aggregate verdict on living standards | The *nomenklatura* evidence makes aggregates unsafe | L17 §5 |
| 58 | Treating the literacy record as propaganda | It is real, and its stated motive was orthodoxy | L18 §1 |
| 59 | Omitting the 1942 religious reversal | The best evidence for the instrumental character of the programme | L18 §2 |
| 60 | Summarising the 1936 Constitution's rights | The single-candidate rule is what empties them | L19 §1 |
| 61 | "Totalitarian" used as a description | It is an ideal type; score by domain | L19 §2 |
| 62 | Using public compliance as evidence of consent | The recorded double existence makes public evidence unreliable in both directions | L16 §5, L19 §3 |
| 63 | Narrating the Second World War | Another owner's subject; only the aftermath is usable | L20 |
| 64 | Reporting the Fourth Plan without its hedge | "If the official statistics are to be believed" | L20 §3 |
| 65 | Naming deported nationalities | Not legible in the copy used here | L20 §5 |
| 66 | Omitting the 1952 harvest | Three-quarters of the 1940 level - the decisive agrarian evidence | L20 §3 |
| 67 | "Stalinism was Leninism" or "was its betrayal" | Decompose into ideology, style and policy; four verdicts | L21 §1 |
| 68 | Importing Soviet foreign policy | Two external facts only: the failed European revolutions and the expectation of attack | L21 §3 |

## Drill set A - date and sequence discrimination

⚠️ For each pair, state which is which and what turns on the distinction.

1. 23 February 1917 / 25 October 1917
2. 2 March 1917 / 3 July 1917
3. April Theses / June offensive
4. Petrograd Soviet elected / Provisional Government formed
5. Constituent Assembly elected / Constituent Assembly dispersed
6. Cheka created / civil war begins
7. Brest-Litovsk / Kronstadt
8. NEP introduced / factions banned
9. Stalin becomes General Secretary / Lenin dies
10. Trotsky expelled from the Party / Bukharin removed from the Politburo
11. First Five Year Plan begins / collectivisation launched in earnest
12. The famine of 1932-3 / the harvest of 1937
13. The 1936 Constitution / the high tide of the Purges
14. The first Soviet atomic bomb / the 1946 harvest
15. The Doctors' Plot / Stalin's death

**Answers, with the point each makes.** (1) February revolution / October seizure - two revolutions, and the Julian dates. (2) Abdication / July Days - the first ends the monarchy, the second nearly ends the Bolsheviks. (3) April / June - the programme precedes the opportunity. (4) End of February / after the abdication - the soviet predates the government, which is why dual power was structural. (5) Mid-November 1917 / January 1918 - a national election held, then refused. (6) December 1917 / by April 1918 - the instrument precedes the emergency, which is the pivot of the intention debate. (7) March 1918 / March 1921 - the external war ended, then the internal settlement forced. (8) Both March 1921 - economic opening and political closure in the same month. (9) April 1922 / January 1924 - the office was held before the vacancy existed. (10) 1927 / 1929 - the oppositions were defeated one at a time. (11) 1928 / 1929 - the plan created the demand that collectivisation was to supply. (12) 1932-3 / 1937 - the catastrophe and the mechanised recovery, with 1936 between them. (13) Both 1936-38 - a franchise text and a terror, simultaneous. (14) August 1949 / 1946 - a bomb and a famine in the same plan. (15) November 1952 / 5 March 1953 - a purge interrupted.

## Drill set B - one-line corrections

⚠️ Rewrite each sentence so that it is defensible on this evidence base.

1. "The Bolsheviks won the 1917 election."
2. "Collectivisation modernised Soviet agriculture."
3. "Stalin killed twenty million people."
4. "The Whites fought to restore the Tsar."
5. "Lenin created a democracy which Stalin destroyed."
6. "The Provisional Government fell because Kerensky was incompetent."
7. "The Five Year Plans made the USSR the world's leading industrial power."
8. "The 1936 Constitution gave Soviet citizens democratic rights."
9. "Soviet women were liberated by the revolution."
10. "The Purges were caused by Stalin's paranoia."

**Model corrections.** (1) They took 175 of about 700 seats against 370 for the Social Revolutionaries, and dispersed the Assembly in January 1918. (2) It raised state procurement from 10.8 to 22.6 million tons between 1928 and 1933 without raising output, which was lower in 1934 than in 1928. (3) Estimates differ because they count different things, from 700 000 executions in the KGB figures for 1937-8 to Suny's ten to eleven million destroyed lives for the decade including famine. (4) Their general aim was a democratic government on Western lines; the coalition included Social Revolutionaries and Mensheviks. (5) Lenin created a one-party coercive framework - the dispersal of January 1918, the Cheka, the ban on factions - which Stalin radicalised into a far wider terror-state. (6) It fell because it held office while the Petrograd Soviet held the army's obedience; the June offensive, Kerensky's own idea, made the collapse quick. (7) They moved it decisively past France and Britain on several measures while leaving it far behind the United States, on a table whose year is not legible in this copy. (8) It listed rights and franchise while providing one candidate per constituency and a legislature sitting about two weeks a year, so very few people took it seriously. (9) Over ten million women entered wage work and the female share rose from 24 to 39 per cent, while abortion was criminalised except to save the mother's life and domestic burdens compounded. (10) They followed a sharp fall in the government's popularity caused by collectivisation and the First Plan, and growing party opposition; psychology is contested, with Khrushchev and Medvedev on opposite sides.

## Drill set C - the seven demands, sorted by stem

⚠️ For each stem, name the demand family, the two evidence banks and the concession.

| Stem | Family | Banks | Concession |
|---|---|---|---|
| "Why did the Romanov dynasty fall?" | Collapse | 1905 settlement; war transmission | The recovery indicators |
| "Was October a coup?" | Two revolutions | October mechanics; legitimacy collapse | The mechanics favour "coup" |
| "How did the Bolsheviks survive to 1924?" | Consolidation | Four instruments; civil-war reasons | The programme's genuine appeal |
| "Was collectivisation necessary?" | Transformation | Harvest and procurement series; the 1927-28 grain problem | The grain problem was real |
| "Did Stalin modernise Russia?" | Transformation | Production comparison; consumption and wages | The literacy and industrial record |
| "Was the USSR totalitarian?" | System | Terror machinery; evasion and inefficiency | The aims were totalitarian |
| "What were the effects on society?" | Society | Nationalities and gender; education, religion, culture | Provision expanded as consumption fell |

## Map 1 - The complete causal architecture, 1905 to 1953

```text

   1906  THE CONCESSION HOLLOWED OUT

              |
   1914  WAR: THE CONVERTING VARIABLE
   -17   trains -> bread; Aug 1915 command -> personalised defeat;
         Jan 1917 elite defection to prevent a worse revolution
              |

   1917  -> Duma and generals -> abdication -> Michael declines
              |
         DUAL POWER: government with office | soviet with the soldiers
              |
   Apr-  FOUR EPISODES: April Theses -> June offensive -> July Days
   Sep   -> Kornilov -> Bolshevik majorities in two city soviets
              |
   Oct   SEIZURE: telegraph, station, palace, arrests; walk-out of the
   1917  other socialist parties -> single-party Sovnarkom
              |

   -18   | Brest-Litovsk
              |

   -21   reds win on leadership, the central core, white disunity, and
         the patriotic dividend of intervention

              |
   Mar   KRONSTADT -> NEP (economic opening)
   1921  + BAN ON FACTIONS (political closure), same month
              |
```

## Map 2 - February and October, on five axes

| Axis | February/March 1917 | October/November 1917 |
|---|---|---|
| Trigger | ✅ Bread riots on 23 February, joined by strikers from a nearby armaments factory | ✅ The Petrograd Soviet's mid-October decision to seize power |
| Decisive actors | ✅ Crowds, strikers, the mutinying garrison; then the Duma and senior generals | ✅ Red Guards and pro-soviet troops on the night of 25-26 October |
| Planning | ⚠️ None identifiable; Chamberlin's "leaderless, spontaneous, anonymous" | ✅ Trotsky's, and it "went off without a hitch" |
| Duration and violence | ✅ Roughly eight days; 40 killed in the first exchange alone | ✅ One night; "almost a bloodless coup" |
| Outcome | ✅ Abdication on 2 March; Michael declines; the monarchy ends | ✅ Ministers arrested; Sovnarkom formed after the other socialist parties walk out |

## Map 3 - The Stalinist balance sheet, with the causal relation marked

| Domain | ✅ Achievement | ✅ Cost | ⚠️ Relation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Heavy industry | Decisive movement past France and Britain on several measures; McCauley's prodigious achievements; Nove's metallurgical base | Poor quality from speed; damaged machinery; wrecking charges and camp sentences | Constitutive: the same targets produced both |
| Agriculture | Procurement doubled; exports multiplied; state control of the countryside achieved | Output flat or falling to 1934; livestock recovering only in 1953; famine 1932-3; the 1952 harvest at three-quarters of 1940 | Constitutive: control was bought by dispossession |
| Labour and consumption | Real wages and provision improving from the mid-1930s; rationing abolished 1936 | 1933 consumption below half the 1900 level; 1937 real wages at three-fifths of 1928; barracks and communal apartments | Substitution: collective provision replaced private consumption |
| Education | Literacy from under half to 94 and 86 per cent; 199 000 schools by 1940 | Explicitly instrumental; traditional discipline restored | Compatible: the achievement served the plan |
| Security and expertise | Complete political control; no challenge to 1953 | Old Bolshevik Party destroyed; best brains lost; army command decapitated | Contradictory: the control cost the capability |
| War-readiness | The industrial base mattered in 1941-45 | The army purge contributed to the disasters of 1941-2; agriculture stayed fragile | Offsetting: entries on both sides, no calculable net |

## Map 5 - The five sensitive-evidence episodes and the rule for each

| Episode | ✅ What the sources record | ⚠️ Rule applied |
|---|---|---|
| The Red Terror, 1918-19 | Official Cheka figures: 245 uprisings suppressed in 1918 and 99 in early 1919; over 3000 peasants killed and 6300 executed; over 3000 more executions in 1919; real toll probably much higher | Report as a perpetrator-derived floor; state no total |
| Civil-war deaths | At least 8 million, including pogroms, starvation and epidemics, against 1.7 million Russian war deaths | Preserve "at least"; never apportion between the sides |
| The famine of 1932-3 | Mainly rural, especially in Ukraine; 1.75 million tons exported in the same period; over 5 million peasant deaths on Lowe's estimate; totals contested | Attribute the estimate; supply no regional or revised figure; do not convert the export figure into a counterfactual |
| The Purges | Four estimates counting four different objects over three different spans | Name estimator, object and span; never average |
| Wartime deportations and the postwar camps | Deaths in transit and after abandonment recorded; about 300 000 deported from western Ukraine; camp population more than doubled to about 2.5 million; 2.8 million returning prisoners arrested | Name no deported nationality, because the copy used here does not legibly supply them; state no deportation death toll |

## Map 6 - The six recurring mechanisms, which is what makes this a system rather than a chronicle

| Mechanism | Where it appears |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ **A concession that becomes a standard** | The October Manifesto of 1905 measured against the regime's own record; the 1936 Constitution supplying a rights vocabulary |
| ⚠️ **An instrument created before the emergency it is said to answer** | The Cheka in December 1917; the ban on factions in March 1921 before there was one man to use it |
| ⚠️ **Doctrine as a repertoire, requirement as the selector** | Nove on war communism; piecework inside the plans; the 1942 religious accommodation; the mid-1930s family laws |
| ⚠️ **External hostility converted into domestic legitimacy** | Foreign intervention in the civil war; the expectation of capitalist attack justifying the plans; patriotic cinema in 1938 |
| ⚠️ **Selection by category rather than by act** | "Bourgeois" in 1918; "kulak" from 1929; "former people" in 1936; returning prisoners after 1945 |
| ⚠️ **Control achieved at the cost of the capability it was meant to secure** | Collectivisation removing the best producers; the Purges removing the expertise and the army command |

## Map 7 - The four thesis templates and their evidence banks

| Template | Evidence bank to deploy behind it |
|---|---|
| **Collapse** | The four Manifesto promises against their record; redemption payments and the land-reform failure; the strike series; the transport failure; August 1915; January 1917 |
| **Two revolutions** | Map 2's five axes in full |
| **Lenin and Stalin** | The dispersal justification; the Cheka's date; the ban on factions; NEP's declared duration; the 1928-29 reversal; the Purges' destruction of the old party |
| **Stalinism** | Map 3's six domains, with the relation column as the argument |
