# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ Compressed recall for the whole topic. Headings follow this topic's own structure rather than a generic template, and nothing here repeats the teaching prose above.

## Framing, calendar and vocabulary

- One title, three subjects: a collapse to March 1917; a seizure and consolidation to 1924; a state-built transformation, 1928-53. Decide which the stem names before selecting evidence.
- Julian calendar, thirteen days behind, to 1918: 23 February = 8 March; 25 October = 7 November.
- Soviet = an elected council of workers' and soldiers' representatives. Bolshevik/Menshevik = the 1903 split over the *Iskra* editorial board; Menshevik = minority. On strict Marxist criteria the Mensheviks were the orthodox party. Social Revolutionaries = non-Marxist, agrarian, collectively operating peasant communities.
- Dual power = office without enforcement against enforcement without responsibility.
- Syllabus position: the political-philosophies-and-effect-on-society clause. This topic owns the historical record of communism in one state, not the doctrine.

## The late-imperial state and the settlement of 1905

- Nicholas II, Tsar 1894-1917, autocrat: not responsible to a parliament.
- 1905 survived because opponents were divided, there was no central leadership, most of the army stayed loyal, and the October Manifesto was issued.
- Manifesto: an elected Duma; freedom of conscience, speech, assembly and association; universal suffrage for Duma elections; no law without Duma approval.
- Unaddressed: industrial conditions and pay; cancellation of redemption payments (from the emancipation of 1861; they had reduced over half the rural population to dire poverty); amnesty for political prisoners.
- First Duma 1906: rigged franchise; demanded confiscation of large estates, a democratic franchise, ministerial approval, the right to strike, abolition of the death penalty; dispersed by troops after ten weeks. Second Duma 1907 the same; franchise then narrowed against peasants and urban workers. Third 1907-12 and Fourth 1912-17: conservative, long-lived, powerless.
- Nicholas, recorded: a democratic republic "would be senseless and criminal".

## 1906-14: the two ledgers

- Strengths: Stolypin PM 1906-11; some 4000 executions in three years; redemption payments abolished; about 2 million peasants bought land by 1916 and 3.5 million emigrated to Siberia; a kulak class created as an intended conservative prop; factory inspection; 1912 sickness and accident insurance; 1908 universal-education programme and 50 000 extra primary schools by 1914; revolutionary parties short of money, split, led from exile.
- Weaknesses: land reform failing by 1911 against population growth of 1.5 million a year; Stolypin assassinated 1911; Lena goldfields April 1912 with 270 miners shot, then over 2000 strikes in 1912, 2400 in 1913, over 4000 in the first seven months of 1914; repression alienating peasants, workers and intelligentsia together; party revival from 1912 with *Pravda*; the dynasty discredited by Stolypin's murder and by Rasputin, and the Duma's 1912 request ignored.
- July 1914: a Bolshevik-organised general strike in the capital, ending on 15 July with the government still controlling army and police.
- Inevitability: Soviet view (war delayed it); Kennan and Haimson; Fitzpatrick's "so vulnerable to any kind of jolt"; against them Read (by no means inevitable) and Service ("general brittleness", "vulnerable plant", not doomed to root-and-branch revolution).

## War and the destruction of state capacity, 1914-17

- Recorded mechanism: war failures made troops and police mutiny, leaving nobody to defend the autocracy.
- Incompetent and corrupt organisation; equipment shortages; arms slow to the front; plenty of food in the country but the military monopolised the trains, so bread was scarce and very expensive.
- Stone: the army acquitted itself reasonably well; Brusilov's 1916 offensive an impressive success.
- August 1915: Nicholas takes supreme command; blunders throw away Brusilov's gains; he draws the blame for later defeats and the death rate.
- January 1917: aristocracy, Duma, industrialists and army turning against him to forestall a worse revolution. Krimov to Duma members, end of 1916: "We would welcome the news of a coup d'etat ... Clearly there is no other way."

## February/March 1917

- 23 February: bread riots joined by strikers from a nearby armaments factory; order to use force; 40 killed; troops refuse; the whole Petrograd garrison mutinies; public buildings, jails, police stations and arsenals seized.
- Duma advises a constitutional monarchy; Nicholas refuses and sends more troops; generals tell him only renunciation can save the monarchy.
- 2 March: abdication in the imperial train near Pskov in favour of the Grand Duke Michael, who refuses; the monarchy ends.
- Readings: Katkov (elite conspiracy decisive); Chamberlin 1935 ("one of the most leaderless, spontaneous, anonymous revolutions of all time"); Soviet (from below but organised); Read, Koenker and Smith (economic motivation; Smith's "outburst of desperation").
- Expectation in March 1917: a democratic republic with an elected parliament. Duma forms a mainly liberal provisional government under Prince G. Lvov; Kerensky from July.

## Dual power and the failure of the Provisional Government

- The Petrograd Soviet was elected at the end of February, before the abdication; similar bodies in Moscow and all provincial cities.
- It ordered all soldiers to obey only the soviet: in the last resort the government could not rely on the army.
- Five failures, all expressions of that: the war continued and the June offensive (Kerensky's idea) collapsed morale and discipline with hundreds of thousands deserting; Constituent Assembly elections delayed on a wartime argument; land reform promised and not delivered while peasants seized land; economic chaos with inflation, bread prices, lagging wages and fuel and raw-material shortages; and a Bolshevik programme of separate peace, land, workers' control and cheaper food built from what the government could not do.

## April to September 1917

- April: political amnesty; Lenin returns from Switzerland in a sealed German train, the Germans hoping for chaos; April Theses - cease supporting the government, all power to the soviets, withdraw from the war.
- 3 July: demonstration at the Tauride Palace; the soviet refuses power; about 400 killed; the false German-spy report; Lenin flees to Finland; Lvov resigns; Kerensky succeeds. Authorship disputed: Pipes (planned) against Service ("testing the waters"); probably spontaneous in origin.
- August: Kornilov moves on Petrograd; his soldiers mutiny; Kerensky orders his arrest; opinion swings to the only party openly proposing a separate peace.
- By October: Bolshevik majorities in the Petrograd and Moscow soviets; a minority in the country. Trotsky, a Bolshevik only since July, chairs the Petrograd Soviet.

## October/November 1917 and its interpretation

- Mid-October decision by the Petrograd Soviet, urged by Lenin, supported by Stalin and Sverdlov; Trotsky planned it and it "went off without a hitch".
- Night of 25-26 October: telegraph offices, railway station, the Winter Palace surrounded, ministers arrested except Kerensky. "Almost a bloodless coup."
- Mensheviks and SRs walk out of the Second Congress of Soviets; Sovnarkom formed with Lenin as chairman - a single-party government produced by a walk-out, not an exclusion.
- Three readings: Soviet (mass movement; force needed in only 16 of 97 major centres; legitimacy); traditional liberal (Ulam's "they picked it up"; Pipes on Lenin's desire for power); libertarian (Berkman on the factory committees).
- Composite verdict: a disciplined seizure of strategic points **and** the culmination of a collapse of legitimacy around the Provisional Government.

## Consolidation, 1917-21

- Decrees: land nationalised without compensation including crown and church estates; workers' control over managers; the eight-hour day; self-determination for every national group; banks, large factories and mines nationalised; tsarist and Provisional debts cancelled; Left SRs admitted as junior partners for their rural support.
- The Cheka under Felix Dzierzynski, from December 1917; the right claimed to close hostile newspapers.
- Constituent Assembly, elected mid-November: Bolsheviks 175 of about 700; SRs 370 (leader Viktor Chernov); nationality groups 80; Left SRs 40; Kadets 17; Mensheviks 15. Dispersed by Red Guards, January 1918, on the doctrine that a party knowing what workers want needs no elected parliament.
- Brest-Litovsk, March 1918: Poland, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Ukraine, Georgia and Finland lost - a third of the farming land, a third of the population, two-thirds of the coalmines, half the heavy industry. Left SRs walk out of Sovnarkom. Lenin: sacrifice space to gain time, expecting revolution to spread.
- Red Terror: Cheka suppressed 245 peasant uprisings in 1918 and 99 in early 1919; official figures of over 3000 peasants killed and 6300 executed, over 3000 more executions in 1919, the real toll probably much higher; selection by the "bourgeois" category - landowners, priests, businessmen, employers, officers, professionals - as "enemies of the people". July 1918: Lenin ordered the killing of the imperial family at Ekaterinburg; graves found after the Soviet collapse; DNA analysis 1992.
- Civil war: Whites a mixed bag of SRs, Mensheviks and ex-tsarist officers aiming at a democratic government on Western lines, not restoration; the commissar grievance and "Long live the soviets and down with the commissars"; Kolchak in Siberia, Denikin in the Caucasus, the Czechoslovak Legion of about 40 000 on the Trans-Siberian Railway near Omsk; British, American and French contingents from 1918 aiming to link with Kolchak, Japanese at Vladivostok.
- Why the Reds won: firmer leadership against divided Whites; control of the central core; White disunity of aim; foreign intervention allowing the Bolsheviks to appear as defenders of Russia.
- Cost: at least 8 million dead against 1.7 million Russian First World War deaths; the rouble at one per cent of its October 1917 value.
- The intention debate: forced (Soviet, Marxist); intended (Pipes - the December 1917 Cheka, the 1908 essay on "excessive generosity", "how can you make a revolution without executions?"); brutalised (Tucker, with Lenin's May 1919 pamphlet on curing backwardness by education); inherited (Read: the tsarist tradition, Stolypin's 4000) and integral (Ulam: Lenin welcomed the civil war). Economic parallel: Nove's "playing it by ear".

## NEP and the political settlement of March 1921

- Cause: peasants producing only for their own needs; droughts of 1920-1; industry at a standstill; the Kronstadt naval mutiny, suppressed by Trotsky sending troops across the ice.
- Content: surplus retained after a proportional tax; private trade restored; small industry and trade privatised; old managers, bonuses and piece-rates; foreign investment encouraged. Retained: heavy industry - coal, iron and steel - power, transport and banking.
- Lenin, May 1921: "not less than a decade and probably more"; not a retreat but an alternative road, ending in co-operative agriculture reached by education rather than force. Medvedev: genuine intentions; twenty more years would have produced a very different USSR. Kamenev and Zinoviev opposed NEP as encouraging kulaks.
- Results: production near 1913 levels in most commodities; electrification; by late 1927 the ordinary Russian probably better off than at any time since 1914 - eight-hour day, two weeks' paid holiday, sick and unemployment pay, healthcare, peasant standards above 1913 - against higher unemployment and continuing food shortages.
- Same month: factionalism banned, with expulsion for persistence; about a third of the party purged during 1921 with Cheka help, many more resigning in opposition to NEP; trade unions subordinated; the Politburo acting as the government; the dictatorship of the proletariat nowhere in evidence and no state withering away.

## The succession, 1922-29

- Lenin: stroke May 1922, two more, death January 1924 aged 53, no succession plan.
- Field: Trotsky (orator, intellectual, organiser of the Red Armies); Kamenev (Moscow); Zinoviev (Leningrad and the Comintern); Bukharin (rising intellectual); Stalin (Secretary-General from April 1922, Politburo member).
- Judgements: Trotsky - "the party's most eminent mediocrity"; Sukhanov - "a vague, grey blur"; Lenin's will - too crude, remove him from the job. Ignored.
- Four mechanisms: Trotsky's brilliance breeding the triumvirate against him; underestimation; the appointment power running from local secretaries through conference delegates to the congresses that elected the Central Committee and Politburo, giving packed bodies by 1928; and doctrinal indeterminacy, since Marx had not described the organisation of socialist society and NEP had departed from socialist principles.
- Doctrines: Bukharin's socialism in one country (consolidation, prosperous peasantry, gradual industrialisation) against Trotsky's permanent revolution (foreign revolution first, then western help); Kamenev and Zinoviev backing Bukharin as a pretext against Trotsky; then the split over continuing NEP against rapid industrialisation at the peasants' expense.
- Sequence: Stalin backs Bukharin twice; Trotsky isolated and off the Commissariat by end-1924; Trotsky, Kamenev and Zinoviev expelled 1927, Trotsky later exiled to Istanbul; Stalin reverses NEP in 1928 on the kulak argument; Bukharin removed from the Politburo 1929. Critics call the turn cynical; the account allows a genuine decision since NEP was faltering on food; Service records the policies as popular with most party members.

## Collectivisation and the famine

- Two reasons: small farms inefficient and unable to feed the industrial cities, with large mechanised farms promising more grain "or so the theory went"; and elimination of the kulaks, the real reason probably political. Stalin: "We must smash the kulaks so hard that they will never rise to their feet again."
- Execution from 1929 by brute force; landless labourers collectivised easily; all property-owning peasants hostile, kulak or not; party detachments urging poorer peasants to seize cattle and machinery; cattle slaughtered and crops burned; refusers arrested, sent to camps or shot; quotas seized by local officials; over 90 per cent of farmland collectivised by 1937.
- Four reasons output failed: the best producers excluded and urban organisers ignorant of agriculture; demoralisation, departures, arrests and deportations; too few tractors after the horses were slaughtered; private plots worked harder than the *kolkhoz*.
- Series - harvest (m tons): 1913 80.1; 1928 73.3; 1929 71.7; 1930 83.5; 1931 69.5; 1932 69.6; 1933 68.4; 1934 67.6; 1936 56.1; 1937 97.4. State procurement: 1928 10.8 to 1933 22.6. Exports: 1927-8 0.029; 1930 4.76; 1931 5.06; 1932 1.73; 1933 1.69. Livestock: cattle 70.5 to 38.4; pigs 26.0 to 11.6; sheep and goats 146.7 to 50.2; recovery to the 1928 level only in 1953.
- Famine 1932-3, mainly rural, especially in Ukraine; 1.75 million tons exported in the same period; over 5 million peasant deaths on Lowe's estimate, with totals contested and no single figure asserted here. The claim that Stalin welcomed the famine is reported as a claim made by some historians; the figure attached to kulaks removed or executed appears in the account without an identified estimator and is not adopted.
- Consequence recorded: the state could obtain grain without haggling, and the risk of a bad harvest shifted from the cities to the countryside.
- One current linkage: the Welsh Government's written statement of 22 November 2025, marking the annual fourth-Saturday remembrance, attributing a genocide characterisation to Senedd Cymru, honouring Gareth Jones's 1933 reporting, and stating no total. The characterisation is attributed, not adopted.

## The Five-Year Plans

- Diagnosis: in 1929 France produced more coal and steel than Russia; Germany, Britain and especially the USA streets ahead. Stalin's reasoning: heavy industry against an expected capitalist attack, and more industrial workers to secure the state.
- Comparative table held: USSR 14.9 pig-iron, 18.4 steel, 164.6 coal, 39.6 electricity; USA 31.9, 47.2, 395.0, 115.9; Britain 6.7, 10.3, 227.0, 30.7; Germany 18.3, 22.7, 186.0, 55.2; France 6.0, 16.1, 45.5, 19.3. Year not legible in the copy used here; not asserted.
- Instruments: foreign technicians; education in colleges, universities and factory schools; ploughing-back of surpluses; piecework and differentials; Stakhanovites, after Stakhanov's 102 tons in one shift in August 1935 with a well-organised team, against 10 tons for highly efficient Ruhr miners.
- Costs: severe punishment for bad workmanship; saboteur and wrecker charges with camp sentences; primitive housing; consumer shortages; poor quality from speed; improvement by the mid-1930s in medical care, education and paid holidays.
- Verdicts: Freeborn (a declaration of war by the state machine, with greater exploitation than under capitalism); McCauley (genuine enthusiasm, prodigious achievements, impossible targets galvanising action); Nove (something drastic was needed; a great industry built; the Urals-Siberian base and 1942; but vast errors, too far too fast, unnecessary brutality, and all criticism treated as subversion).

## The Purges

- Background: popularity collapsed by summer 1930 through collectivisation and First Plan hardship; growing party opposition; the Ryutin Platform circulated. The pages carrying the narrative from there through the show trials are illegible in the copy used here.
- Standard chronology: Kirov assassinated 1 December 1934; Moscow show trials 19-24 August 1936, 23-30 January 1937 and 2-13 March 1938; Tukhachevsky and seven other commanders executed 11 June 1937; NKVD Order No. 00447 of 30 July 1937; Yezhov replaced by Beria 25 November 1938.
- Machinery: Stalin gave Yezhov orders on scale and personally approved execution lists, then made him the scapegoat and had him shot; Politburo members co-signed warrants; executants at every level were willing; local party bosses initiated their own terrors.
- Results: alternative leaders eliminated; the masses terrorised; government, republics, armed forces and economy subdued; rule with Molotov, Kaganovich, Mikoyan, Zhdanov, Voroshilov, Bulganin, Beria, Malenkov and Khrushchev to 1953.
- Consequences: the human cost; Lenin's old Bolshevik Party the main victim; the loss of many of the best brains; the army purge contributing to the disasters of 1941-2.
- Estimates, never averaged: Conquest for 1937-8 (about 7 million arrests, about a million executions, about 2 million camp deaths, no more than 10 per cent of camp inmates surviving); the official KGB figures released in the early 1990s for the same span (700 000 executions; 3.6 million in camps and prisons at the end of the decade); Suny (ten to eleven million destroyed lives for the decade, including 4-5 million famine deaths); Yakovlev (sixty to seventy million victims of communism after 1917). Many vital records deliberately destroyed.
- Readings: Volkogonov (an evil mind lacking moral sense; the archival evidence of personal approval); Getty (political infighting; the obsessive fears of all the leaders; no overall plan; a unifying cult built to protect position); Fitzpatrick (continuing revolution; abnormal circumstances). Khrushchev ("sickly suspicious") against Medvedev (perfectly sane, coolly ruthless, vanity and lust for power).

## Nationality and federalism

- 1914 inheritance from Poland and Finland through the Caucasus and Central Asia to the Baltic states; Finland independent November 1917; Poland and the Baltic republics by Brest-Litovsk; Ukraine, Georgia, Armenia and Azerbaijan declared independence March 1918 and were forced back during the civil war; six Soviet republics by 1925.
- 47 per cent of the USSR's population non-Russian. Two-handed approach: cultures and languages encouraged with some republican independence, more liberal than tsarist Russification; Moscow deciding all important matters with force available. It worked until 1985.
- 1932: the Ukrainian party purged as "bourgeois nationalist deviationists" for admitting collectivisation had failed; similar campaigns in Belorussia, Transcaucasia and Central Asia; 1951: Georgian leaders removed and shot.

## Women, family and everyday life

- Over 10 million women entered wage work in the 1930s; the female share rose from 24 to 39 per cent; about two-thirds of light industry by 1940; also construction, lumbering and machine-building. Context: the disappearance of men through collectivisation, famine and the Purges; high desertion and divorce.
- Nursery places doubled 1929-30; mid-1930s laws criminalised abortion except to save the mother's life, allowed maternity leave up to sixteen weeks and gave subsidies; burdens compounded for working-class and peasant women. Wives' Movement from 1936 - culturedness and home comfort, then driving, shooting and flying as war approached.
- Consumption: 1933 bread and flour for a married Moscow worker less than half the 1900 level; 1937 real wages about three-fifths of 1928. Urban population up 31 million between 1926 and 1939; communal apartments with shared kitchens and people in corridors; Magnitogorsk half barracks in 1938; poor sanitation outside Moscow.
- Provision: rationing abolished 1936; canteens and free work clothes; free education and healthcare; close on 30 000 cinemas; Gorky Park; theatres and libraries. Privileged strata - party members, *nomenklatura*, intelligentsia, engineers, experts, Stakhanovites - with delivered bread, lower prices, better housing and *dachas*; a "them and us" attitude.

## Education, religion and culture

- Literacy: under half in 1917; enrolment order of January 1930; pupils from 14 to about 20 million, 1929-31; 199 000 schools by 1940; 1939 census 94 per cent urban and 86 per cent rural for ages 9-49; 99 and 98 per cent by 1959. Motive explicitly orthodoxy; exams, punishments and teacher authority restored; more mathematics and science.
- Religion: working priests from about 60 000 in 1925 to under 6000 by 1941; one church building in forty in use by 1941; Muslim and Jewish leaders also killed; intensification linked to clerical opposition to collectivisation; 1942 reconciliation with Christians, Jews and Muslims, places of worship reopened and credited with maintaining morale.
- Literature: Cultural Revolution 1928-31; RAPP against the AUW; AUW dissolved, AUSW formed excluding about half, publication restricted to union members; both disbanded and replaced by the Union of Soviet Writers under Gorky; Zhdanov 1934 on "the ideological remoulding and re-education of the toiling people". Ostrovsky and Sholokov; five-year-plan novels. Mandelstam died in a camp; Ginsburg served eighteen years; Akhmatova and Pasternak withheld work; *Dr Zhivago* and *The Master and Margarita* published after Stalin's death; at least 600 writers announced as having perished; Meyerhold's theatre closed 1938, and Meyerhold arrested, tortured and shot. Nineteenth-century classics reinstated as "revolutionary democrats".
- Art and music: abstract art rejected; photographic style; busts of Lenin and Stalin; RAPM against modernism and jazz, relaxed by the mid-1930s. Prokofiev returned 1933. Shostakovich: *The Nose* banned 1930; *Lady Macbeth of Mtsensk* successful in 1934 with over 80 Leningrad and over 90 Moscow performances; Stalin walked out in January 1936, the *Pravda* attack called it "a cacophony, crude and vulgar", and Gorky's intercession probably saved him; the Fifth Symphony of 1937 restored him.
- Cinema: "intelligible to the millions"; Shumyatsky appointed 1930, arrested and shot 1938; over 300 films 1933-40; cinemas from about 7000 to around 30 000; Eisenstein's *Alexander Nevsky*, 1938.
- *Kul'turnost'*: hygiene, sheets, cutlery, sobriety, no wife- or child-beating; Kotkin's "speak Bolshevik"; over 13 000 new bread shops from late 1934 with politeness lessons and wrapped loaves.

## Constitution and the totalitarian debate

- 1936: the USSR as a socialist state of workers and peasants; the franchise extended to "former people"; secret ballot; a Supreme Soviet; four-yearly elections; rights of speech, thought, press, religion, employment, assembly and demonstration.
- Emptied by: a two-week annual session; a Praesidium and a Council of People's Commissars with Stalin as secretary; one candidate per constituency, aiming at nearly 100 per cent to show the government's policies were popular; and unstated real power in the Politburo and its general secretary. Very few took it seriously.
- Totalitarian ideal type: dictatorial one-party rule controlling all activities, indoctrinating and mobilising, using mental and physical terror. Ulam and Conquest for; Fitzpatrick's social revisionism (beneficiaries, initiatives from below, bargaining); Getty (Stalin's role exaggerated; the system inefficient and chaotic); mutual accusations of whitewashing and Cold War prejudice.
- Balanced conclusion: Stalin's policy preferences carried out after 1928; opinion not wholly ignored; totalitarian aims with unsuccessful practice; evasion by peasants and workers; counter-productive tightening; central-regional tension; the system over-centralised, disorganised, inefficient, corrupt, sluggish and unresponsive, yet extremely efficient at terror. Service's "listening state"; the double existence.

## 1945-53

- Cost: 6.2 million military dead, 15 million wounded, 4.4 million captured or missing, about 17 million civilian deaths, total not far short of 25 million; 25 million homeless; the modernisation programme restarted in the west. Stalin read victory as vindication.
- No relaxation: peasants taking land back and workers protesting; armed resistance in the Baltic states and western Ukraine crushed, about 300 000 deported from western Ukraine; the camp population more than doubled to about 2.5 million; 2.8 million returning prisoners arrested, only about a third allowed home; about 3 million who had been in Allied hands sent to camps; a recorded motive of cheap labour for coalmines and other projects.
- Fourth Five Year Plan 1946-50 restored industrial production to 1940 levels "if the official statistics are to be believed"; the first Soviet atomic bomb exploded in Kazakhstan, August 1949.
- Agriculture: the 1946 harvest below 1945 with famine, starvation and reports of cannibalism; peasants leaving the collectives; the 1952 grain harvest only three-quarters of 1940. Nove: a country capable of making an atomic bomb could not supply its citizens with eggs.
- From August 1946 Zhdanov's campaign against the intelligentsia; hundreds of writers expelled; leading composers disgraced and banned; Zhdanov died August 1948; the Leningrad party organisation arrested, convicted of plotting to seize power and executed.
- November 1952: the Doctors' Plot, thirteen Moscow doctors arrested, six of them Jewish, with an outburst of anti-Semitism; evidence of a further purge in preparation with Molotov, Mikoyan and Beria on the list; Stalin died of a brain haemorrhage on 5 March 1953.

## Judgement, boundaries and answer architecture

- Leninism decomposes into ideology, style and policy. Ideology: near-complete continuity, strengthened by the late Lenin's move towards socialism in one country. Style: continuity of kind, rupture of degree - Volkogonov on both. Policy: rupture at 1928-29. Apparatus: Pavlova locates its development under Stalin, though her argument breaks off in the copy used here. Verdict: Leninist structures radicalised into a far wider terror-state.
- Licensed counterfactuals: no war; an earlier peace or election; Lenin living; a different succession. Unlicensed: "no terror without Stalin."
- Foreign policy usable here: only the failure of the expected European revolutions, and the expectation of capitalist attack. Everything else belongs to the international-relations owner.
- Six handovers: the war's course; interwar diplomacy; the fascist regimes; the Second World War; the Cold War; communism as doctrine.
- Procedure: subject, directive, two pieces of evidence, the concession, the one-sentence graded verdict. Templates: collapse; two revolutions; Lenin and Stalin; Stalinism. Structures: 10 marks in 150 words, 15 and 20 marks both in 250, so a 20-mark answer is denser, not longer, and the added dimension is almost always the social one.

the strengths and weaknesses of 1906-14 with Stolypin's executions and land purchases, the Siberian migration, the factory inspectors, the 1912 insurance scheme, the education programme and the school figure, the failure of land reform against population growth, Stolypin's assassination, the Lena shootings and the strike series, the repression and its three alienated groups, the party revival and *Pravda*, and the royal scandals; the inevitability verdict with Kennan, Haimson, Fitzpatrick, Read, Service and the Soviet position; the war failures with Stone, Brusilov, the August 1915 appointment, the January 1917 elite turn and the Krimov statement; the October narrative with the mid-October decision, Trotsky's planning, the four objectives and the walk-out; the coup-or-insurrection debate with the sixteen-of-ninety-seven figure, Ulam's and Pipes's positions and Berkman's libertarian statement; the first decrees, the Left SR concession, the Cheka and Dzierzynski; the Constituent Assembly result and its dispersal with Lenin's justification; Brest-Litovsk with its territorial and proportional losses; the violence debate with Pipes's Cheka argument, the 1908 essay and the execution remark; the Red Terror with the Cheka figures, the "bourgeois" category and the Ekaterinburg killings; the civil war with the Whites' composition and aim, the commissar grievance and the two slogans, Kolchak, Denikin, the Czechoslovak Legion, the intervening contingents and the Baltic independence; the death toll and the rouble figure; the brutalisation debate with Tucker, Read, Ulam and Nove; NEP with Kronstadt, the contents, Lenin's defence and quoted formulations, Medvedev's reading, the results and the 1927 finding; the ban on factions, the 1921 purge, the trade unions and the Politburo; Lenin's strokes, death and cult, with Taylor's and Lewin's positions; Stalin's background, office and the recorded judgements of Trotsky, Sukhanov and Lenin's will; the four mechanisms of his rise, the two doctrines, the sequence of defeats and Service's finding on party opinion; the economic diagnosis with the 1929 comparison, the comparative production table, the instruments, Stakhanov, the drawbacks, Freeborn's judgement, and the McCauley and Nove verdicts; collectivisation with its two reasons, the quoted intention, the execution, the four reasons for the output failure, the four statistical series and the famine passage; the political background with the Ryutin Platform; the Purge interpretations with Getty, Fitzpatrick and Volkogonov, the scapegoating of Yezhov, the supporting clique, the four consequences and the four estimates; the 1936 Constitution with its text and its four emptying devices; 2. **Period control on the book.** Only those two chapters were used. Nothing from the book's chapters on the First World War, the peace settlements, interwar diplomacy, the fascist regimes, the Second World War, the Cold War, the post-1953 Soviet Union or the world economy appears anywhere in this session, because all of it belongs to other owners.

3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook, monograph or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded as an open gap rather than filled from outside.

4. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above, from the canonical owners, or - in the single current linkage of Lesson 14 - from the official page identified in the live-source table. Participant quotations are confined to Nicholas II on a democratic republic, General Krimov on a coup d'etat, Stalin on smashing the kulaks and on life becoming more joyous, Lenin on the duration of NEP and on executions, Trotsky and Sukhanov on Stalin, and Lenin's will on the General Secretaryship. Historians are quoted only where the core account itself quotes them - Chamberlin, Smith, Ulam, Berkman, Fitzpatrick, Service, Read, Nove, McCauley, Freeborn, Getty, Volkogonov, Medvedev, Khrushchev, Taylor, Kotkin and Yakovlev - and each is reproduced as the account records it.

5. **Extraction-damage control.** Four defects in the copy held here are declared rather than worked around. The pages covering the narrative from the Ryutin Platform through the 1934 assassination, the show trials and the army purge are blank or illegible, so **no detail of any trial, charge, confession or investigation is asserted**, and the connecting chronology is supplied from the declared material-fact pass and flagged as such. The caption year of the comparative production table is not legible, so **no year is asserted and the figures are used only for relative position**. The civil-war map page is heavily degraded, so the commanders, the Legion's approximate strength, the intervening states and the stated objective are used while **no arrival date, troop strength or operation is asserted**. The opening of the deportation passage in the final chapter is truncated, so **no deported group is named, no total given and no region specified**. The close of the Pavlova passage breaks off, so **no element of her argument beyond its legible opening is asserted**.

6. **Sensitive-event control.** Five episodes - the Red Terror, the civil-war toll, the famine of 1932-33, the Purges, and the wartime and postwar deportations - are governed by explicit rules stated in the "How to read" section, applied at the point of use, and tabulated at Map 5. **No casualty total is stated anywhere in this session that a held source does not state; no held total is presented as settled; estimates are never averaged or reconciled; the four Purge estimates are always accompanied by the object and span each counts; the civil-war figure always retains its "at least" and is never apportioned between the sides; and the export figure of 1932-33 is stated as a fact about priorities and never converted into a counterfactual about lives that would have been saved.** One figure inside a held sentence - the number of kulaks removed or executed - appears in the account without an identified estimator; it is reproduced once, in Lesson 14, solely as the account's own wording with the attribution gap disclosed, and **no argument anywhere in this session rests on it**.

7. **Attribution control.** Responsibility is stated about governments, parties, commissariats, police organisations, congresses, local officials and named individuals throughout. The kulak category is explicitly refused as a description of a real actor, on the strength of the account's own statement that all property-owning peasants were hostile whether kulaks or not. Where a held source uses a collective formulation, it is reported as that source's wording and is not adopted as this session's own.

## The bounded material-fact pass, as declared

⚠️ **Why the pass was necessary and how it was bounded.** The two owners and the core book supply the chronology, the institutions, the statistics, the historiography and the complete answer architecture. Before drafting, one bounded material-fact pass was declared and confined to three categories: essential standard connecting facts that the sources used here require but do not legibly supply; the exact wording and routing status of any verified question relevant to this topic; and one current linkage. **No second pass was opened, no category was extended, and each item was verified once and not reopened.**

| Cluster verified | Result carried |
|---|---|
| **Cluster 1: essential standard chronology** | ✅ Petrograd Soviet Order No. 1, 1 March 1917 Julian / 14 March Gregorian; the Decree on Peace and the Decree on Land adopted by the Second All-Russian Congress of Soviets on 26 October 1917 Julian / 8 November Gregorian; the Cheka established 20 December 1917 by the Gregorian calendar, which is the early-December date the core account gives by the Julian; the Constituent Assembly dispersed 5-6 January 1918 Julian / 18-19 January Gregorian; the Decree on Red Terror of 5 September 1918, following the attempt on Lenin's life on 30 August 1918; the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk signed 3 March 1918; the Tenth Party Congress of 8-16 March 1921 with the resolution on party unity adopted on 16 March; the USSR formally established 30 December 1922; Sergei Kirov assassinated in Leningrad on 1 December 1934; the Moscow show trials of 19-24 August 1936, 23-30 January 1937 and 2-13 March 1938; Marshal Tukhachevsky and seven other senior commanders tried and executed on 11 June 1937; NKVD Order No. 00447 of 30 July 1937; Yezhov replaced by Beria on 25 November 1938; the 1936 Constitution adopted on 5 December 1936; and *Holodomor* as the name used in Ukraine for the famine of 1932-33. ⚠️ Of these, the items actually carried into the teaching are the Order No. 1 instruction that underlies Lesson 6, the two decrees of 26 October, the December 1917 and January 1918 datings, the Brest-Litovsk signature date, the March 1921 congress and unity resolution, the December 1922 constitution of the USSR, the entire 1934-38 purge chronology of Lesson 16, the 5 December 1936 adoption date, and the term used in Lesson 14's current linkage |
| **Cluster 2: exact question wording and routing status** | ✅ The verbatim stem of the 2021 General Studies Paper I, Question 13 - *"There arose a serious challenge to the Democratic State System between the two World Wars." Evaluate the statement. (Answer in 250 words)* - 15 marks, verified against the official paper record and consistent with the repository's own reproduction of it in the rise-of-fascism owner's live edition. ✅ Routing status: **zero** direct topic-only routes in the 2018-2025 Mains ledgers; **five** World-History-tagged items across that span, none routed here; **nil** in all three Prelims ledgers; **nil** in all six 2026 ledgers; and none of the four pre-2018 recurring demands routed here |
| **Cluster 3: one current linkage** | ✅ The Welsh Government written statement "Holodomor Commemoration 2025", published on gov.wales and dated 22 November 2025 - tabulated below and used in Lesson 14 section 6 only |

⚠️ **Control on the pass.** No item from the pass overrides an owner or the core book at any point, and no held statement was found to be wrong on its own terms, so **nothing in this session is a correction of a source**. The pass produced **no historical narrative, no statistical series, no casualty figure and no interpretive claim**; it produced dates, institutional names and one verified question stem. Where the pass and a held source address the same point - the founding of the Cheka, given as early December 1917 in the account and 20 December 1917 in the standard record - both are displayed as the same event under two calendars rather than reconciled by preference.

## Endpoints identified but not retrieved in this pass

Nothing in the teaching depends on text that was not read. The following were identified and **not** fetched, in accordance with the bounded History research rule: any second history textbook, monograph or survey on the Russian revolutions, the civil war, NEP, collectivisation, the Five-Year Plans, the Purges, Soviet society or Stalin; the works of Katkov, Chamberlin, Pipes, Ulam, Berkman, Read, Koenker, Smith, Kennan, Haimson, Fitzpatrick, Service, Stone, Tucker, Nove, McCauley, Freeborn, Conquest, Suny, Getty, Volkogonov, Medvedev, Yakovlev, Kotkin, Lewin, Taylor and Pavlova, all of which are known to this session only through the core account's report of their positions; the texts of the October Manifesto, the Fundamental Laws, the Soviet decrees, the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk, the 1924 and 1936 Constitutions, the Five-Year Plan documents, the NKVD orders or any trial transcript; Lenin's collected writings, including the 1908 essay, the April Theses, the May 1919 pamphlet and the Testament, all of which are used only as the core account reports them; the Soviet statistical handbooks and any archival series for output, camp populations, deportations, executions or famine deaths beyond the figures the sources used here record; the Gareth Jones dispatches of 1933; and any material behind the current linkage beyond its own published page. In every case the canonical owners or the core OCR history source supplied the teaching content.

- Zero objective items appear anywhere in this file, and no answer key is asserted or implied.
- The seven original Mains questions are this session's own and are labelled as such; none is represented as an examination item.
